Thesis
Mathias Sonnleitner

Abstract




The power of random information for numerical approximation

and integration

Dipl.-Ing. Mathias Sonnleitner

A dissertation submitted to the

faculty of computer science and mathematics

in partial fulfillment of the requirements for the degree of

doctor of natural sciences

Advisors:

Prof. Dr. Joscha Prochno

Univ.-Prof. Dr. Aicke Hinrichs

a

Reviewers:

Prof. Dr. Joscha Prochno

Univ.-Prof. Dr. Aicke Hinrichs

Prof. Dr. Alexander Litvak

Date of oral exam:

2022-05-18

Abstract

This thesis investigates the quality of randomly collected data by employing a framework built on information-based complexity, a field related to the numerical analysis of abstract problems. The quality or power of gathered information is measured by its radius which is the uniform error obtainable by the best possible algorithm using it. The main aim is to present progress towards understanding the power of random information for approximation and integration problems.

In the first problem considered, information given by linear functionals is used to recover vectors, in particular from generalized ellipsoids. This is related to the approximation of diagonal operators which are important objects of study in the theory of function spaces. We obtain upper bounds on the radius of random information both in a convex and a quasi-normed setting, which extend and, in some cases, improve existing results. We conjecture and partially establish that the power of random information is subject to a dichotomy determined by the decay of the length of the semiaxes of the generalized ellipsoid.

Second, we study multivariate approximation and integration using information given by function values at sampling point sets. We obtain an asymptotic characterization of the radius of information in terms of a geometric measure of equidistribution, the
distortion, which
is well known in the theory of quantization of measures. This holds for isotropic Sobolev as well as Hölder and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces on bounded convex
domains
. We obtain that for these spaces, depending on the parameters involved, typical point sets are either asymptotically optimal or worse by a logarithmic factor, again extending and improving existing results.

Further, we study isotropic discrepancy which is related to numerical integration using linear algorithms with equal weights. In particular, we analyze the quality of lattice point sets with respect to this criterion and obtain that they are suboptimal compared to uniform random points. This is in contrast to the approximation of Sobolev functions and resolves an open question raised in the context of a possible low discrepancy construction on the two-dimensional sphere.

1
\cdot
Introduction

One could say that mathematics is an endless process in which the following sequence repeats itself indefinitely. Asking a question, searching for an answer which is often (to the possible discomfort of non-mathematicians) understood as a proof of the mere existence of a solution, discovering the structures behind and raising questions about them. It may come as no surprise that this work is no different as it is guided by a question which arose in previous research and in turn triggers new open problems. In order to give more details, let us introduce the setting and give the motivation behind. The subject of study belongs to theoretical numerical analysis, which deals less with the numerics behind implementable algorithms than with the analysis of the given numerical problem itself. As we understand it, this branch of mathematics lies at a crossroads between approximation theory, functional analysis, geometry, information-based complexity (IBC) and probability. In what follows, we draw upon these fields to set the stage, and fill in details in Chapter
2
.

Consider the abstract numerical problem of finding a solution
S(f)
for each object
f
(or problem instance) in a class
F
. For one reason or another, it might be the case that we cannot solve the problem exactly for each problem instance. For one, the quantity
S(f)
might be difficult to compute, say it is the integral of a complicated function
f
belonging to some class of functions
F
. Second, we may only have incomplete knowledge about
f
, meaning
it cannot be described through the finite amount of available data.

In either case, we might try to use finitely many observations of any particular
f\in F
to approximate the solution
S(f)
. Let
L_{1}(f),\dots,L_{n}(f)
be real numbers denoting the
n\in\mathbb{N}
observations we collect about
f
, where the measurements
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}\colon F\to\mathbb{R}
are known and may be applied to any
f\in F
to produce observations. To avoid writing them in full, we use the notation
N_{n}(f)=\big(L_{1}(f),\ldots,L_{n}(f)\big)
for
information
about
f
, which we shall use to construct an approximation
A(f)
to the true solution
S(f)
. Necessarily, if we are only to use this information, the approximation has to be of the form
A(f)=\varphi\big(N_{n}(f)\big)
, where
\varphi
takes
n
real numbers as input.

To illustrate, let us give two examples which will accompany us throughout this work. In the integration problem, the object
f
is a function belonging to a class
F
of functions and has integral
S(f)
(with respect to some fixed measure). In this case, information may be given for example by function evaluations
N_{n}(f)=\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)
and
\varphi\big(N_{n}(f)\big)
should approximate the integral of
f
. In the approximation problem, the object
S(f)=f
is to be approximated itself from information
N_{n}(f)
given by function evaluations or from linear measurements such as Fourier coefficients.

We view
N_{n}
as a map from
F
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, returning for each
f
information
N_{n}(f)
about
f
. We then speak of the
information map

N_{n}
. Similarly, we can interpret
\varphi
as a map from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the set of possible approximants and call it the
reconstruction map
. The composition
A=\varphi\circ N_{n}
is then an algorithm using the information map
N_{n}
. In practice, such an algorithm may represent an actual (future) implementation which runs on a computer (or any other Turing machine) and handles finitely many inputs to produce an output in finite time.

In general, finite information
N_{n}(f)
is insufficient to determine
f\in F
and, even though we may know
f
itself, we assume it is an unknown element of
F
except for information
N_{n}(f)
. Therefore, any, even the best, algorithm using the information map
N_{n}
is bound to incur a non-zero error on some
f\in F
.
The minimal worst-case
error over all possible algorithms using the information map
N_{n}
is called the
radius of information

N_{n}
and measures its quality or power. The smaller its radius, the better the information map.

Naturally, one is interested in solving the given numerical problem of approximating
S(f)
with an error as small as possible by using
n
measurements. A lower bound on this error is given by the smallest possible radius over all information maps using at most
n
measurements. This quantity is called the
n
-th minimal radius of information. Then our main aim is to understand whether (near-)optimal information, whose radius is close to this lower bound (in an asymptotic sense), has to be something singular, arcanely constructed, or rather is typical in the sense that most information maps using
n
measurements have a radius comparable to the
n
-th minimal radius of information. We phrase this as two related questions.

How typical is optimal information? How optimal is typical information?

As a shortcoming of our rather abstract approach this pair of questions is not yet well-posed. Although we shall make it more precise in Chapter
2
, we understand it rather as a theme with many facets and do not expect a definite answer but a whole spectrum of them. In the following, we wish to give some idea
how
possible solutions may look like.

First, we need to clarify what “typical” and “most” mean in this context. This can be resolved by putting a measure on the set of allowed measurements and thus considering random measurements. In many situations, a canonical choice of measure suggests itself but it may be specific to the problem. The random measurements can be collected to form random information. Then, determining the optimality of typical information amounts to studying the radius of random information and
therefore
we will use the terms “typical” and “random” interchangeably.

From a practical point of view, there are several issues we will ignore in this work. We are interested in the radius of information as a theoretical quantity and are not so much concerned with explicit algorithms. Also, we do not consider the often non-trivial process of generating the required random objects. Further, in most cases, we will not be interested in constants but only asymptotic behaviour. Instead, we wish to gain insight into the qualititative behaviour of the typical radius compared to the minimal radius. In this way, we hope to increase our understanding of the problems themselves. For further motivation we draw in the following from previous work in this direction.

To the best of our knowledge, the pair of questions above has not been studied in the literature in such a general context except by Hinrichs, Krieg,
Novak, Prochno and
M. Ullrich in the articles
[
HKN+20
]
and
[
HKN+21
]
which initiated part of the research presented in this thesis. The work
[
HKN+20
]
asks about the
power of random information
, that is, the typical quality of random information coming in by independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations for various numerical problems. The mentioned works contain results on integration and approximation in Sobolev spaces as well as recovery in ellipsoids and associated Hilbert spaces. This thesis extends these insights about the power of random information and gives possible answers to the two questions above.

In
[
HKN+20
]
at least two arguments were put forward to justify the use, and consequently the study, of random instead of optimal information. First, one can easily increase the number of measurements and thus decrease the error without problem if there is a method of sampling random measurements. This allows for some flexibility compared to specifically chosen information which may need to be re-computed if one adds an additional measurement. Second, optimal information is often tailored to the specific problem,
whereas
typical information can be used for many different problems. This behaviour reflects a kind of
universitality
.

To mention potential applications, the assumption of i.i.d. observations is common in (statistical) learning theory, which infers functional dependencies from empirical data from real-world applications, see, e.g., Vapnik
[
Vap98
]
. This field is behind many developments associated with the nowadays popular concept of a neural network which should replace the object it is modeled on, the (human) brain, in modern applications such as pattern recognition or autonomous driving. Therefore, the study of the power of random information may contribute to the understanding of the effectiveness of algorithms used in these fields.

As mentioned, we expect a spectrum of possible ways how random information might compare to optimal information. Essentially, we distinguish the following ends of the spectrum. Random information may either be close to optimal or completely useless. In fact, these are the only two possibilities appearing in a problem treated in Chapter
3
. Let us describe what we can conclude from either one.

If typical information is close to optimal or, equivalently, the power of random information is best possible with high probability, then one might use random information if optimal information is not available or costly to obtain. Sometimes it is even the case that the best known information is a realization of random information. This is related to the
Probabilistic method
using which one proves the existence of an object with a desired property by establishing it with positive probability for a randomly chosen object from a suitably constructed probability space. For an illustration and examples ranging from random graphs to combinatorial discrepancy we refer to the book of the same name
[
AS16
]
by Alon and Spencer. Let us add here that this approach is behind the
construction of suitable
point sets for numerical integration or the use of random matrices in the field of compressed sensing. The latter is a toolbox to efficiently process signals from applications such as facial recognition or magnetic resonance imaging, where only a few large wavelet coefficients are necessary to obtain a good approximation.

On the other hand, a large gap between the power of random information and the optimal behaviour may suggest that measurements have to be carefully selected in order to obtain good algorithms. On a practical note, we may deduce from this how
not
to gather information. It appears that the required lower bounds on the power of random or typical information, which also exhibit the limitations of the Probabilistic method, are far and few in between. Although it is sensible to focus on upper bounds as guarantees for random algorithms, we believe that the other side of the coin deserves attention too.

Apart from its contribution to solving numerical problems, the study of random information combines different fields of mathematics and may increase our knowledge about random structures. For example, if information is collected via function samples, then the radius of information is related to geometric features of the point set such as the distortion or the discrepancy, see Chapters
4
and
6
, respectively. If the point set is drawn randomly, this is connected to the study of Voronoi tessellations induced by a Poisson point process,
see, e.g.,
Yukich
[
Yuk08
]
. Further, if the underlying set is a sphere, the radius of the largest hole in a random point set determines the Hausdorff distance between the ball and its approximation by the convex hull of the points. These topics belong to stochastic geometry as described for example in the book by Schneider and Weil
[
SW08
]
.

As an example of the power of random information, we would like to mention the famous result on the polynomial tractability of star-discrepancy by Heinrich, Novak, Wasilkowski and Woźniakowski
[
HNWW01
]
drawing from progress in empirical process theory due to Talagrand
[
Tal94
]
, see also Novak and Woźniakowski
[
NW08
, Sec. 3.1.5]
. The concept of tractability quantifies high-dimensional behaviour and this example shows that random information can be an antidote to the so-called
curse of dimensionality
, which occurs if the problem complexity depends exponentially on the dimension.

In fact, random information is part of the
blessing of dimensionality
as observed for example by Kainen
[
Kai97
]
. Put briefly, this term summarizes regularizing effects as the dimension increases such as the
concentration of measure phenomenon
for which we refer to the book by Ledoux
[
Led01
]
of the same name.

Concentration of measure has been extensively used in the local theory of Banach spaces which evolved the field of asymptotic geometric analysis at the midpoint between geometry and functional analysis. There, properties of normed spaces as the dimension tends to infinity are studied. Famous questions like the thin-shell or the hyperplane conjecture are still open and the progress towards their solution shows how unintuitive high-dimensional objects may be to the imagination of us three-dimensional beings, see the books by Artstein-Avidan, Giannopoulos and V. D. Milman
[
AGM15
]
as well as V. D. Milman and Schechtman
[
MS86
]
for an extensive overview of the subject.

A related phenomenon specific to high dimensions has been called
existence versus prevalence
by Vershynin
[
Ver06
]
, see also Giannopoulos, V. D. Milman and Tsolomitis
[
GMT05
]
, Litvak, Pajor and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
LPT06
]
as well as the references given there. Roughly speaking, it claims that the existence of one structure with a certain desired property implies that actually most structures satisfy this property. In the works mentioned, the diameter of a random sections of convex bodies has been considered but this also occurs also in related situations. This is a further example of the power of random information and will be discussed in Chapter
3
.

Outline.

We give an overview of the structure of the remainder of this thesis. First, Chapter
2
gives definitions of the concepts introduced above and lays the foundation for the remaining chapters. In particular, the notion of random information is discussed for linear information given by functionals (Section
2.2
) and standard information given by function evaluations (Section
2.3
).

The main body is composed of three Chapters
3
,
4
and
6
which are modeled onto published research during work on this thesis, plus an interlude Chapter
5
connecting Chapters
4
and
6
. Let us first describe the common structure of the triple (
3
,
4
,
6
). Each opens with a detailed introduction which embeds the related publication(s) into a wider context and serves as a motivation. This is followed by a discussion of the results together with existing work. Afterwards, concepts of the proofs behind the results are highlighted in order to make the proofs more accessible and to prepare for follow-up work. Remaining open questions will be posed and discussed at the end in order to provide points of departure for subsequent research and to continue the mathematical process. Throughout these chapters, we will use the optional notation “
Theorem X.x
([XYZ00, Thm. X])” to clarify where the corresponding result is derived from.

Chapter
3
treats linear information for the recovery of vectors belonging to general convex bodies and also to generalized ellipsoids. The results obtained there extend the work from the Hilbert space case in
[
HKN+21
]
and are related to Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators. We present two upper bounds on the radius of random information, one relying on asymptotic geometric analysis, and another relying on compressed sensing. Then we discuss implications for the important special case of polynomial semiaxes, where we conjecture a dichotomy for the power of random information. This is based on the joint work
[
HPS21
]
with Hinrichs and Prochno.

In Chapter
4
we study standard information for the approximation and integration of functions, mainly from Sobolev but also from Hölder and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. Motivated by the study of the power of random information, we derive a characterization of the radius of information given by a point set in terms of the distortion, which measures the size of an average or the largest hole. As a consequence, this yields the optimality of random information for a certain range of parameters including integration. We discuss connections to quantization theory which we use to deduce our result. This chapter is derived from the joint work
[
KS20
]
with Krieg.

The subsequent Chapter
5
does not contain any new results but instead collects material on optimal transport and quantization theory with the aim of discussing the weights used by (near-)optimal algorithms for integration problems. We find that these weights can be given explicitly in terms of the Voronoi cells of the underlying point set. Further, we argue why in some cases weights can be assumed to be normalized and sometimes even of equal size, which yields a (somewhat improvised) connection to the geometrical concept of discrepancy.

Finally, in Chapter
6
standard information is discussed in the context of equal weight cubature rules for numerical integration and discrepancy, which is introduced in a general fashion. The main result is a lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy of lattice point sets which answers a question raised by Aistleitner, Brauchart and Dick
[
ABD12
]
and shows that structured point sets may be at a disadvantage compared to random ones. We derive this from a characterization of the isotropic discrepancy of a lattice point set in terms of the so-called spectral test and derive an asymptotic equivalence for the radius of information given by a lattice point set. The results are taken from joint work with Pillichshammer, namely,
[
PS20
]
and the follow-up work
[
SP21
]
.

Almost all of the proofs are outsourced to the Appendices
A
-
D
at the end of this thesis, which mirror Chapters
3
-
6
.

Notation.
At this point we wish to clarify some notation. The positive integers are denoted by
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
and we write
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Given a sequence
x=(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
0<p\leq\infty
, write
\|x\|_{p}:=(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|x_{i}|^{p})^{1/p}
for
p<\infty
and
\|x\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}|x_{i}|
for
p=\infty
. The unit ball of
\ell_{p}
, the space of sequences
x
with
\|x\|_{p}<\infty
, is
\mathbb{B}_{p}:=\{x\colon\|x\|_{p}\leq 1\}
. We write
\ell_{p}^{m}
for the
m
-dimensional subspace spanned by the first
m\in\mathbb{N}
coordinates and
\mathbb{B}_{p}^{m}
for its (closed) unit ball. If
1\leq p\leq\infty
, then
p^{*}
is the Hölder conjugate with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{*}}=1
, where
a/\infty:=0
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
. If
p=2
, the space
\ell_{2}^{m}
is equipped with the standard inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}:=\partial\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}
is the sphere bounding the unit ball.

For any real
a\in\mathbb{R}
we use the notation
(a)_{+}:=\max\{a,0\}
and
a=\lfloor a\rfloor+\{a\}
, where
\lfloor a\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}
is the integral and
0\leq\{a\}<1
the fractional part of
a
. Logarithms are always taken to basis
{\rm e}=2.71828\dots
.

If
A
is any set, then
\mathbf{1}_{A}
denotes the indicator function of
A
with
\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)=1
if
x\in A
and zero else. If
B
is another set, we do not exclude the case of equality in the notation
A\subset B
. If
A
is finite, we use
\#A
to denote its cardinality.

We assume that all random variables are defined on a common probability space and use
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
for probability and expectation, respectively. The asymptotic notations
\asymp,\lesssim,\gtrsim
are explained in Definitions
2.3
and
2.13
. Other notation is explained at its first point of appearance and collected in the
List of symbols
.

2
\cdot
Preliminaries

In this chapter, we introduce the framework supporting the remainder of the thesis. We wish to clarify the notions used in the introduction and for the sake of completeness allow for some redudance. First, in Section
2.1
, we explain fundamental concepts such as the radius or minimal radius of information. Section
2.2
introduces the setting of recovery using linear information on which Chapter
3
is based. In Section
2.3
we discuss standard information which is obtained by function
samples and
a special case of linear information. This is the basis for Chapter
4
, and in parts also Chapters
5
and
6
.

2.1
The radius of information

Recall that for each
f
in a class of objects
F
we want to compute a solution
S(f)
to a numerical problem. Here, the solution operator
S\colon F\to G
maps into a set
G
containing the set of possible outcomes
S(F):=\{S(f)\colon f\in F\}
. We use
n
measurements
L_{1}(f),\ldots,L_{n}(f)\in\mathbb{R}
, where
L_{i}\colon F\to\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, and combine them into information
N_{n}:=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})\colon F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Using a reconstruction map
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
, we construct from the given information
N_{n}(f)
an approximation
\varphi\big(N_{n}(f)\big)\approx S(f)
. Note that we assume the knowledge of the information map
N_{n}
, and the map
\varphi
may (and will) depend on the information map
N_{n}
. The following diagram illustrates the situation.

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{N_{n}}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}

We impose additional structure and assume that
F
and
G
are normed vector spaces equipped with norms
\|\cdot\|_{F}
and
\|\cdot\|_{G}
, respectively. In this way, we arrive at the setting of information-based complexity, as treated for example by Traub, Wasilkowski and Woźniakowski
[
TWW88
]
and in the book series by Novak and Woźniakowski
[
NW08
,
NW10
,
NW12
]
.

The problem formulation suggests measuring the closeness
\approx
between the approximation
A(f)=\varphi\big(N_{n}(f)\big)
and the true value
S(f)
with
\|S(f)-A(f)\|_{G}
, which is called the
error
of the algorithm
A
at
f\in F
. It is useful to have a uniform error guarantee for the algorithm
A
as the instance
f\in F
producing information
N_{n}(f)
may be unknown. Therefore, consider the
worst-case error

e(S\colon F\to G,A):=\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\|S(f)-A(f)\|_{G}

(2.1)

of
A
over the unit ball
F_{0}:=\{f\colon\|f\|_{F}\leq 1\}
of
F
. Abusing notation, we frequently replace the supremum by
\sup_{\|f\|_{F}\leq 1}
.

One reason to consider the worst-case error over the unit ball is that the norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
measures the size or complexity of the input and elements with larger norm may be more difficult to approximate. More precisely, if both
A
and
S
are linear maps, we can use the worst-case error to obtain a bound on the error of
A
at an arbitrary
f\in F
by normalizing it. This provides some motivation to consider this error criterion and results from

\displaystyle\|S(f)-A(f)\|_{G}=\|f\|_{F}\,\big\|S(f/\|f\|_{F})-A(f/\|f\|_{F})\big\|_{G}\leq\|f\|_{F}\,e(S\colon F\to G,A).

To ensure a finite worst-case error, it is convenient to suppose that
S
is bounded, that is,

\sup_{\|f\|_{F}\leq 1}\|S(f)\|_{G}=e(S\colon F\to G,0)<\infty.

In other words, when we do not have any information, we take the algorithm equal to zero and the resulting initial error is supposed to be finite.

Formally, the radius of information is defined as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

The radius of an information map
N_{n}\colon F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is

r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n}):=\inf_{\varphi}e({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S\colon F\to G},\varphi\circ N_{n}),

(2.2)

where the infimum is taken over all reconstruction maps
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
.

Equivalently, the radius
r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})
is the minimal worst-case error over all algorithms
A=\varphi\circ N_{n}
using the information map
N_{n}
. The term “radius” is of geometric origin and is related to the fact that the algorithm which chooses a center of the set of elements of equal information has minimal worst-case error. The concept of a Chebyshev radius from numerical analysis is similar, see, e.g., Novak
[
Nov88
, Sec. A.3]
.

In the following, we shall consider only continuous and linear measurements belonging to a class
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\rm all}:=F^{\prime}
, where
F^{\prime}
is the dual space of
F
and contains all continuous and linear functionals from
F
to
\mathbb{R}
. Sections
2.2
and
2.3
will discuss the two choices of
\Lambda
considered in this thesis, namely the case of linear information
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\rm all}
and the case of standard information
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\rm std}
consisting of function evaluations.

In general, the class
\Lambda
gives rise to information maps
N_{n}
of the form

N_{n}=(L_{1},\dots,L_{n}),\quad\text{where }L_{i}\in\Lambda\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,n,

(2.3)

where
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the cardinality of
N_{n}
. The minimal radius is a benchmark for the quality of any information
N_{n}
of the form (
2.3
) and is given as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

The
n
-th minimal radius of information with respect to
\Lambda
is

r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda,n):=\inf_{N_{n}}r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(2.4)

where the infimum is over all information maps
N_{n}\colon F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the form (
2.3
).

This is the best we can do using
n
optimal measurements from the class
\Lambda
. By definition, the
n
-th minimal radius will be a non-increasing function of
n
and, under suitable conditions, will tend to zero as the amount of information increases.
This means
that we can obtain arbitrarily good approximations using finite information, see also Remark
2.7
below. Further, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
N_{n}
as in (
2.3
), we have

r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})\geq r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda,n).

(2.5)

We shall be interested in the question whether (
2.5
) can be reversed up to a constant for most information maps.

Question 2.1
.

Does it hold that for most information maps
N_{n}
with measurements from
\Lambda
that

r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})\lesssim r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda,n)?

This is a very general formulation of the problem studied in this thesis. In order to make sense of it, it is necessary to clarify what “most” shall mean. This shall be done in the remaining sections of this chapter. For now, we shall be concerned with explaining the notation
\lesssim
.

Definition 2.3
.

Given two sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of non-negative real numbers, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq C\,b_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In this case, we may also write
b_{n}\gtrsim a_{n}
. If there exists another constant
c>0
such that also
a_{n}\geq c\,b_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
(
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
) holds, then we write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
and speak of asymptotic equivalence. We shall use this notation also for non-negative real numbers indexed for example by
(n,m)
instead of
n
and then demand that the implicit constants are independent of
n
and
m
. Further we may use subindices as in
\lesssim_{p}
to indicate that the implicit constant may depend on an additional parameter
p
.

The notation makes clear that we are mainly interested in the asymptotic behaviour, that is, in
n
tending to
\infty
. If Question
2.1
were true, then the asymptotic equivalence

r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})\asymp r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda,n)

would hold for “most” information maps
N_{n}
with measurements belonging to
\Lambda
. In this case we say that the radius of typical information is asymptotically equivalent to the minimal radius. As we are mostly concerned only with this asymptotic formulation, we will often be careless about constants, which may be deduced from our proofs.

At the end of this section, we define the numerical problems central to this thesis.

Approximation.

In the approximation problem, the map
S
is equal to the embedding
\mathrm{id}\colon F\to G
which takes
f\in F
to
\mathrm{id}(f)=f
itself. We assume that
F\subset G
and further that the embedding
\mathrm{id}\colon F\to G
is bounded, i.e., there exists a constant
C
such that
\|f\|_{G}\leq C\|f\|_{F}
for all
f\in F
. In this case we speak of a continuous embedding of
F
into
G
and write
F\hookrightarrow G
. We then use the notation

r(F\hookrightarrow G,N_{n}):=r(\mathrm{id}\colon F\to G,N_{n})

and analogously for the minimal radius. The upcoming section will introduce random linear information for approximation as a preparation for Chapter
3
and Section
2.3
will introduce standard information used in Chapter
4
for the approximation problem.

Integration.

In the integration problem, the space
F
is a space of real-valued functions on some measure space and
S
takes a function
f\in F
to its integral, so that
G=\mathbb{R}
. Here, we assume that that
F
is continuously embedded into the space of integrable functions. Then
S
is a continuous linear functional itself and therefore it does not make sense to consider linear information but only standard information for this problem which will be treated in Chapters
4
,
5
and
6
in various guises.

Remark 2.4
.

In this thesis, we consider only non-adaptive information maps of the form (
2.3
) and use the same measurements for every
f\in F
. This is in
contrast
to adaptive information, where one may use the measurement
L_{n}(f)
to select a suitable
L_{n+1}\in\Lambda
. Note that if
S
is linear between normed spaces
F
and
G
, as in the majority of the problems we will consider, then adaption can only yield an improvement of at most a constant factor, see, e.g.,
[
NW08
, Thm. 4.4]
. This justifies our assumption.

2.2
Linear information and Gelfand widths

In this section, we consider linear information
N_{n}
, which is of the form

N_{n}=(L_{1},\dots,L_{n}),\quad\text{where }L_{i}\in\Lambda^{\rm all}\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,n,

(2.6)

with
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that
\Lambda^{\rm all}
is the set of all continuous linear functionals on
F
. In the following, we shall assume that
S
is linear and bounded as this is the case for the approximation and the integration problems we consider. Then the radius of information (Definition
2.1
) satisfies the following well-known relation.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
S\colon F\to G
be a bounded linear map between normed spaces. Then we have, for all linear information maps
N_{n}
as in (
2.6
),

\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G}\leq r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})\leq 2\,\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G},

where
\ker N_{n}:=\{f\in F\colon N_{n}(f)=0\}
is the kernel of
N_{n}
.

This states that the radius of any linear information
N_{n}
is equivalent to the radius of the smallest ball in
G
containing the symmetric set
\{S(f)\colon f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}\}
of possible solutions having zero information. For convenience a proof of Proposition
2.5
will be provided at the end of this section. Before
we apply
it to random information, we discuss its implications for the minimal radius.

Derived from Definition
2.2
, the
n
-th minimal radius of linear information is given by

r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm all},n):=\inf_{N_{n}}r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(2.7)

where the infimum is over all linear information maps
N_{n}
of the form (
2.6
). This is closely related to the following concept.

Definition 2.6
.

The
n
-th Gelfand number of
S\colon F\to G
is given by

c_{n}(S\colon F\to G):=\inf_{E_{n-1}}\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap E_{n-1}}\|S(f)\|_{G},

where the infimum is over all linear subspaces
E_{n-1}\subset F
of codimension
n-1
and for convenience we set
c_{n}(S\colon F\to G)=0
if
n\geq\dim(F)
.

It is well known that the Gelfand numbers are comparable to the minimal radius in the sense of

c_{n+1}(S\colon F\to G)\leq r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm all},n)\leq 2\,c_{n+1}(S\colon F\to G),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.8)

A proof can be found for example in
[
Hei94
, Prop. 3.1]
by Heinrich but may deduced also from Proposition
2.5
by taking the infimum over all linear information maps
N_{n}
of cardinality
n
, since continuity implies that a subspace
E_{n}
of
F
is of codimension
n
precisely when it is the kernel of such an
N_{n}
.

In the following, let us consider the approximation problem introduced at the end of the previous section. Our assumptions imply that Proposition
2.5
applies to the embedding
F\hookrightarrow G
which we deal with for the approximation problem. In view of this, we may also define, for every linear subspace
E\subset F
,

\mathrm{rad}_{G}(F_{0},E):=\sup_{f\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}F_{0}\cap E}}\|f\|_{G},

(2.9)

which is the radius of the section of the unit ball
F_{0}
with the subspace
E
measured in the norm of
G
. Then, for all linear information maps
N_{n}
we have

\mathrm{rad}_{G}(F_{0},\ker N_{n})\leq r(F\hookrightarrow G,N_{n})\leq 2\,\mathrm{rad}_{G}(F_{0},\ker N_{n}).

(2.10)

To lay the foundation for Chapter
3
, let us consider now the finite-dimensional case with
F=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{K})
and
G=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{G})
of dimension
m\in\mathbb{N}
and equipped with norms
\|\cdot\|_{K}
and
\|\cdot\|_{G}
, respectively. The unit ball
K:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\colon\|x\|_{K}\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a convex body, that is a compact convex set with non-empty interior. We will focus mainly on the special case of
\|\cdot\|_{G}=\|\cdot\|_{2}
such that
G=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{2})=\ell_{2}^{m}
becomes the Euclidean space equipped with its usual distance. In this case, we drop
G
from the notation and replace (
2.10
) by

\mathrm{rad}(K,\ker N_{n})\leq r(K,N_{n})\leq 2\,\mathrm{rad}(K,\ker N_{n}),

(2.11)

where
r(K,N_{n}):=r\big((\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{K})\hookrightarrow\ell_{2}^{m},N_{n}\big)
. This yields the equivalence between the radius of any linear information to the radius of the section of
K
with the subspace
\ker N_{n}
. Most of the time, we shall assume that
n<m
as otherwise vectors may be reconstructed exactly by simply measuring their coordinates.

By means of (
2.8
), the minimal radius of information is then up to a constant of two equivalent to the Gelfand numbers of id
\colon(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{K})\to\ell_{2}^{m}
, which we denote by

c_{n}(K):=\inf_{E_{n-1}}\sup_{f\in K\cap E_{n-1}}\|x\|_{2},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(2.12)

where the infimum is again over all linear subspaces
E_{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
of codimension
n-1
. The number
c_{n}(K)
is also called the
n
-th Gelfand width of
K
in
\ell_{2}
.

Remark 2.7
.

Generally speaking, the Gelfand numbers/widths quantify the degree of compactness and are studied as part of the theory of
s
-numbers and
n
-widths, see Pietsch
[
Pie87
]
and Pinkus
[
Pin85
]
for further information. It is known (see
[
Pie87
, Prop. 2.4.10]
) that a bounded linear map
S
is compact if and only if the sequence of Gelfand numbers
(c_{n}(S\colon F\to G)\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
decays to zero. By Proposition
2.5
this transfers to the sequence of minimal radii and justifies assuming compactness of embeddings.

Now that we have elaborated sufficiently on the minimal radius and thus optimal linear information we would like to consider random linear information in this finite-dimensional setting. To this end, let us note that any linear functional
L\colon F\to\mathbb{R}
is automatically continuous and of the form
L(x)=\langle y,x\rangle,x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for some
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, where
\langle y,x\rangle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}x_{i}}
is the standard inner product. In this case, we say that
L
is represented by the vector
y
. In this way, a linear information map
N_{n}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be identified with a
n\times m
-matrix with the representer of
L_{i}
forming
the

i
-th row.

Via the correspondence between linear functionals and their representers, a random measurement amounts to a random vector
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Arguably, random information should not give priority to some particular direction. This amounts to supposing that the direction
X/\|X\|_{2}
is uniformly distributed on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\colon\|x\|_{2}=1\}
. To explain, the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
can be equipped with a unique normalized rotation invariant measure
\sigma^{(m-1)}
and we call a random vector
X^{\prime}
uniformly distributed on it if
\mathbb{P}(X^{\prime}\in A)=\sigma^{(m-1)}(A)
for all Borel sets
A\subset\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
. There is a simple and elegant way to construct such an object.

Take
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
independent standard Gaussians, that is random variables with density
(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{2}/2)
, and form the random vector
G_{1}:=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
. Then the rotation invariance of
G_{1}
implies that its direction
G_{1}/\|G_{1}\|_{2}
is uniformly distributed on the unit sphere. This motivates the following definition of random linear information.

Definition 2.8
.

A random linear measurement is given by
x\mapsto\langle G_{1},x\rangle
and random linear information of cardinality
n
consists of the measurements represented by
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}
, where
G_{2},\dots,G_{n}
are i.i.d. copies of
G_{1}
. It can be identified with the
n\times m
-matrix
G_{n,m}
filled with independent standard Gaussian entries and acts by mapping
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
(\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{ij}x_{j})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Here and in the following, we simplify notation by dropping the arguments of random variables.

The radius of such random (Gaussian) information is the random variable
r(K,G_{n,m})
. By relation (
2.11
), we have, for all realizations,

\mathrm{rad}\big(K,\ker(G_{n,m})\big)\leq r(K,G_{n,m})\leq 2\,\mathrm{rad}\big(K,\ker(G_{n,m})\big).

Here, the kernel
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}:=\ker(G_{n,m})
is a random subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
of codimension
n
in the following sense, see for example Mattila
[
Mat95
, Ch. 3]
and Meckes
[
Mec19
]
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{m,m-n}
be the Grassmannian parametrizing all subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
which are of dimension
m-n
and thus of codimension
n
. There is a unique probability measure on
\mathcal{G}_{m,m-n}
which is invariant under rotation and
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
is distributed according to this measure. Note that the probability that
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
has codimension smaller than
n
is zero.

In analogy to the radius of information, the radius of any section is at least the radius of a minimal section and thus, for every realization,

\mathrm{rad}(K,n):=c_{n+1}(K)\leq\mathrm{rad}(K,E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}),\quad 1\leq n<m.

The equivalence (
2.11
) between the power of random (Gaussian) information for the recovery of vectors from an
m
-dimensional normed space with unit ball
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and the radius of its intersection with a random subspace allows to pose Question
2.1
as follows.

Question 2.2
.

What is the power of random standard information?

Do we have for random sections that

\mathrm{rad}(K,E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\lesssim\mathrm{rad}(K,n)
,
or equivalently

for Gaussian information that

r(K,G_{n,m})\lesssim r(K,n)

with high probability?

This question will be taken up again in Chapter
3
where we present results for a special class of sets, the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids and study the asymptotics as
n
becomes large but remains much smaller than
m
. As we shall also consider unit balls of quasi-normed spaces, we conclude this section by commenting on them.

A quasi-normed space
F
is a linear space equipped with a quasi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
instead of a norm. In replacement of the triangle inequality it satisfies

\|f_{1}+f_{2}\|_{F}\leq C_{F}\big(\|f_{1}\|_{F}+\|f_{2}\|_{F}\big)\quad\text{for some constant }C_{F}\geq 1\text{ and all }f_{1},f_{2}\in F.

If norms are replaced by quasi-norms, Proposition
2.5
and relation (
2.8
) remain valid with changed constants. For the convenience of the reader we state this in the following proposition and provide a proof.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
S\colon F\to G
be a bounded linear operator between quasi-normed spaces, that is,
F
and
G
are equipped with quasi-norms with constant
C_{F},C_{G}\geq 1
, respectively. Then we have, for all linear information maps
N_{n}
as in (
2.6
),

C_{G}^{-1}\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G}\leq r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})\leq 2\,C_{F}\,\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ker N_{n}=}\{f\in F\colon N_{n}(f)=0\}
.

Proof.

For the lower bound on the radius of information we have to prove that

\inf_{\varphi}\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\big\|S(f)-\varphi\big(N_{n}(f)\big)\big\|_{G}\geq C_{G}^{-1}\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G}

where
F_{0}=\{f\in F\colon\|f\|_{F}\leq 1\}
and the infimum is over all
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
. Thus fix an arbitrary
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
. For every
f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}
also
-f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}
and, since
S
is linear,

\|S(f)-\varphi(0)\|_{G}\geq C_{G}^{-1}\|S(f)\|_{G}\quad\text{ or }\quad\|S(-f)-\varphi(0)\|_{G}\geq C_{G}^{-1}\|S(f)\|_{G}

(2.13)

holds. Moreover, the symmetry of
F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}
implies

\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\|S(f)-\varphi(N_{n}f)\|_{G}\geq\sup_{f\in F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\max\big\{\|S(f)-\varphi(0)\|_{G},\|S(-f)-\varphi(0)\|_{G}\big\}.

Together with (
2.13
) this proves the lower bound.

For the upper bound we specify a map
\varphi
by
\varphi(y)=S(g)
for any
y\in N_{n}(F_{0})
, where
g\in F_{0}
with
N_{n}g=y
is arbitrary. Then

\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\|S(f)-\varphi(N_{n}f)\|_{G}\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},f_{2}\in F_{0}\\
N_{n}f_{1}=N_{n}f_{2}\end{subarray}}\|S(f_{1})-S(f_{2})\|_{G}\leq\sup_{f\in 2C_{F}F_{0}\cap\ker N_{n}}\|S(f)\|_{G}

since
f_{1}-f_{2}\in 2C_{F}F_{0}
if
f_{1},f_{2}\in F_{0}
and
N_{n}(f_{1}-f_{2})=0
if
N_{n}f_{1}=N_{n}f_{2}
. Since
S
is linear, a rescaling concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Note that Proposition
2.9
may be seen as a replacement for
[
HPS21
, Lem. 3]
which applies even to sets not necessarily being unit balls of quasi-normed spaces
and
is more general than what we shall need. The proof of
[
HPS21
, Lem. 3]
is very similar to the one of Proposition
2.9
.

Remark 2.11
.

One might also consider other distributions on the space of linear functionals giving rise to other notions of typical information. For example, Mendelson, Pajor and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
MPT07
]
studied recovery of vectors using information given by i.i.d.
copies of a linear measurement

x\mapsto\langle X,x\rangle
, where
X
is an isotropic and subgaussian random vector, that is, for some
\alpha\geq 1
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}X=0,\quad}\mathbb{E}|\langle X,x\rangle|^{2}=\|x\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\langle X,x\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\alpha\|x\|_{2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
is an Orlicz norm with function
\psi_{2}(t)=e^{t^{2}}-1
, see, e.g.,
[
AGM15
, Ch. 3.5.2]
. This includes, aside from the Gaussian measure, the uniform measure on
\{-1,1\}^{m}
and the normalized volume on various symmetric convex bodies such as
\ell_{p}
-balls,
2\leq p\leq\infty
.

2.3
Standard information and sampling algorithms

In the following, we will discuss standard information where the measurements correspond to function evaluations at a point set. In particular, the objects of study will be functions and
F
will be a normed space of functions
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
on some (infinite) set
D
, which may be a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a manifold or an even more general space. As in Section
2.1
, let
S\colon F\to G
, where
G
is a normed space.

The class of standard information is
\Lambda^{\rm std}:=\{\delta_{x}\colon x\in D\}
, where for all
x\in D
the delta functional
\delta_{x}
is the point evaluation
f\mapsto\delta_{x}(f):=f(x)
for all
f\in F
. Then a standard information map is given by

N_{n}=(\delta_{x_{1}},\ldots,\delta_{x_{n}}),\quad\text{where }x_{i}\in D\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,n.

(2.14)

We can identify such an information map
N_{n}
with the point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
it relies on and write
N_{n}(f)=\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)
. Here and in the following, we always assume that such point sets are non-empty and finite, and that their points are pairwise distinct. For every
x\in D
the evaluation functional
\delta_{x}
is linear and we shall also assume that it is continuous or bounded, that is, there is a constant
C>0
such that, for every
f\in F
, we have
|f(x)|\leq C\|f\|_{F}
. Then
\Lambda^{\rm std}\subset\Lambda^{\rm all}
holds.

The definition of the radius of the information map
N_{n}=(\delta_{x_{1}},\ldots,\delta_{x_{n}})
can be written in terms of the point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and we use the notation

r(S\colon F\to G,P_{n}):=r(S\colon F\to G,N_{n})=\inf_{\varphi}\big\|S(f)-\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)\big\|_{G},

where the infimum is over all maps
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
. From now on we shall always replace standard information
N_{n}
as in (
2.14
) by its underlying point set and refer to the radius of information given by a point set also as the quality of the point set itself.

A special case of (
2.4
), the
n
-th minimal radius of standard information is

r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm std},n)=\inf_{\#P_{n}=n}r(S\colon F\to G,P_{n}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

where the infimum is over all
n
-point sets
P_{n}\subset D
. Here, the number
\#P_{n}=n
denotes the cardinality of
P_{n}
. The
n
-th minimal radius is sometimes called
n
-th sampling number and may be expressed as

r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm std},n)=\inf_{S_{P_{n}}}e(S\colon F\to G,S_{P_{n}})

where the infimum is over all sampling operators of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{P_{n}}\colon F\to G},\qquad S_{P_{n}}(f)=\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)

(2.15)

where
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset D
consists of
n
points and
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G
is arbitrary. By Proposition
2.5
, for any bounded linear
S
and any point set
P_{n}\subset D
,

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in F_{0}\\
f|_{P_{n}}=0\end{subarray}}\|S(f)\|_{G}\leq r(S\colon F\to G,P_{n})\leq 2\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in F_{0}\\
f|_{P_{n}}=0\end{subarray}}\|S(f)\|_{G},

(2.16)

and by taking the infimum over all
n
-point sets this holds for the sampling numbers. Here,
f|_{P_{n}}
is the restriction of
f
to
P_{n}
. Via Proposition
2.9
this transfers to the quasi-Banach case, see also Novak and Triebel
[
NT06
, Prop. 19]
.

Since the infimum in the definition of the sampling numbers is over the smaller set of function evaluations, we have

r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm std},n)\geq r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm all},n),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

and in general comparing the power of standard and linear information is of much interest in IBC, see for example
[
NW12
]
.

To formalize Question
2.1
on the power of random information for standard information, we need to give meaning what “most” means and for this need a “uniform” measure on the set of evaluation functionals
\Lambda^{\rm std}
. As each functional
\delta_{x}\in\Lambda^{\rm std}
is in one-to-one correspondence with a point of
x\in D
, this amounts to putting a measure on
D
. In general, let
D=(D,\Sigma,\lambda)
be a finite measure space, where the measure
\lambda
will be assumed to be a probability measure. Often, a suitable choice of
\lambda
can be thought of as uniform measure and may be used to define random standard information as follows.

Recall that for linear information on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
the space itself parametrizes the linear functionals and we defined a random linear functional as a Gaussian vector. For standard information we can identify measurements with points and use random points instead.

Definition 2.12
.

A random point evaluation is given by
\delta_{X}
, where
X
is a random point
X
in
D
distributed according to
\lambda
, that is
\mathbb{P}(X\in A)=\lambda(A)
for every
A\in\Sigma
. For random standard information of cardinality
n\in\mathbb{N}
we take the map
N_{n}=(\delta_{X_{1}},\ldots,\delta_{X_{n}})
where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent copies of
X
. It can be identified with the random point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}:=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
.

In many cases there are obvious choices for such a measure
\lambda
. For example, we will deal with
D
being some
d
-dimensional open set equipped with the normalized Lebesgue measure defined by
\mathrm{vol}(A\cap D)/\mathrm{vol}(D)
for all Borel sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\mathrm{vol}=\mathrm{vol}_{d}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Further examples include Riemannian manifolds, on which the metric induces a volume measure, or the Grassmannian with its rotation invariant probability measure, which was discussed in the previous section.

For each realization, the random point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
is a point set and we ignore the possibility that points of it coincide as this event will have probability zero in the cases we consider. The radius of random standard information is the random quantity
r(S\colon F\to G,P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})
and it is at least the
n
-th minimal radius of standard information. In other words, random points can not be better than optimal points. We translate Question
2.1
to standard information as follows.

Question 2.3
.

What is the power of random standard information?

Do we have for the radius of random information that

r(S\colon F\to G,P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\lesssim r(S\colon F\to G,\Lambda^{\rm std},n)

with high probability?

In order to give an answer to this question, we will study the radius of information of arbitrary realizations and derive expressions which can be estimated for the random point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
. The following notation which is a variant of the asymptotic notation in Definition
2.3
will be useful.

Definition 2.13
.

Given two real numbers
a(P_{n})
and
b(P_{n})
which depend on a point set
P_{n}\subset D
, we write
a(P_{n})\asymp b(P_{n})
if there exist
c,C>0
such that

c\,a(P_{n})\leq b(P_{n})\leq C\,a(P_{n})\quad\text{for all }P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset D,n\in\mathbb{N},

and analogously for
\lesssim
and
\gtrsim
.

We will deal with Question
2.3
in Chapters
4
,
5
and
6
. We want to describe the approximation and the integration problems for which we study it in more detail. Let
D=(D,\Sigma,\lambda)
be a finite measure space. If
D
is additionally a topological space, then the continuity of the evaluation functionals is ensured by assuming that
F\hookrightarrow C_{b}(D)
, i.e.,
F
is continuously embedded into
C_{b}(D)
, the space of bounded continuous real-valued functions on
D
equipped with the supremum norm
\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in D}|f(x)|
. In this case, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\sup_{x\in D}|f(x)|\leq C\|f\|_{F}
for all
f\in F
. This assumption, which is slightly stronger than necessary, is satisfied by the function spaces considered in Chapter
4
.

Let further
\mu
be a probability measure on
D
which is absolutely continuous with respect to
\lambda
and has bounded density
\varrho\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
.

L_{q}
-approximation.
This is the approximation problem considered at the end of Section
2.1
with
(G,\|\cdot\|_{G})=(L_{q}(D,\mu),\|\cdot\|_{L_{q}(D,\mu)})
for some
0<q\leq\infty
. Here,
L_{q}(D,\mu)
is the space of (equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
with finite
L_{q}
-norm

\|f\|_{L_{q}(D,\mu)}:=\begin{cases}\Big(\int_{D}|f(x)|^{q}{\rm d}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/q}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0<q<\infty,}\\
\esssup_{x\in D}|f(x)|&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q=\infty}.\end{cases}

(2.17)

The embedding into
C_{b}(D)
ensures, for all
0<q\leq\infty
, the embedding into
L_{q}(D,\varrho)
because of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|f\|_{L_{q}(D,\mu)}^{q}\leq C\|\varrho\|_{\infty}\lambda(D)\|f\|_{F}^{q}}
, for all
f\in F
. If
\mu=\lambda
we will omit it in the notation and simply write
L_{q}(D)
.

Integration.
Here, we choose
S={\rm INT}_{\mu}
and
G=\mathbb{R}
, where

{\rm INT}_{\mu}\colon F\to\mathbb{R},\quad{\rm INT}_{\mu}(f):=\int_{D}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x),\quad f\in F,

is the integration functional. Note that
{\rm INT}_{\mu}(f)\leq\|f\|_{L_{1}(D,\mu)}
and thus
F\hookrightarrow C_{b}(D)
also ensures the continuity of
\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}
. In general, we omit the space
G=\mathbb{R}
in the notation and write
e(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},A)
for the worst-case error of an algorithm
A
and
r(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},P_{n})
for the radius of information given by
P_{n}\subset D
. If
\mu=\lambda
, we shall use
{\rm INT}
instead of
\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}
.

Both problems will be studied in Chapter
4
for Sobolev spaces and also other function spaces defined on subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For simplicity, only the case of
\mu=\lambda=\mathrm{vol}
, the Lebesgue measure, will be considered there. At this point, we remark that on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
measurability and integrability are understood in the sense of Lebesgue, except mentioned otherwise. The integration problem is also central to Chapter
5
, where it is treated with respect to general probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and Chapter
6
, where mainly the cube equipped with
\mathrm{vol}
is treated.

Remark 2.14
.

Since the measure
\mu
influences the contribution of subsets of
D
to the approximation error, one may use
\mu
instead of
\lambda
to define random standard information. However, in this thesis we will not consider sampling from distributions different from the uniform one.

3
\cdot
Gaussian information for generalized ellipsoids

In this chapter, we will present results from
[
HPS21
]
on the power of random linear information for the recovery of vectors from
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids, and thus generalize the work
[
HKN+21
]
for the usual ellipsoids. To give some idea, the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids can be defined as the images of
\ell_{p}
-balls
\mathbb{B}_{p}^{m}
under diagonal operators and their Gelfand widths are
thus
related to the Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators. In the upcoming Section
3.1
we present their connection to certain function spaces after surveying the literature on random sections of convex bodies.

Let us describe the remainder of this chapter. In Section
3.2
we present our main results from
[
HPS21
]
and discuss connections with existing work. Then in Section
3.3
we shall look at the important example of
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with polynomial semiaxes and assess the power of random linear information for those. Afterwards in Sections
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
we explain the methods behind the proofs, essentially covering topics ranging from asymptotic geometric analysis to sparse approximation. Finally, in Section
3.7
we collect points of departure for further research.

3.1
Introduction and motivation

Consider the
m
-dimensional approximation problem from Section
2.2
, where linear information is used to recover vectors in the
\ell_{2}^{m}
-norm from the unit ball of an
m
-dimensional normed space, which is a symmetric convex body
K
. Motivated by Question
2.2
, we study the radius of Gaussian information which is equivalent to the radius of the intersection of
K
with a random subspace of codimension
n
. We will be interested in the case of
m
being much larger than
n
, say at least
m>2n
.

The study of the radius, or equivalently the diameter, of the section of a convex body with a linear subspace has its roots in the local theory of Banach spaces, see V. D. Milman
[
Mil85a
,
Mil85b
]
. Today, it is part of the field of asymptotic geometric analysis, where it is connected with low
M^{*}
-estimates, which will be discussed in Section
3.4
as an important tool behind our bounds. With particular focus on subspace dimensions proportional to the dimension of the convex body, this was investigated, among others, by Giannopoulos and Milman
[
GM97
,
GM98
]
, also together with Tsolomitis
[
GMT05
]
, and quite recently by Litvak, Pajor and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
LPT06
]
.

In
[
LPT06
]
a random matrix perspective was employed to show that, on the scale of proportional subspaces, typical intersections of a symmetric convex body
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
are not much larger than minimal intersections. More precisely, typical intersections of
K
with
N
-codimensional random subspaces are at most a factor
C(\varepsilon)
larger than the
n
-th Gelfand width
c_{n}(K)
provided that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N/m>\varepsilon}
and
N/n>1+\varepsilon
for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
. This is an instance of the “existence versus prevalence” phenomenon mentioned in the introduction, see also
[
GMT05
,
Ver06
]
.

In contrast to
[
LPT06
]
, where the knowledge of one minimal section suffices, Litvak and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
LT00
]
obtained a bound on the radius of random section in terms of a suitable average over the tail of the sequence of minimal radii. To make this precise, define the random Gelfand numbers of the operator
\mathrm{id}\colon(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{K})\to\ell_{2}^{m}
, that is the random Gelfand widths of
K
, for
1\leq n\leq m
, by

\mathrm{cr}_{n}(K):=\inf\{a>0\colon\mathrm{rad}(K,E_{n-1}^{\rm ran})<a\},

(3.1)

where in this relation a property holds for a random subspace
E_{n-1}^{\rm ran}
of codimension
n-1
if it is satisfied with exponentially high probability in
n
, that is, with probability at least
1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exp\big(-\alpha_{0}(n+1)\big)}
for a suitable
\alpha_{0}>0
fixed beforehand. Then, for not necessarily symmetric
K
,
[
LT00
, Thm. 3.2]
yields constants
c,C>0
such that the random Gelfand widths satisfy

\mathrm{cr}_{n}(K)\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=\lfloor c\,n\rfloor}^{m}\frac{c_{j}(K)}{\sqrt{j}},\quad 1\leq n\leq m.

(3.2)

Recall that the Gelfand width
c_{n+1}(K)=\mathrm{rad}(K,n)
is the minimal radius over
n
-codimensional sections of
K
, which is in turn equivalent to
r(K,n)
, the
n
-th minimal radius of linear information. It can be deduced from this bound that if the Gelfand widths decay at a polynomial rate faster than
n^{-1/2}
, then the random Gelfand widths are asymptotically equivalent to the Gelfand widths and Question
2.2
can be answered in the positive. We will say more about this in the next section when we compare this bound to our results.

The bound in (
3.2
) is very general but we do not have a matching lower bound. To increase our understanding, we will study concrete examples such as
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids which generalize the usual ellipsoid

\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m}:=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\colon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{|x_{i}|^{2}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\leq 1\Big\},

(3.3)

with semiaxes
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots)
of positive length. For the sake of
motivation, we
provide some details about the connection between the sections of ellipsoids and linear information for Hilbert space embeddings before introducing
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids.

In the context of function spaces, ellipsoids appear in the study of a compact embedding of a Hilbert space into an
L_{2}
-space. This is well known and for example elaborated in
[
HKN+21
]
, see also Pinkus
[
Pin85
, Ch. IV]
. To give the details, let
S=\mathrm{id}\colon H\to K
be a compact embedding between separable Hilbert spaces
H,K
. Then one can form an orthonormal basis
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
of
K
using the normalized eigenfunctions of the compact selfadjoint operator
S^{*}S
defined on
K
, where
S^{*}
is the adjoint. The basis is assumed to be ordered such that the singular numbers
\sigma_{j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}
with
S^{*}Se_{j}=\lambda_{j}e_{j}
, where
j\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfy
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\cdots
. The unit ball in
H
is then given by all functions
f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}e_{j}
with

\|f\|_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{j}^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\leq 1,

that is an infinite dimensional ellipsoid.

Linear functionals on
H
can then be identified with sequences and random information becomes a sequence of independent standard Gaussian random variables. It is known that optimal information using
n
linear functionals is given by evaluating the first coefficients
a_{j}=\langle f,e_{j}\rangle_{H}
and that the optimal algorithm is
A(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle f,e_{j}\rangle_{H}e_{j}
, which is the projection
onto
the coordinates in which the ellipsoid is largest. Then the
n
-th minimal radius of linear information is given by the singular value
\sigma_{n+1}
, which is also the radius of the minimal section of
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m}
, i.e.,

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},n)=r(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},n)=\sigma_{n+1},\quad 0\leq n<m.

This is because
sections of an ellipsoid are again ellipsoids and
the diameter of an ellipsoid is twice its radius. Thus, we have equality in Proposition
2.5
and consequently relation (
2.8
) instead of the constant two, see, e.g.,
[
TWW88
]
or
[
NW08
, Ch. 4]
.

In
[
HKN+21
]
the relation between random and minimal sections of an ellipsoid has been investigated and by
[
HKN+21
, Thm. 3]
there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\sum_{j\geq\lfloor n/4\rfloor}\sigma_{j}^{2}\Big)^{1/2},\quad\text{for }1\leq n<m,

(3.4)

with exponentially high probability (in
n
). Similar to the bound (
3.2
) it yields that the typical radius is asymptotically equivalent to the minimal radius provided the latter decays fast enough for increasing
n
.

However, if the semiaxes
\sigma=(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
decay too slowly and are not square-summable, that is
\|\sigma\|_{2}=\infty
, then the behaviour of the radii of typical sections changes completely. In this case,
[
HKN+21
, Thm. 5]
yields that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\sigma_{1}

(3.5)

with exponentially high probability, provided that
m
is large enough compared to
n
.

Taken together, these bounds yield the following dichotomy on the behaviour of the radius of random information compared to the minimal radius of information. Namely, if
\|\sigma\|_{2}<\infty
, then the radius of random information,
r(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},G_{n,m})
, is close to optimal for all
m\,(>n)
. On the other hand, if not, then random information is asymptotically useless if
m\,(>n)
is large enough. Both statements hold with exponentially high probability.

Remark 3.1
.

It should be noted that in
[
HKN+21
]
also an upper bound more suitable for exponentially decaying sequences of semiaxes was obtained, where one looses at most a polynomial factor. We will not consider this but focus our attention on polynomially decaying semiaxes later on.

We wish to extend the results about ellipsoids and investigate the above dichotomy for the more general
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids, defined as follows.

Definition 3.2
.

An
m
-dimensional
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid,
0<p\leq\infty
, with semiaxes of length
\sigma_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{m}>0
is given by

\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}:=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\colon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{|x_{i}|^{p}}{\sigma_{i}^{p}}\leq 1\Big\},

where the sum over the
p
-th powers is replaced by the maximum if
p=\infty
.

The
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
is the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
equipped with the (quasi-)norm

\|x\|_{p,\sigma}:=\|(x_{i}/\sigma_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\|_{p},\quad\text{}x\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

(3.6)

and therefore it is convex if and only if
p\geq 1
. See Figure
3.1
for an illustration.

aaa
p=2

aaa
p=1

aaa
p=\frac{1}{2}

aaa
p=\infty

Figure 3.1
:
Two-dimensional
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with semiaxes
\sigma_{2}=0.6\,\sigma_{1}
.

As a first motivation to study
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids, consider the diagonal operator
D_{\sigma}
with multiplier sequence
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})
taking
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to
D_{\sigma}(x)=(\sigma_{1}x_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}x_{m})
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
is the image of the
\ell_{p}
-unit ball
\mathbb{B}_{p}^{m}
under
D_{\sigma}
, and a change of variables shows that, for any linear subspace
E_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n})=\sup_{\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap E_{n}}\|x\|_{2}=\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{p}^{m}\cap E_{n}}\|D_{\sigma}x\|_{2}.

By taking the infimum over all subspaces of codimension
n
we obtain

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)=c_{n+1}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{2}^{m})\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.7)

This relation is used in Appendix
A.1
to determine the minimal radius for the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids.

In the study of operators between (quasi-) Banach spaces, diagonal operators are important prototypes, in part due to their particular structure, and in part because some interesting operators act like them. For example, differential operators on periodic functions may be analyzed via diagonal operators applied to their Fourier coefficients, and also embeddings between Besov and Sobolev spaces can be modeled using them. We refer to Carl
[
Car81
]
, König
[
Kön86
, Ch. 3.c]
, Kühn
[
Küh05
]
, Linde
[
Lin85
]
, Pietsch
[
Pie87
, Ch. 7.7.1]
and Schütt
[
Sch84
]
for detailed information on this topic.

Additionally, we would like to mention that
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids have been studied quite recently by Juhos and Prochno
[
JP22
]
with focus on the asymptotic volume distribution of the intersection of two such generalized ellipsoids as the dimension of the underlying space tends to infinity, and by van Handel
[
van18
]
as examples to investigate the intricacies of entropy bounds on the expected supremum of Gaussian processes.

To give more details, it has been observed by Talagrand in
[
Tal14
, Ch. 2.5]
that ellipsoids
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m}
are examples where the generic chaining approach is superior to Dudley’s bound on the expected supremum of a Gaussian process
(Z_{t})_{t\in K}
with
Z_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{j}t_{j}
, which is indexed by a convex body
K
, in terms of entropy numbers of
K
is not optimal. Intuitively, this is because an ellipsoid is smaller than its entropy numbers suggest. As noted by van Handel
[
van18
]
, this extends to
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids. We will discuss this in the upcoming section.

At the end of this section, let us briefly present how
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids relate to the following spaces of periodic functions, which were introduced by Sprengel
[
Spr00
]
in order to generalize error estimates for interpolation using sparse grids.

Definition 3.3
.

For
1\leq q\leq\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
define

A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):=\Big\{f\in D^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\colon\|f\|_{A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}:=\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big|(1+\|k\|_{2}^{2})^{\alpha/2}c_{k}(f)\big|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty\Big\},

where
\big(c_{k}(f)\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
are the Fourier coefficients of
f
belonging to
D^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the space of periodic distributions on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

This scale of function spaces encompasses the Wiener algebra of periodic functions with absolutely summable Fourier coefficients (
q=1
and
\alpha=0
) and periodic Sobolev spaces (
q=2
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
). In one dimension, the case
q=\infty
and
\alpha>1
corresponds to Korobov spaces, whose multi-dimensional generalizations are integral to the study of lattice rules, see, e.g., Niederreiter
[
Nie92
, Ch. 4]
. In general, a description of the space
A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
in terms of smoothness is
probably
out of reach as already in one dimension there is no precise relation between smoothness and decay conditions on the Fourier coefficients.

To make precise how the function spaces of Definition
3.3
are connected to
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids, enumerate the integer points
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by
\big(k(j)\big)_{j=1}^{\infty}
such that their Euclidean norm is non-decreasing. Then, for every
f\in A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the sum defining its norm can be regrouped to

\|f\|_{A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{q}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{|c_{k(j)}|^{q}}{\sigma_{j}^{q}}

where
\sigma_{j}:=(1+\|k(j)\|_{2}^{2})^{-\alpha/2}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, via the correspondence
f\leftrightarrow\big(c_{k}(f)\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, the unit ball of
A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
may be identified with an infinite-dimensional
\ell_{q}
-ellipsoid. Then a straightforward volume estimate implies the asymptotic relation
\sigma_{j}\asymp j^{-\alpha/d}
, and if
\alpha/d>1-1/q=1/q^{*}
or
\alpha=0
and
q=1
, then
A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is embedded into the continuous functions. Curiously enough, we will encounter this condition on the semiaxes in our study of the behaviour of the radius of a random section.

3.2
Discussion of results

Having sufficiently motivated the study of sections of
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with random subspaces, we discuss our results obtained in
[
HPS21
]
. We first state a general upper bound on the radius
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})
of a random section for
1\leq p\leq\infty
(Theorem
3.4
), which we compare with the bound of (
3.2
) given in
[
LT00
]
. Then we discuss Corollary
3.7
which extends a bound on the supremum of a Gaussian process indexed by an
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid obtained by van Handel
[
van18
]
. Afterwards an upper bound on the typical radius (Theorem
3.9
) obtained in the range
0<p<1
together with a consequence (Corollary
3.10
) for the Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators is presented.

In Section
3.3
we shall discuss ramifications for polynomial semiaxes. We shall also state a lower bound (Proposition
3.11
) and discuss a dichotomy for the optimality of Gaussian information depending on the decay of the semiaxes. This leads us to Conjecture
3.13
.

The following main result bounds the typical radius from above via the
p^{*}
-norm of the tail of the sequence of semiaxes. Here and in the following, the parameter
p^{*}
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
, that is
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{*}}=1
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
HPS21
, Thm. A]
)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
, we have

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\begin{cases}n^{-1/2}\sup\limits_{k\leq j\leq m}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}&\text{if }p=1,\\
\sqrt{p^{*}}n^{-1/2}\Big(\sum\limits_{j=k}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}}&\text{if }1<p\leq\infty,\end{cases}

with probability at least
1-c_{1}\exp(-c_{2}n)
, where
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)
are absolute constants, and
k=k(n)\asymp n
for
p=1
while
k=k(n)\asymp\frac{n}{p^{*}}
for
p>1
.

The proof will be discussed in Section
3.4
.

Remark 3.5
.

The notation we use here is different from
[
HPS21
]
, where we used
\mathrm{rad}
also for the radius of information and the radius of Gaussian information
r(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},G_{n,m})
. Here, the radius of a typical section
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})=\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},\ker G_{n,m})
is used to state the results. However, by Proposition
2.9
these notions are equivalent up to a constant of two if
p\geq 1
and a constant depending on
p
otherwise. To see this, note that
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
is the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{p,\sigma}
, which satisfies, by the
p
-triangle inequality,

\|x+y\|_{p,\sigma}\leq 2^{(1/p-1)_{+}}\big(\|x\|_{p,\sigma}+\|y\|_{p,\sigma}\big)\quad\text{for every }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Theorem
3.4
canonically extends the bound (
3.4
) obtained in
[
HKN+21
]
for the case
p=2
and shows that if
\sigma\in\ell_{p^{*}}
we get a bound on the radius of most sections. In order to compare this with the more general upper bound (
3.2
) from above, obtained by Litvak and Tomczak-Jaegermann in
[
LT00
]
, we introduce the following concept. Similar to
[
HKN+21
]
we will be interested in bounds which are independent of the dimension
m
and may be extended to infinite
m
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
. The Lorentz (quasi-)norm of a (possibly finite) sequence
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
is given by

\|x\|_{p,q}:=\|i^{1/p-1/q}x_{i}^{*}\|_{q},

where
x_{1}^{*}\geq x_{2}^{*}\geq\cdots
is the non-increasing rearrangement of
(|x_{i}|)_{i=1}^{\infty}
, see, e.g.,
[
Pie87
, Ch. 2.1]
.

For convenience let us recall that (
3.2
) yields

\mathrm{cr}_{n}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m})\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=\lfloor c\,n\rfloor}^{m}\frac{c_{j}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m})}{\sqrt{j}},\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}

with exponentially high probability in
n
.

To compare this with the upper bound of Theorem
3.4
, we need to know the behaviour of the Gelfand widths
\big(c_{n}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m})\big)_{n\leq m}
or, equivalently, the minimal radius of information. Due to equality (
3.7
), we can use results about Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators to infer the behaviour of
\big(c_{n}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m})\big)_{n\leq m}
in terms of the sequence of semiaxes
\sigma
. We collect these results in Appendix
A.1
and note that for
p<2
one knows this behaviour only to polynomial order.

The bound in (
3.2
) is finite for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
if and only if
\big(c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{2})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell_{2,1}
. From Proposition
A.2
in Appendix
A.1
one can deduce that for
p\geq 2
this holds if and only if
\sigma\in\ell_{p^{*},1}
. This is a stronger requirement than
\sigma\in\ell_{p^{*}}
, which is needed by Theorem
3.4
for a finite upper bound independent of
m
. Note that this observation is already true for
p=2
and thus could have been made after the statement of (
3.4
).

Let us elaborate. For
p=2
the semiaxes coincide with the minimal radii, i.e., we have
\sigma_{n+1}=\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},n)
. If
r(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},n)\asymp n^{-\lambda}(\log n)^{\alpha}
for
\lambda>1/2
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
both (
3.2
) and (
3.4
) imply that the radius of a random section satisfies

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\lesssim\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m},n)

(3.8)

with exponentially high probability, i.e., we have asymptotic optimality of random information. If
\lambda=1/2
and
\alpha<-1/2
, then the bound (
3.4
) gives (
3.8
) with an additional factor of
\sqrt{\log n}
on the right-hand side. This improves upon the general bound (
3.2
) by a factor of
\sqrt{\log n}
. A slightly smaller improvement of
(\log n)^{1/p}
may be deduced in the case of
p>2
and
\alpha<-1+1/p
from Theorem
3.4
and Proposition
A.1
.

Summarizing, we may say that, applied to
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
with
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the bound (
3.2
) is in general slightly weaker than Theorem
3.4
. However, we wish to emphasize that the bounds differ only at an logarithmic scale and for comparing the polynomial order, as done in Section
3.3
, they are of equal strength.

As a consequence of the proof of Theorem
3.4
, more precisely of Proposition
3.15
in Section
3.4
below, we obtain the following corollary on the supremum of a Gaussian process indexed by an
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid.

Corollary 3.7

(
[
HPS21
, Cor. 2]
)
.

Let
g_{1},g_{2},\dots
be independent standard Gaussians. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{y\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}y_{j}\leq C\begin{cases}\sup\limits_{1\leq j\leq m}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}&\text{if }p=1,\\
\sqrt{p^{*}}\big(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\big)^{1/p^{*}}&\text{if }1<p\leq\infty.\end{cases}

In view of the dependence on the parameter
p
, this bound extends the mentioned work by van Handel
[
van18
]
who deduced it for
1\leq p<\infty
but with an unspecified constant in
p
which cannot be obtained using his approach, see
[
van18
, Remark 3.4]
. In Corollary
3.7
, we obtain an upper bound on the behavior in
p
and thus complement his result.

Remark 3.8
.

Employing estimates for entropy numbers of diagonal operators from
[
Car81
]
as in
[
van18
]
, it can be deduced that the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
with semiaxes satisfying

\sigma\in\ell_{p^{*}}\text{ but }\sigma\not\in\ell_{p^{*},1}\text{ for }1<p<\infty\quad\text{or}\quad\quad\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}<\infty\text{ but }\sigma\not\in\ell_{\infty,1}

is an example where Dudley’s bound, in contrast to Corollary
3.7
, fails to give a finite bound if the dimension becomes large. In the case
1<p<\infty
, van Handel explains this with the fact that the entropy numbers need not be estimated for the whole set but for certain thin subsets, in this case dilations of
\ell_{2p-2}
-ellipsoids. For
p\geq 2
an alternative explanation can be given in terms of
p
-convexity, see
[
Tal14
, Ch. 4]
. Note that in finite dimension Dudley’s bound can be off by at most
\log m
, see
[
Tal14
, Ch. 2]
.

Regarding the case
0<p<1
, it seems that little is known in general about random sections of unit balls of quasi-Banach spaces and different techniques are necessary than in the case of convex bodies. Relying on methods commonly used in the field of compressed sensing, e.g., in a work of Foucart, Pajor, Rauhut and T. Ullrich
[
FPRU10
]
on the Gelfand widths of
\ell_{p}
-balls in the quasi-Banach regime, see also Donoho
[
Don06
]
, we deduce the following upper bound for the radius of random information when the semiaxes have polynomial decay.

Theorem 3.9

(
[
HPS21
, Thm. B]
)
.

Let
0<p\leq 1
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda>0
. Then there exist constants
c,C,D>0
such that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
1\leq n<m
with

n\geq D\log({\rm e}m/n),

we have

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\,\Big(\frac{\log({\rm e}m/n)}{n}\Big)^{\lambda+1/p-1/2}

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-c\,n)
.
