To the best of our knowledge, this seems to be the best known upper bound for the minimal radius of sections of
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with
0<p<1
and thus the best information available is given by random Gaussian measurements. Below, we shall prove the more general Theorem
3.24
, where the radius is measured in the
\ell_{q}
-quasi-norm for
p<q\leq 2
. This yields the following bound on Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators in the quasi-Banach regime and extends known results on the identity between
\ell_{p}^{m}
and
\ell_{q}^{m}
from the mentioned works
[
Don06
]
and
[
FPRU10
]
.

Corollary 3.10

(
[
HPS21
, Cor. 4]
)
.

Let
0<p\leq 1
and
p<q\leq 2
. Assume that
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda>0
. Then there exist constants
C,D>0
such that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
1\leq n<m
with

n\geq D\log({\rm e}m/n),

we have

c_{n+1}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m})\leq C\,\Big(\frac{\log({\rm e}m/n)}{n}\Big)^{\lambda+1/p-1/q}.

3.3
Discussion of results – polynomial semiaxes

In the following, we discuss the consequences of our results for
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with
1\leq p\leq\infty
and polynomially decaying semiaxes. Our main reason for doing so is to be able to compare the minimal radius
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)
with the radius of typical sections
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})
and to make progress towards extending the dichotomy for the ellipsoids
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m}
mentioned in Section
3.1
. We give some further motivation to look at polynomial semiaxes.

An incentive can be seen in the function spaces
A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
of Definition
3.3
, whose unit ball can be identified with an infinite-dimensional
\ell_{q}
-ellipsoid with semiaxes
\sigma_{j}\asymp j^{-\alpha/d}
. Let us mention here that Theorem
3.4
can be applied to this sequence if and only if
\alpha/d>1/p^{*}
. In this case, one could use an isomorphism to obtain a bound on random linear information for the spaces
A_{q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

For the sake of further motivation, let us mention that the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
equipped with the Lorentz (quasi-)norm
\|\cdot\|_{p,q}
bears some similarity to an
\ell_{q}
-ellipsoid with semiaxes
\sigma_{j}=j^{1/q-1/p}
. Under the assumption that the coefficients are ordered non-increasingly, one may interpret recovery of functions whose Fourier or wavelet coefficients belong to a Lorentz ball as a possible application of our results. This includes for example nonlinear approximation classes, in part related to Besov spaces, see for example
[
DeV98
]
.

Let now
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and some
\lambda>0
. Equivalent results hold if this is weakened to
\sigma_{j}\asymp j^{-\lambda}
. In order to assess the size of the radius of a typical section of
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
, we need to know the minimal radius
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Its behavior is known exactly when
p\geq 2
but can only be deduced up to subpolynomial factors when
1\leq p<2
, see Appendix
A.1
. Therefore, we will ignore subpolynomial terms in the following discussion and use only the rate of polynomial decay to compare.

To make this precise, we define the rate of polynomial decay (in
n
) of an infinite array
a=(a_{n,m})_{m\in\mathbb{N},1\leq n<m}
of real numbers by

\mathrm{decay}(a):=\sup\{\varrho\geq 0\colon\exists C>0\text{ with }a_{n,m}\leq C\,n^{-\varrho}\text{ for all }m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }1\leq n<m\}.

This definition is derived from the concept of a diagonal limit order used to study Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators arising from a polynomially decaying sequence, see, e.g., Pietsch
[
Pie07
, 6.2.5.3]
.

Further, we need to suppose that
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some

\lambda>\frac{1}{s}:=\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\Big)_{+}=\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p},0\Big\},

since the minimal radius does not decay if
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{s}
. For the decay rate of the minimal radius Corollary
A.4
implies

\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big)=\begin{cases}\lambda\cdot\frac{p^{*}}{2}&\text{if }1\leq p<2\text{ and }\lambda<\frac{1}{p^{*}},\\
\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.9)

By Theorem
3.4
, if
\sigma
decays polynomially with exponent
\lambda>\frac{1}{p^{*}}
, then there is
C>0
such that the radius of a random section satisfies, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
,

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\begin{cases}n^{-\lambda-1/2}\sqrt{\log n}&\text{if }p=1,\\
n^{-\lambda+1/2-1/p}&\text{if }1<p\leq\infty,\end{cases}

(3.10)

with probability
1-c_{1}\exp(-c_{2}n)
.

Similar to the above, let us define
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)
to be the supremum over all
\varrho\geq 0
such there exist
C,C_{1},C_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq Cn^{-\varrho}

holds with probability at least
1-C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}n)
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
. This notion is somewhat related to the notion of the random Gelfand widths.

Then the combination of (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) implies, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\lambda>\frac{1}{p^{*}}
,

\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}=\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big).

(3.11)

That is, the rate of decay of random information is equal to the one of optimal information which provides a positive partial answer to Question
2.2
for
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with polynomially decaying semiaxes.

Therefore, the bound of Theorem
3.4
yields an optimal bound on the rate of polynomial decay of typical sections in the case of
\lambda>\frac{1}{p^{*}}
. However, if
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{p^{*}}
, it does not yield a useful result. Instead, we have the following lower bound on the radius of a random section if
1<p\leq 2
and
m
is large enough compared to
n
.

Proposition 3.11

(
[
HPS21
, Prop. 1]
)
.

Let
1<p\leq 2
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda},j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda
with
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{p^{*}}
. Then, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1),

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
m>n
large enough, we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

In other words, if
1<p\leq 2
and the semiaxes decay too slowly compared to
\frac{1}{p^{*}}
, random information is asymptotically as good as no information at all, that is, its radius does not decay as the following corollary summarizes in terms of random sections.

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
1\leq p\leq 2
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda},j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda
with
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{p^{*}}
. Then

\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=0.

We visualize
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)
, or equivalently the rate of decay of the radius of random information, in Figure
3.2
and discuss it in the following.

\frac{1}{2}
1
\lambda
\frac{1}{2}
1
\frac{1}{p}
p=1
\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}
?
0
0

Figure 3.2
:
This diagram depicts our knowledge about
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)
in the
\frac{1}{p}-\lambda
-plane, where
0<p,\lambda\leq\infty
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda},j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Above the line
\lambda=1-\frac{1}{p}
, where
1\leq p\leq\infty,
we just deduced that Theorem
3.4
yields that random information is optimal up to an additional logarithmic factor if
p=1
. The decay rate is equal to
\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}
, see (
3.11
). As noted above, below and including the line
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}
, where
2\leq p\leq\infty,
optimal information does not decay at all, in other words, information is useless and does not help to recover vectors. Geometrically, this corresponds to the fact that, no matter how large the codimension
n\,(<m)
of a subspace is, the section with
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
has a radius bounded from below.

In the square, that is for
p\geq 2
and
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
, it follows via
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{m}\subset\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
from the lower bound (
3.5
) for the case
p=2
that, no matter how large we choose
n
, if
m
is large enough, then with high probability
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})
is bounded from below by a constant. That is,
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=0
and so random information is useless. By Corollary
3.12
this also holds for the triangle given by
1<p<2
and
0<\lambda<1-\frac{1}{p}
.

Finally, note that on the right-hand side of the dashed line where
p=1
, that is, where
0<p<1
, Theorem
3.9
provides an upper bound with decay rate
\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}
, which depends on
m
. We do not have a corresponding lower bound for optimal information in this region.

We pose the following conjecture claiming that there is a threshold of decay separating regimes of completely different behavior of random information.

Conjecture 3.13
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda},j\in\mathbb{N},
with
\lambda>\frac{1}{s}
. Then,

\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big)&\text{if }\lambda>\frac{1}{p^{*}},\\
0&\text{if }\lambda\leq\frac{1}{p^{*}}.\end{cases}

In fact, this dichotomy may also be true more generally for non-polynomial semiaxes depending on whether
\|\sigma\|_{p^{*}}<\infty
or not, see Question
3.3
in Section
3.7
.

3.4
Low
M^{*}
-estimates and the rounding of ellipsoids

In this section we will explain how Theorem
3.4
follows from a (low(er))
M^{*}
-estimate using a rounded version of the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
. To illustrate the ideas behind this approach, which has been used already in
[
HKN+21
]
, and in a similar form also in
[
LT00
]
(and many other papers), we will consider a general convex body
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

As mentioned, unit balls of normed spaces and in particular the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids, where
1\leq p\leq\infty
, are examples of convex bodies, and additionally symmetric. In the following,
K
need not be symmetric but for convenience we assume that it contains the origin in its interior.

A well-known type of result in the field of asymptotic geometric analysis is the low
M^{*}
-estimate, which consists of an estimate of the radius of the intersection of
K
with a random subspace in terms of its mean width and a factor depending on the proportion of the dimension of subspace. Let us introduce the necessary notions and give a formal statement together with some history.

The mean width of
K
measures the average width of
K
over all directions belonging to the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\colon\|x\|_{2}=1\}
. To formally define it, let
h_{K}\colon\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\to\mathbb{R}
,
u\mapsto\sup_{y\in K}\langle u,y\rangle
be the support function of
K
, which measures the distance from the origin of the supporting hyperplane in direction
u
. That is, the width into direction
u
is given by
h_{K}(u)+h_{K}(-u)
. The (half) mean width of
K
is then

M^{*}(K):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{m-1}}h_{K}(u)\,{\rm d}\sigma^{m-1}(u),

(3.12)

where
\sigma^{m-1}
is the normalized surface measure on
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
. Intuitively, this is the expected radius of a one-dimensional random subspace.

Using the probabilistic representation of the uniform measure on
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
in terms of standard Gaussians (see Section
2.2
) shows that
M^{*}(K)
can be written as the supremum of a Gaussian process (see, e.g.,
[
AGM15
, Lem. 9.1.3]
), namely,

M^{*}(K)=\frac{1}{c_{m}}\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in K}\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{j}g_{j},

(3.13)

where
g_{1},g_{2},\ldots
are independent standard Gaussian random variables and
c_{m}\asymp\sqrt{m}
.

We will state now a form of the lower
M^{*}
-estimate as presented in
[
AGM15
, Thm. 9.3.8]
with
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
there.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a convex body containing the origin in its interior. For any
1\leq n<m
there exists a subset of the Grassmannian
\mathcal{G}_{m,m-n}
with Haar measure at least
1-\frac{7}{2}\exp(-\frac{1}{72}a_{n}^{2})
such that for any subspace
E_{n}
in this set and all
x\in K\cap E_{n}
we have

\|x\|_{2}\leq 2\frac{a_{m}}{a_{n}}M^{*}(K),

where, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

a_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}g_{j}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\Gamma((k+1)/2)}{\Gamma(k/2)}\asymp\sqrt{k}.

(3.14)

Thus it implies

\mathrm{rad}(K,E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq 2\frac{a_{m}}{a_{n}}M^{*}(K),

for the random subspace
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
with the claimed probability.

The statement of Proposition
3.14
is adapted from Gordon
[
Gor88
]
who proved it using a min-max principle to derive a result on subspaces escaping through a mesh. In
[
LT00
]
and
[
AGM15
, Ch. 7]
one may find some history starting with V. D. Milman
[
Mil85a
,
Mil85b
]
. In particular, Gordon’s result improves upon the work
[
PT86
]
due to Pajor and Tomczak-Jaegermann who obtained a larger constant. In fact, this would be sufficient for our purposes as we do not care about the size of the absolute constants.

As in the mentioned works
[
LT00
]
,
[
LPT06
]
and
[
MPT07
]
, see also
[
AGM15
, Ch. 7.5]
, we use the technique of rounding to improve the power of the low
M^{*}
-estimates as follows. Define the
rounded body
by

K_{\varrho}:=K\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}=\{x\in K\colon\|x\|_{2}\leq\varrho\},

where
\varrho>0
is some cutoff threshold. The idea to obtain a bound on the radius of the section with the random subspace
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
is then to derive a bound on
M^{*}(K_{\varrho})
for an unspecified
\varrho>0
, which gives by the
M^{*}
-estimate a bound on
\mathrm{rad}(K_{\varrho},E_{n})
for most realizations
E_{n}
of
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
, and to afterwards minimize over all
\varrho>0
such that

\mathrm{rad}(K_{\varrho},E_{n})<\varrho\quad\text{and consequently}\quad\mathrm{rad}(K,E_{n})<\varrho

(3.15)

holds for these subspaces. The improvement in this case comes from the fact that we can decrease the mean width
M^{*}(K_{\varrho})
by decreasing
\varrho
and thus cut off the peaky parts of
K
, which are somewhat irrelevant to random subspaces.

For the proof of Theorem
3.4
we apply this technique to the
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
which results in cutting away the contribution of the first
cn
semiaxes, where
0<c<1
. This will be a consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.15

(
[
HPS21
, Prop. 3]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
0\leq k<m
and
\varrho>0
,

M^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m})\lesssim\begin{cases}m^{-1/2}\Big(\varrho\sqrt{k}+\sup\limits_{k+1\leq j\leq m}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}\Big)&\text{if }p=1,\\
\sqrt{p^{*}}m^{-1/2}\Big(\varrho\sqrt{k}+\big(\sum\limits_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\big)^{1/p^{*}}\Big)&\text{if }1<p\leq\infty.\end{cases}

This follows from an application of Hölder’s inequality together with estimates on the norm of a Gaussian vector. A similar approach was used by Gordon, Litvak, Mendelson and Pajor in
[
GLMP07
]
for estimating the supremum of a Gaussian process indexed by the intersection of the
\ell_{p}
-ball
\mathbb{B}_{p}^{m}
with a scaled
\ell_{q}
-ball
\varrho\mathbb{B}_{q}^{m}
. Using a suitable cutoff
\varrho>0
, which we will not attempt to optimize, one can obtain from Proposition
3.15
the proof of Theorem
3.4
. The details are deferred to Appendix
A.2
.

Now, Corollary
3.7
follows from Proposition
3.15
by setting
k=0
and
\varrho>0
large enough such that
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\subset\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}
together with the representation (
3.13
) of the expected supremum of a Gaussian process indexed by a convex body.

3.5
A lower bound

The idea behind the proof of the lower bound of Proposition
3.11
for polynomial semiaxes in the case
1<p\leq 2
is as follows. To bound the radius of
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
intersected with a random subspace from below, we have to find with a certain probability a vector in it which has large enough norm. In fact, it is often even possible to find a vector having a single large coordinate with high probability as the following observation made in
[
HKN+21
]
shows.

Lemma 3.16

(
[
HKN+21
, Lem. 25]
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
it holds that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
,

\mathbb{P}\Big[\sup\big\{x_{1}^{2}\colon\|x\|_{2}=1,G_{n,m}x=0\big\}\geq 1-\frac{n}{\varepsilon m}\Big]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Using this, we can prove the following more general version of Proposition
3.11
.

Proposition 3.17

(
[
HPS21
, Prop. 4]
)
.

Let
1<p\leq 2
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
with
n\leq\varepsilon\sigma_{m}^{2}m^{2/p^{*}}
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\geq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{1+\sigma_{1}}\Big]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

In order to prove Proposition
3.17
we take a random vector as given by Lemma
3.16
which belongs to the intersection of the unit sphere with the kernel of
G_{n,m}
and has a large first coordinate. Then we show using Hölder’s inequality that the renormalization by the factor
1+1/\sigma_{1}
lies in
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
provided that the dimension
m
is large enough compared to
n
when
\sigma_{m}m^{1/p^{*}}
is increasing. For details we refer to Appendix
A.2
.

In the case
p=2
this method yields
bounds which are optimal with respect to polynomial order, see
[
HPS21
, Prop. 24]
.
The restriction to

1\leq p\leq 2
is due to Hölder’s inequality and different ideas seem to be necessary for the case
p>2
. We do not know if also there it is sufficient to exhibit vectors with a single large coordinate but we believe that this may be true. A possible approach could consist in finding a variant of Lemma
3.16
where the sphere is replaced by the surface of the cube.

Remark 3.18
.

From Proposition
3.17
it can be deduced that the statement of Proposition
3.11
remains true in the case of
\sigma_{j}=j^{-1/p^{*}}a_{j},j\in\mathbb{N},
with
a_{j}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
.

Remark 3.19
.

In
[
HKN+21
]
both bounds (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) were proven using random matrices. In particular, knowledge on the singular values of structured Gaussian matrices was employed and the Hilbert space structure was used in a crucial way. This is the reason why we could not extend the method they used without the loss of additional factors in the dimension
m
. Perhaps, new results on structured Gaussian random matrices may be of help, see, e.g., Guédon, Hinrichs, Litvak and Prochno
[
GHLP17
]
.

3.6
Sparse approximation

In order to describe the ideas behind the proof of Theorem
3.9
, we give a short introduction to a subfield of compressed sensing, known as sparse approximation or recovery. We refer to the book of Foucart and Rauhut
[
FR13
]
for a mathematical background on the subject.

It is in part due to random matrices that sparse recovery experienced a relatively recent surge of interest. The seminal papers of Candès, Romberg and Tao
[
CRT06
]
and Donoho
[
Don06
]
were among the first to investigate this and found that random matrices satisfy with high probability the restricted isometry property which enables exact reconstruction of sparse vectors. Further, an universality principle has been discovered, namely, random matrices can be used to gather measurements which are effective independently of the basis with respect to which a signal is sparse.

The fact that random information is the best information available for many problems in sparse recovery is reflected in the statement of Theorem
3.9
which will be deduced from the more general Theorem
3.24
below. To this end, let us give some background on sparse approximation.

Let
m,s\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq s\leq m
. A vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is called
s
-sparse if at most
s
of its coordinates are non-zero. Given information
N_{n}z=y
, where
N_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is a suitable linear information map, sparse vectors can be reconstructed via
\ell_{p}
-minimization, where
0<p\leq 1
, that is,

\Delta_{p}(y):=\text{arg min}\|z\|_{p}\quad\text{subject to }N_{n}z=y.

(3.16)

Note that
\Delta_{p}
is a map from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
which depends on
N_{n}
. If the matrix
N_{n}
satisfies the restricted isometry property with a small restricted isometry constant
\delta_{2s}(N_{n})
of order
2s
, which is the smallest
\delta>0
such that

(1-\delta)\|z\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|Az\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1+\delta)\|z\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{for all }2s\text{-sparse }z\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

(3.17)

then
s
-sparse vectors
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
can be recovered exactly, i.e.,
z=\Delta_{p}(N_{n}z)
. This
is
a consequence of the next proposition which gives a bound on the error of reconstruction using
\ell_{p}
-minimization in terms of the following concept.

Definition 3.20
.

Given
0<p\leq\infty
, the error of best
s
-term approximation of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
in the
\ell_{p}
-(quasi-)norm is

\sigma_{s}(x)_{p}:=\inf\{\|x-z\|_{p}\colon z\text{ is }s\text{-sparse}\}.

Proposition 3.21

(
[
FPRU10
, Thm. 1.3]
)
.

If
0<p\leq 1
and
N_{n}
satisfies the restricted isometry property with constant
\delta_{2s}<\sqrt{2}-1
, then, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\|x-\Delta_{p}(N_{n}x)\|_{p}\leq C^{1/p}\sigma_{s}(x)_{p},

where
C>0
is a constant that depends only on
\delta_{2s}
. In particular, the reconstruction of
s
-sparse vectors is exact.

The restricted isometry property can be interpreted as a condition of uniformity on the information map
N_{n}
, which is satisfied by a rescaling of the Gaussian matrix
G_{n,m}=(g_{ij})_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m}
with high probability as the following result shows.

Proposition 3.22

(
[
FR13
, Thm. 9.2]
)
.

For every
\delta\in(0,1)
there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
the restricted isometry property
\delta_{s}(n^{-1/2}G_{n,m})\leq\delta
holds with probability at least
1-2\exp(-C_{2}n)
provided that

n\geq C_{1}s\log({\rm e}m/s).

In fact, the statement is more general and applies also to subgaussian random matrices, see also Remark
2.11
. Taken together, Propositions
3.21
and
3.22
show that, provided that enough, in fact not much more than the number of its non-zero entries if
m
is not too large compared to
n
, Gaussian measurements are available, a sparse vector can be reconstructed exactly with high probability using
\ell_{p}
-minimization. Together with the following lemma this completes the proof strategy for Theorem
3.9
.

Lemma 3.23

(
[
HPS21
, Lem. 4]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, for some
\lambda>(1/q-1/p)_{+}
. Then, for all
1\leq s\leq m/2
,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}}\sigma_{s}(x)_{q}\asymp s^{-\lambda+1/q-1/p},

where the implicit constant is independent of
s
and
m
.

Its proof, given in Appendix
A.3
, is inspired by the proof for the
\ell_{p}
-balls which can be found in Vybíral
[
Vyb12
]
.

To state the afore mentioned generalization of Theorem
3.9
, where we measure the radius in the quasi-norm of
\ell_{q}^{m}
instead of
\ell_{2}^{m}
, we define the radius
\mathrm{rad}_{G}(K,E)
of the section of any set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
with a linear subspace
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
in
G
by

\mathrm{rad}_{G}(K,E):=\sup_{x\in K\cap E}\|x\|_{G},

where
G
is equipped with a quasi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{G}
. By Proposition
2.9
it is related to the radius of information for recovery of vectors in
K
in
\|\cdot\|_{G}
.

Theorem 3.24

(
[
HPS21
, Thm. C]
)
.

Let
0<p\leq 1
and
p<q\leq 2
. Assume that
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda>0
. Then there exist constants
c,C,D>0
such that, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
1\leq n<m
with

n\geq D\log({\rm e}m/n),

we have

\mathrm{rad}_{\ell_{q}^{m}}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\,\Big(\frac{\log({\rm e}m/n)}{n}\Big)^{\lambda+1/p-1/q}

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-c\,n)
.

The proof of this theorem is a straightforward adaption of the proof of
[
FPRU10
, Thm. 3.2]
and relies on Lemma
3.23
as well as other techniques from sparse recovery. Again, details are provided in Appendix
A.3
.

Remark 3.25
.

Our original proof of Theorem
3.24
as given in the preprint
[
HPS21
]
used
\ell_{r}
-minimization with
r=p
which does not yield the claimed bound without additional modifications. Instead of giving a corrected version of this variant, we can set
r=\min\{1,q\}
as in the proof of
[
FPRU10
, Thm. 3.2]
since Lemma
3.23
is more general than
[
HPS21
, Lem. 4]
. To keep the presentation self-contained, we give a complete proof instead of only stating the required modifications.

3.7
Open questions

Question 3.1
.

As mentioned in Section
3.1
, knowledge of a single minimal section may imply a bound on typical sections of similar dimension which is in contrast to (
3.2
), derived from
[
LT00
]
, where knowledge of the whole sequence
\big(c_{n}(K)\big)_{1\leq n\leq m}
is needed. This difference has been observed at the end of Section 1.2 in
[
MY21
]
by E. Milman and Yifrach, who stated that it can be expected that knowledge of the whole sequence of minimal radii is needed to obtain good upper bounds,
but a precise formulation is unavailable
.

Is the knowledge of the whole sequence of Gelfand widths necessary to obtain an optimal bound on the radius of a random section?

Question 3.2
.

To the best of our knowledge, general lower bounds on the radius of typical information or the random Gelfand widths, which could complement the upper bound (
3.2
), are not available. Related to Question
3.1
we pose the following one.

Can the upper bound (
3.2
) be improved? What about lower bounds?

It would be interesting to have for example a negative statement if the Gelfand widths are not square-summable. We hope that our study of
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids sheds some light on these questions.

Question 3.3
.

As discussed in Section
3.3
, Conjecture
3.13
is verified except for the two cases

1.

1<p<2
and
\lambda=\frac{1}{p^{*}}
,

2.

p>2
and
\frac{1}{2}<\lambda\leq\frac{1}{p^{*}}
.

Can one prove Conjecture
3.13
or find counterexamples to it?

It seems reasonable to conjecture even that
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big)
as long as
\|(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\|_{p^{*}}<\infty
, while
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\big)=0
whenever
\|(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\|_{p^{*}}=\infty
. With an eye on the case
p=2
, it may also be true that the threshold is determined by the square-summability of the sequence of minimal radii or Gelfand widths.

Question 3.4
.

In Corollary
3.7
we obtained an upper bound on the dependence on
p
of the supremum of a Gaussian process indexed by
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
. As we do not have a corresponding lower bound, the following would be interesting to know.

What is the correct dependence in Corollary
3.7
on
p
?

A possible approach could be based on the equality case in Hölder’s inequality used for the proof. See also
[
GLMP07
]
, where lower bounds on the mean width are provided.

Question 3.5
.

If
0<p<1
, we do not have a lower bound on the minimal radius of information and therefore cannot compare typical with optimal information.

How sharp is Theorem
3.9
or its extension Theorem
3.24
?

We comment on a possible approach to a lower bound to Theorem
3.24
or Theorem
3.9
and more generally a lower bound on the Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators in the quasi-Banach regime. In the case of the
\ell_{p}
-balls, that is
\sigma_{1}=\cdots=\sigma_{m}
, a matching lower bound is provided in
[
FPRU10
, Prop. 2.1]
. Its proof relies on a combinatorial lemma providing a partially overlapping covering of the index set
\{1,\ldots,m\}
and does not extend to
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with decaying semiaxes.

In
[
FPRU10
]
the authors had to find a different method of proof than the one used by Donoho
[
Don06
]
who relied on the validity of Carl’s inequality, which compares entropy numbers with Gelfand numbers, in quasi-Banach spaces. However, this was only shown several years later by Hinrichs, Kolleck and Vybíral
[
HKV16
]
. With this additional knowledge one could possibly prove a lower bound to Theorem
3.24
using a lower bound on the entropy numbers of diagonal operators as shown by Kühn
[
Küh01
]
for the case of the identity operator.

Question 3.6
.

An important tool in the proof of Theorem
3.9
, Lemma
3.23
itself is proven only for polynomial semiaxes. Naturally, the following question arises.

Can Lemma
3.23
be generalized to other types of semiaxes?

A generalization to other semiaxes would directly generalize Theorem
3.24
and thus Theorem
3.9
to the same class of semiaxes.

A possible approach to a lower bound may be to bound the supremum from below by the average of
\sigma_{s}(x)_{q}
over
x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
. Then one can use an expression of a uniform vector in
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
in terms of asymptotically independent vectors, see
[
JP22
]
, and use results from Gordon, Litvak, Schütt and Werner
[
GLSW06
]
on
k
-th minima (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(m-k)}
-th maxima) of independent random variables.

4
\cdot
Function recovery and quantization

Passing from linear to standard information, this chapter is concerned with the quality of random samples compared to optimal samples. We present the results obtained in
[
KS20
]
together with Krieg for the
L_{q}
-approximation problem and the integration problem for Sobolev but also Hölder and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. Compared to the original presentation, the proof technique employed here is slightly different, however, and allows for an extension to integration in Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

Subsequently, we will motivate the study of random standard information, in particular for the important example of Sobolev spaces. Afterwards, in Section
4.2
we will present the obtained results for Sobolev spaces and extensions to other spaces. In particular, we will characterize the radius of information given by a point set in terms of a geometric quantity
. This allows
us to compare the expected radius of random information to the minimal radius of information
and consequently to determine
the power of random information in terms of the given parameters.

Then we will give some details on the proof strategy in Section
4.3
, where we discuss an asymptotically optimal construction for any point set, Section
4.4
, where a lower bound and the relation to the integration problem is established, and Section
4.5
, where the geometric quantity will be discussed. Finally, in Section
4.6
we collect remaining open questions.

4.1
Introduction and motivation

Given a function
f
belonging to some function space
F
defined on a set
D
we can use standard information, that is, function evaluations
f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})
at some point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset D
, in order to solve a numerical problem. We are interested in comparing the radius of random points with the sampling numbers in order to give possible answers to Question
2.3
. We refer to Section
2.3
for a background.

As function evaluations are in particular linear measurements, linear information can lead to better algorithms than relying on standard information alone. In general, however, evaluations are more easily obtained than, say, Fourier coefficients and thus there is an interest in algorithms using standard information. Further, as we will briefly discuss in the following, recent research shows that for an important type of problem function evaluations can be almost as good as optimal linear information.

In the case of
L_{2}
-approximation in a function space
F
, it has been obtained by Krieg and M. Ullrich for Hilbert spaces
[
KU21a
]
, and for more general spaces in
[
KU21b
]
, that a least squares algorithm using random points is up to a logarithmic factor as good as the best linear algorithm. There, the sampling density depends on
F
and is allowed to be uniform in some cases. Together with an improvement by M. Ullrich
[
Ull20
]
this shows that typical information (with respect to this density) is close to optimal. There was an improvement by Nagel, Schäfer and T. Ullrich
[
NSU22
]
based on progress in the related field of sampling discretization due to Nitzan, Olevskii and Ulanovskii
[
NOU16
]
in turn building on the solution of the Kadison-Singer conjecture by Marcus, Spielman and Srivastava
[
MSS15
]
.
Recently, Dolbeault, Krieg and M. Ullrich
[
DKU22
]
provided sharp upper bounds.
However, in general, the logarithmic gap between the
power of standard and linear information cannot be dispensed with
as an example of Sobolev spaces with low smoothness given by Hinrichs, Krieg, Novak and Vybíral
[
HKNV21
]
shows.

It seems difficult to improve the current results on the radius of typical information for general function spaces. We are positive that the methods and results from
[
KS20
]
, which we discuss in this chapter, carry over to other settings. Further, we hope to shed light on possible conditions for asymptotic optimality of (sequences of) point sets in more general function spaces, and on the power of random standard information for those.

A starting point for our study was an open question by Hinrichs, Krieg, Novak, Prochno and M. Ullrich
[
HKN+21
]
, who obtained for the
L_{q}
-approximation problem for Sobolev spaces
W^{1}_{p}([0,1])
that uniform random points are asymptotically on average optimal if
q<p
and worse by a logarithmic factor if
q\geq p
. They made use of known asymptotics for averages of spacings between uniformly distributed points on an interval, which is related to the norm of a random point in a simplex as studied by Baci, Kabluchko, Prochno, Thäle and the author in
[
BKP+22
]
. In
[
KS20
]
we extended the work
[
HKN+21
]
to bounded convex sets
in arbitrary dimension
.

Another motivation was to improve a related suboptimal bound on the radius of random information for the approximation of Sobolev functions on manifolds obtained by Ehler, Gräf and Oates
[
EGO19
]
using known asymptotics for the radius of the largest hole amidst a set of uniformly distributed independent random points. Together with D. Krieg we could close the logarithmic gap from
[
EGO19
]
in almost all cases in the work
[
KS21
]
, where we transferred the results from
[
KS20
]
via charts to manifolds.

In general, one reason to study Sobolev spaces is their ubiquity in the theory of function spaces, which is in part due to their fundamental role as solution spaces of partial differential equations (PDEs). See for example Evans
[
Eva10
]
for a gentle introduction and Maz’ja
[
Maz85
]
for a comprehensive account. Let us give their definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a domain, that is an open and non-empty set. For a smoothness parameter
s\in\mathbb{N}
and an integrability parameter
1\leq p\leq\infty
we consider the Sobolev space

W^{s}_{p}(\Omega):=\Big\{f\in L_{p}(\Omega)\colon\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}:=\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq s}\|D^{\alpha}f\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}<\infty\Big\},

where the sum is over all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}\leq s
and the expression
D^{\alpha}f=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}}f
denotes a weak partial derivative of order
|\alpha|
.

By definition, the Sobolev space
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
is a subspace of
L_{p}(\Omega)
in which two elements are identified if they differ only on a set of measure zero. Without any additional assumptions, function evaluation is therefore not well defined. The embedding
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\Omega)
into the bounded continuous functions eliminates this concern. It holds if

s>d/p\quad\text{if }1<p\leq\infty\quad\text{or}\quad s\geq d\quad\text{if }p=1,

(4.1)

under additional assumptions on
\Omega
which will be satisfied for the domains we consider, see, e.g.,
[
Maz85
, Sec. 1.4.5]
. In fact, we shall need the slightly stronger condition of
s>d/p
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
, which we assume from now on. In this case we have a compact embedding
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\Omega)
, see, e.g.,
[
Maz85
, Sec. 1.4.6]
.

Several
s
-numbers of embeddings between Sobolev spaces have been studied, see, e.g., Pinkus
[
Pin85
, Ch. VII]
for an overview. There, and also for example in the book by Lorentz, von Golitschek and Makovoz
[
LvGM96
, Ch. 14]
, discretization techniques allow to use bounds from embeddings between sequence spaces. This is connected to the study of diagonal operators as mentioned in the introduction to Chapter
3
.

Let us describe the situation for sampling numbers or equivalently the minimal radius of standard information. Recall that the
n
-th minimal radius of information is given in the case of
L_{q}
-approximation by

r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n\big)=\inf_{S_{P_{n}}}\sup_{\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)},

where the infimum is over all sampling operators of the form

S_{P_{n}}\colon W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\to L_{q}(\Omega),\qquad S_{P_{n}}(f)=\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})\big)

(4.2)

for some
n
-point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset\Omega
and
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to L_{q}(\Omega)
. Here, we dropped the
\Lambda^{\rm std}
in the notation as we will only be concerned with standard information from now on.

On a bounded Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Definition
B.2
below), the minimal radius is known to satisfy

r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n\big)\,\asymp\,n^{-s/d+(1/p-1/q)_{+}},

(4.3)

where
(1/q-1/p)_{+}=1/q-1/p
if
q<p
and zero otherwise, see the discussion below. Note that the rate of decay improves if the smoothness
s
is large compared to the dimension
d
but deteriorates otherwise. This is in contrast to anisotropic function spaces of mixed smoothness where the
dependence on the dimension is through a logarithmic factor
. We refer to Temlyakov
[
Tem18
, Ch. 6.9]
for more information and note that for an important class of function spaces of mixed smoothness the
asymptotics of the sampling numbers in
L_{2}
are resolved by
[
DKU22
]
.

For the integration problem, which corresponds to
q=1
above, it is known that

r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega),{\rm INT},n\big)\,\asymp\,n^{-s/d}.

Recall that, in analogy to the above, the
n
-th minimal radius of information is given by

r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega),{\rm INT},n\big)=\inf_{S_{P_{n}}}\sup_{\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}f(x){\rm d}x-S_{P_{n}}(f)\Big|,

where the infimum is over all sampling operators
as in
(
4.2
) with
L_{q}(\Omega)
replaced by
\mathbb{R}
.

In fact, it is known that for integration as well as
L_{q}
-approximation the sampling operators achieving the optimal asymptotic rate can be chosen linear, i.e., of the form

A_{P_{n}}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}f(x_{i}),

(4.4)

where
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
is a suitably chosen point set and
u_{i}
are real numbers in the case of the integration problem and bounded real-valued functions on
\Omega
in the case of the approximation problem. In general, the optimal algorithm for the integration
problem
given any standard information is linear. This is a well-known result due to Smolyak and Bakhvalov, see, e.g.,
[
NW08
, Thm. 4.7]
.

The above asymptotics for the minimal radius are classical for special domains like the cube and we do not know their origin. They have been obtained, for example, in the context of finite elements, which are used for the numerical approximation of solutions of PDEs, see, e.g., Heinrich
[
Hei94
]
who refers to the treatise of Ciarlet
[
Cia78
]
.

Using the moving least squares method, Wendland
[
Wen01
]
proved a result on the local reproduction of polynomials, see Section
4.3
. This was then used by Novak and Triebel in
[
NT06
]
to prove the asymptotic behaviour of the radius of minimal information for approximation (
4.3
) for bounded Lipschitz domains. For sufficiently high smoothness, this may also be deduced from an earlier result due to Narcowich, Ward and Wendland
[
NWW04
]
also relying on
[
Wen01
]
. At this point, it seems worthwhile noting that in
[
NT06
]
the authors also compared the minimal radius of standard information to the one of linear information and concluded that, if one restricts to linear methods, linear information can be asymptotically better than standard information if and only if
p<2<q
.

As standard information is given by point sets, it is perhaps natural to look at their geometry to find (asymptotically) optimal point sets. In the works above it has been found that (sequences of) point sets covering the domain well, so that no point is far from the point set, are asymptotically optimal. That is, it was supposed that the covering radius

h_{P_{n},\Omega}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})

(4.5)

which is the supremum of the distance function

\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty),\qquad\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n}):=\min_{y\in P_{n}}\|x-y\|_{2}

(4.6)

to the
n
-point sampling set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
has to be of the best possible order
n^{-1/d}
, see also (
4.7
) below. This assumption of an optimal covering radius is very common in numerical analysis; to give an impression, we refer to Arcangéli, López de Silanes and Torrens
[
ALdST07
]
, Brauchart, Dick, Saff, Sloan, Wang and Womersley
[
BDS+15
]
, Duchon
[
Duc78
]
, Mhaskar
[
Mha10
]
, as well as Wendland and Schaback
[
WS93
]
, while being well aware of the
incompleteness of this enumeration
. In the following section, we will see that the covering radius is not sufficient to describe which (sequences of) point sets achieve the optimal rate. This allows for an asymptotic description of the expected power of random standard information.

4.2
Discussion of results

Motivated by the question whether random points or typical information is optimal, we derived a geometric characterization for the radius of information given by a point set, Theorem
4.2
below. This yields a criterion (Corollary
4.3
) of optimality given a sequence of point sets. Using results from quantization theory we show Proposition
4.5
and derive Corollary
4.6
which settles Question
2.3
on the power of random information for the
L_{q}
-approximation and the integration problem above. Finally, Theorem
4.10
extends the results to other spaces, including Hölder classes, Sobolev spaces of fractional smoothness and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

In order to investigate the power of random standard information, we analyzed the quality of arbitrary point sets and arrived at the following theorem.

Theorem 4.2

(
[
KS20
, Thm. 1]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex domain,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
. Then, for any point set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
,

\displaystyle r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),P_{n}\big)\,\asymp\,\begin{cases}\big\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\big\|_{L_{\infty}(\Omega)}^{s-d(1/p-1/q)}&\text{if }q\geq p,\\
\big\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\big\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}^{s}&\text{if }q<p,\end{cases}

(
a
)

\displaystyle r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega),{\rm INT},P_{n}\big)\,\asymp\,r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{1}(\Omega),P_{n}\big),

(
b
)

where
\gamma=s(1/q-1/p)^{-1}
and
\asymp
is to be interpreted as in Definition
2.13
. The algorithm achieving the upper bounds can be chosen linear, that is, of the form (
4.4
).

The proof and the assumption of convexity will be discussed in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
.

Theorem
4.2
implies for a sequence of point sets
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, each consisting of
n
points, that the radius of information of
P_{n}
is asymptotically determined by the radius of the largest hole amidst the points if
q\geq p
and by an average of the distance to the point set if
q<p
. The case
q\geq p
, where the covering radius determines the radius of information is included for completeness and can be deduced from the results of
[
NWW04
]
and
[
NT06
]
mentioned before. Our contribution was to show that the covering radius is too large to capture the radius of information for
q<p
, and in particular, for numerical integration. In general, the assumption of a small covering radius is unnecessarily strong as we shall see in Corollary
4.3
below.

Theorem
4.2
is a tool to analyze the asymptotic optimality of arbitrary (sequences of) point sets and, in particular, random or typical ones. Before we will come to the quality of random points, let us comment on the optimal behaviour of the minimal
L_{\gamma}
-norm of the distance function to any point set with at most
n
points. It is well known that this satisfies

\inf_{\#P_{n}\leq n}\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,\asymp\,n^{-1/d}\qquad\text{for every}\quad 0<\gamma\leq\infty,

(4.7)

where the infimum is taken over all point sets
P_{n}\subset\Omega
with at most
n
points. This can be deduced from a standard volume argument using balls centered at the points. For completeness a proof is provided in Appendix
B.1
.

Thus, asymptotically optimal points, such as for example a suitably scaled lattice (see Chapter
6
), can be said to have holes of order
n^{-1/d}
. Plugging this into Theorem
4.2
, one can see that the rates for the minimal radius described in the introduction are achieved by such point sets. This
yields
the following characterization of (asymptotically) optimal point sets.

Corollary 4.3

(
[
KS20
, Cor. 1]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex domain,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
. Assume that for each (or at least infinitely many)
n\in\mathbb{N}
an
n
-point set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
is given. These point sets are asymptotically optimal, i.e.,

r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n\big)\,\asymp\,r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),P_{n}\big),

if and only if

\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\,\asymp\,n^{-1/d},

where
\gamma=s(1/q-1/p)^{-1}
for
q<p
and
\gamma=\infty
for
q\geq p
.

By Theorem
4.2
b, we clearly get the same characterization for numerical integration as for the problem of
L_{1}
-approximation. The relation between these two problems will be commented on later in Section
4.4
.

Our results for
q<p
seem to be novel already for
d=1
, where recent results were obtained in
[
HKN+21
]
. To the best of our knowledge, similar results only have been known for the spaces
W_{\infty}^{s}(\Omega)
with
s\leq 2
, see Sukharev
[
Suh79
]
and Pagès
[
Pag98
]
. The latter author uses concepts from the theory of quantization of measures which will be discussed later on.

To illustrate the advantage of the characterization of optimal points in Corollary
4.3
compared to conditions involving the covering radius, consider the following example depicted in Figure
4.1
.

Example 4.4

(
[
KS20
, Sec. 2.4]
)
.

Take for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
an
n
-point set
P_{n}
on a bounded convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With regard to asymptotic behaviour,
we ask:

How large can the largest hole admist
P_{n}
be for this to be still optimal as a sampling set for
L_{q}
-approximation of
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
-functions?

By Corollary
4.3
the sequence of point sets
(P_{n})
can be (but not necessarily is) asymptotically optimal for this problem if and only if

h_{P_{n},\Omega}\lesssim n^{-1/d+1/(\gamma+d)}.

(4.8)

This means that the radius of the largest hole is allowed to exceed the optimal covering radius
n^{-1/d}
by the polynomial factor
n^{1/(\gamma+d)}
. A proof is provided in Appendix
B.1
. We also refer to
[
BDS+15
, Thm. 1.2]
, where the necessity of condition (
4.8
) (for
q=1
) has been observed for numerical integration on the sphere.

Figure 4.1
:
An otherwise well-spaced point set containing a single large hole
B_{n}
whose radius may be larger than the other holes by a polynomial factor without destroying the asymptotic optimality. Courtesy of D. Krieg.

Now that we have looked at optimal points, let us come to random points which have almost optimal covering properties as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
KS20
, Prop. 1]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be independent and uniformly distributed on a bounded convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consider the random
n
-point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
. Then, for any
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}\,\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}^{\alpha}\,\asymp\,\begin{cases}n^{-\alpha/d}&\text{if }0<\gamma<\infty,\\
n^{-\alpha/d}(\log n)^{\alpha/d}&\text{if }\gamma=\infty.\end{cases}

Essentially, the proposition claims that the average hole size of random points is on average of optimal order
n^{-1/d}
, whereas the largest hole is logarithmically larger compared to optimal points. Its proof will be discussed in Section
4.5
.

Together with Theorem
4.2
we obtain the following corollary on the power of random information for
L_{q}
-approximation and integration in Sobolev spaces.

Corollary 4.6

(
[
KS20
, Cor. 2]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be independent and uniformly distributed on a bounded convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
. Consider the random
n
-point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\,r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}\big)\,\asymp\,\begin{cases}r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n/\log n\big)&\text{if }q\geq p,\\
r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n\big)&\text{if }q<p.\end{cases}

Summarizing, we get that, on average,
n
random points are as asympotically as good as
n/\log n
optimal ones if
q\geq p
and asymptotically optimal if
q<p
. This generalizes the one-dimensional result in
[
HKN+20
, Thm. 4.1]
(
s
has to be replaced by one there) and gives an answer to Question
2.3
for the integration and the
L_{q}
-approximation problem.

Before discussing the extension to other function spaces, let us note a few things.

Remark 4.7
.

We stated Corollary
4.6
for the expected value of the radius of random information but
in fact it
also holds with arbitrarily high probability by adjusting the constants and using Markov’s inequality giving that, for
q<p
,

\mathbb{P}\big[r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}\big)>C\,\varepsilon^{-1}\,r\big(W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),n\big)\big]\leq\varepsilon,

where
C>0
is some constant independent of
n
and
\varepsilon>0
can be made arbitrarily small.

Remark 4.8
.

Proposition
4.5
is a slightly modified version of Proposition 1 in
[
KS20
]
. There, we overlooked that estimates on arbitrary large moments are necessary for deducing Corollary
4.6
. Thus, the proof of
[
KS20
, Cor. 2]
is flawed and it is necessary to carry out some minor amendments. The details will be explained at the end of Section
4.5
.

Remark 4.9
.

Instead of the worst-case error criterion one can consider a randomized error criterion for a general random algorithm, where one first takes the expected error on an individual function and then the supremum over all functions in the unit ball. Together with Krieg and Novak we studied in
[
KNS22
]
the power of random information in the randomized setting by adapting the techniques from Section
4.3
below. See also the references cited there for further information.

In the remainder of this section we will present generalizations of the above results to other classes of sets
\Omega
and also to other function spaces on
\Omega
including Sobolev-Slobodeckij spaces of fractional smoothness, Triebel-Lizorkin spaces, Hölder-Zygmund spaces and Bessel potential spaces.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex domain. Several of the function spaces mentioned are part of the scale of Triebel-Lizorkin spaces
F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
, where
0<p<\infty
,
0<\tau\leq\infty
and
s>d/p
, which ensures
F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\Omega)
.
As for
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
the condition
s>d/p
can be weakened for
p\leq 1
but for simplicity we will always assume that
s>d/p
holds for the
F
-spaces we consider.
These are closely related to Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
for which also
\tau=\infty
is possible and we provide a more information on both scales in Appendix
B.5
. Here, we simply note that
F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
covers a variety of interesting spaces:

•

For
\tau=2
and
1<p<\infty
, we obtain the fractional Sobolev space (or Bessel potential space)
H^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
, see e.g.
[
NT06
]
. If additionally
s\in\mathbb{N}
, we arrive at the classical Sobolev spaces
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
from Definition
4.1
.

•

For
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq p<\infty
and
\tau=p
, we obtain the Sobolev-Slobodeckij space
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
of fractional smoothness, see e.g. Dupont and Scott
[
DS93
]
, and note that
F^{s}_{pp}(\Omega)
is equal to the Besov space
B^{s}_{pp}(\Omega)
.

We also want to discuss the Hölder spaces
C^{s}(\Omega)
, which are not included in this scale. For
s\in\mathbb{N}
, the Hölder space
C^{s}(\Omega)
is the space of all
s
times continuously differentiable functions with

\|f\|_{C^{s}(\Omega)}:=\max_{|\alpha|\leq s}\,\sup_{x\in\Omega}\,|D^{\alpha}f(x)|<\infty,

where
\alpha
is a multi-index as in Definition
4.1
. For
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
, the space
C^{s}(\Omega)
is defined to be the space of functions
f\in C^{\lfloor s\rfloor}(\Omega)
with

|f|_{C^{s}(\Omega)}:=\max_{|\alpha|=\lfloor s\rfloor}\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Omega\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\,\frac{|D^{\alpha}f(x)-D^{\alpha}f(y)|}{\|x-y\|^{\{s\}}}<\infty,

(4.9)

where
s=\lfloor s\rfloor+\{s\}
with
0<\{s\}<1
and
C^{0}(\Omega)=C(\Omega)
. It is equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{C^{\lfloor s\rfloor}(\Omega)}+|\cdot|_{C^{s}(\Omega)}
.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
KS20
, Thm. 2]
)
.

Theorem
4.2
, Corollary
4.3
and Corollary
4.6
remain valid for arbitrary real parameters
0<p,q,\tau\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
s>d/p
if we replace
W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)
either by
C^{s}(\Omega)
for
p=\infty
or by
F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
for
p<\infty
.

We defer the proof to Appendix
B.5
. In particular, Theorem
4.10
implies that the fine index
\tau
, measuring smoothness on a finer scale, does not influence the asymptotic quality of point sets.

In order to discuss general domains, we define the function spaces on a general bounded measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by restriction of the corresponding function spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let

A(\Omega):=\{f|_{\Omega}\colon f\in A(\mathbb{R}^{d})\},\qquad\|g\|_{A(\Omega)}:=\inf_{f\in A(\mathbb{R}^{d})\colon f|_{\Omega}=g}\|f\|_{A(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(4.10)

for
A\in\{W_{p}^{s},F^{s}_{p\tau},C^{s}\}
with parameters as above. If there is a bounded extension operator
{\rm ext}\colon A(\Omega)\to A(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\rm ext}(f|_{\Omega})}=f
these spaces coincide with the (intrinsically) defined ones above. Here and everywhere else in this thesis, equality between function spaces is meant up to equivalence of norms and the equivalence constants vanish in the asymptotic notation.

Remark 4.11
.

It can be deduced from the proofs below that Theorem
4.2
, Corollary
4.3
and Corollary
4.6
and also Theorem
4.10
remain valid in the case

•

q\geq p
if
\Omega
satisfies an interior cone condition, see Definition
4.15
in the following section, and if there is a bounded extension operator. This includes all bounded Lipschitz domains. For more information see Appendix
B.2
for Sobolev spaces as well as Appendix
B.5
for the other function spaces.

•

q<p
if
\Omega
is a bounded convex set with non-empty interior.

Remark 4.12
.

In our paper
[
KS20
]
Theorem
4.10
did not apply to Theorem
4.2
b, the equivalence of the integration to the
L_{1}
-approximation problem. Here, we use a novel approach not published yet in order to remedy this. The idea is to use a different way to construct fooling functions and estimate their norm. We want to thank M. Ullrich for pointing us to the concept of atomic decompositions which we, together with my coauthor D. Krieg, employed to prove this extension. See also the discussion in Section
4.4
.

Remark 4.13
.

As mentioned, our paper
[
KS21
]
relies on the work of
[
KS20
]
discussed here. There, we showed an analogon of Theorem
4.2
for Bessel potential spaces on the sphere. In particular, the lower bounds in
[
KS21
, Thm. 2]
(see also Remark 2 there) partially rely on the lower bound discussed in the previous Remark
4.12
, which we provide in this thesis.

4.3
Local moving least squares on good cubes

In the following, we will discuss the strategy behind the proof of the upper bound of the characterization given in Theorem
4.2
a (and its extension, Theorem
4.10
). The goal is to find, for each point set
P_{n}
in the domain
\Omega
, a reconstruction map
\varphi
using information given by the function values at
P_{n}
such that the corresponding algorithm has a worst-case error over the unit ball of the Sobolev space
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
which is bounded by a constant times the
L_{\gamma}(\Omega)
-norm of the distance function to
P_{n}
.

For finding a suitable algorithm we use the technique of moving least squares which enables to reconstruct polynomials exactly and then use the fact that Sobolev functions can be well approximated by polynomials via Taylor’s theorem. Let us give some details.

Building on earlier work, moving least squares approximation was developed by Lancaster and Salkauskas
[
LS81
]
in 1981 and in recent decades attracted attention due to the increase in computing power, see Wendland
[
Wen05
, Sec. 4.4]
for additional references. It is a special case of weighted least squares approximation, a variant of the usual least squares method, working as follows.

Given a point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and a function
f
belonging to a space
F
, one tries to find a function
g_{m}
in a fixed
m
-dimensional subspace
X_{m}
of
F
which minimizes the weighted sum of squares

\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f(x_{i})-g(x_{i})|^{2}\,w(x,x_{i}),\quad w\geq 0,

for the point
x
and then returns
g_{m}(x)
as an approximation to
f(x)
. This procedure reproduces elements from
X_{m}
exactly provided they are uniquely defined by the function values at
P_{n}
. In general, increasing the number of points
n
allows to increase the dimension
m
and thus for a better approximation. On a side note, weighted least squares approximation is the backbone of the results
in
[
DKU22
,
KU21b
,
KU21a
]
described in the introduction to this chapter and effective for recovery, see for example Cohen and Migliorati
[
CM17
]
.

In the case of moving least squares on a
d
-dimensional domain, the finite-dimensional space is the space of polynomials

\mathcal{P}_{m}^{d}:=\Big\{\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\colon c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\Big\}

of degree at most
m\in\mathbb{N}
in
d
variables, where
x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
is the monomial associated to the multi-index
\alpha
. Further, the weight function
w
is a bump function depending on
(x-x_{i})/\delta
, where
\delta>0
is a suitably chosen scaling parameter. If the point set
P_{n}
is locally dense enough, then polynomials can be reconstructed exactly using moving least squares. This will be made precise by the following lemma which follows directly from results by Wendland
[
Wen01
]
(see also
[
Wen05
, Thm. 4.7]
in his book).

To measure the local density of a point set
P_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
on a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use the covering radius as given in (
4.5
), which measures the radius of the largest hole of the point set
P_{n}
in
\Omega
. In the following, a
hole
in a point set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
will be a ball contained in
\Omega
empty of
P_{n}
, i.e., not containing a point of
P_{n}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact set satisfying an interior cone condition with parameters
r
and
\theta
, and let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. There are constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending solely on
\theta,m
and
d
such that for any
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset K
with covering radius
h_{P_{n},K}\leq c_{1}r
there exist continuous functions
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\colon K\to\mathbb{R}
with

(i)

\displaystyle\pi(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(x_{i})u_{i}(y)
for all
y\in K

and
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{d}
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}(y)|\leq c_{0}
for all
y\in K
and

(iii)

u_{i}(y)=0
for all
i\leq n
and
y\in K
with
\|x_{i}-y\|\geq c_{2}h_{P_{n},K}
.

Before we elaborate on the interior cone condition, let us give some background on how this lemma follows from
[
Wen05
, Thm. 4.7]
. There, it is required that the point set
P_{n}
(or rather an underlying sequence of
n
-point sets) is quasi-uniform, that is there exists a constant
c_{\rm qu}>0
with

h_{P_{n},K}\leq q_{P_{n}}\leq c_{qu}h_{P_{n},K},

(4.11)

where
q_{P_{n}}:=\frac{1}{2}\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}
is the separation distance of
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. In Appendix
B.3
we will deduce Lemma
4.14

by throwing away points too close to others in order to arrive at a quasi-uniform point set without affecting the local density by much.

Definition 4.15
.

We say that a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies an interior cone condition with radius
r>0
and angle
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
if, for all
x\in\Omega
, there is a direction
\xi(x)\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that the cone

C(x,\xi(x),r,\theta):=\left\{x+\lambda y\colon y\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\langle y,\xi(x)\rangle\geq\cos\theta,\lambda\in[0,r]\right\}

with apex
x
is contained in
\Omega
. See Figure
4.2
for an illustration.

\Omega
r
\xi(x)
x
\theta
r
\xi(x)
x
\theta

Figure 4.2
:
For each
x\in\Omega
the cone
C(x,\xi(x),r,\theta)
may point in a different direction but is of the same shape.

The interior cone condition above is related to similar regularity conditions, in part proposed to characterize the class of domains for which certain (Sobolev) function space embedding and extension theorems hold, see for example the books by Adams and Fournier
[
AF03
]
and Grisvard
[
Gri85
]
. Let us mention here that bounded convex domains satisfy an interior cone condition as above. The following lemma is a consequence of
[
Wen05
, Prop. 11.26]
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Any bounded convex set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
containing a closed ball of radius
r
satisfies an interior cone condition with radius
r
and angle
\theta=2\arcsin\bigl(r/2\,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\bigr)
.

Here and in the following, balls and cubes are always assumed full-dimensional. For applying Lemma
4.14
to a non-compact set, and in particular a bounded convex domain, we shall need the following lemma, proven in Appendix
B.3
.

Lemma 4.17
.

If a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies an interior cone condition, then its closure
\overline{\Omega}
satisfies an interior cone condition with the same parameters
r
and
\theta
.

Let us continue
by
explaining the ideas behind the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
a. The strategy is different depending on whether
q\geq p
, where we basically extend the proof of
[
NT06
, Prop. 21]
to point sets with non-optimal covering radius and employ Lemma
4.14
on
\overline{\Omega}
, or
q<p
, where it will be applied to small parts of the domain, which themselves satisfy an interior cone condition. This case distinction is also reflected by the structure of Appendix
B
, where Sections
B.3
and
B.4
contain the proofs needed for the case
q\geq p
and
q<p
, respectively. The proof for the already known case
q\geq p
is meant as a preparation for the novel approach developed in
[
KS20
]
for the case
q<p
on the basis of the mentioned works
[
NT06
,
NWW04
,
Wen01
]
. In the following, we will explain the ideas behind starting with a brief description of the simpler case.

In the case
q\geq p
, we apply Lemma
4.14
to the closure of the bounded convex domain
\Omega
, which satisfies an interior cone condition by Lemma
4.16
and Lemma
4.17
. To bound the approximation error of the resulting algorithm
f\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})u_{i}
, we cover
\Omega
by parts of diameter
h_{P_{n},\Omega}
and use error estimates on the local approximation of Sobolev functions by polynomials. For more details see Section
B.3
.

In the case
p>q
, we cannot achieve the desired bound by applying Lemma
4.14
to the whole domain. Instead, we have to suitably cover it by pieces adapted to the point set
P_{n}
which satisfy an interior cone condition in such a way such that the constants involved in the error bounds do not depend on the point set. Our approach requires that the radius of the interior cone condition satisfied by any piece of the cover should be proportional to the diameter of the piece. This is where the convexity of the domain comes into play. If the cover is constructed with cubes as it is the case in our work
[
KS20
]
, the following lemma supplies the required interior cone condition. To dispose of the convexity assumption, a different approach seems to be required as Figure
4.3
shows, see Question
4.3
below.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex set containing a closed ball of radius
r>0
. Then there exist constants
c_{\theta}>0
and
\theta^{\prime}\in(0,\pi/2)
depending on
\Omega
and
r
such that closure of the intersection of
\Omega
with any cube
Q(x,\varrho)
of radius
0<\varrho\leq r
centered at some
x\in\Omega
satisfies an interior cone condition with radius
c_{\theta}\varrho
and angle
\theta^{\prime}
.

Note that the exact choice of
r>0
does not matter as this lemma is applied to very small cubes if the points become more and more dense. We use the notation

Q(x,\varrho):=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\|x-y\|_{\infty}<\varrho\}\quad\text{and}\quad B(x,\varrho):=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\|x-y\|_{2}<\varrho\}

(4.12)

for an open cube or ball centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and of radius
\varrho>0
.

x
\Omega

Figure 4.3
:
The depicted bounded domain
\Omega
is not convex and its intersection with the small cube has two connected components. By translating the cube to the left, one of the components becomes arbitrarily small and therefore cannot contain a single cone which has radius proportional to the cube. This can happen for an arbitrarily small cube and shows that Lemma
4.18
does not apply to
\Omega
. This remains unchanged by suitably rounding the corners of
\Omega
such that its boundary is smooth.

One reason why we choose cubes in order to construct the covering is that they form a family which is invariant under translations and rescalings. This is needed to ensure the independence of the constants in the Sobolev embedding theorem, which is used for the estimates on the local error of polynomial approximation by Sobolev functions, see Lemma
B.1
. However, other
set systems such as balls
may work as well, see Remark
4.23
below.

Combined with Lemma
4.14
, the local cone condition from Lemma
4.18
yields the following local approximation result, which will be applied for each cube in a suitable cover of the domain.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex set containing a ball of radius
r>0
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
. There are constants
c_{\rm good}\in(0,1)
and
C>0
, such that for any cube
Q(x,\varrho)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of radius
0<\varrho\leq r
centered at
x\in\Omega
and any point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\Omega
with

\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)}\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})\leq c_{\rm good}\,\varrho,

(4.13)

there are bounded continuous functions
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\colon\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)}\Big|f(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})u_{i}(y)\Big|\leq C\,\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q(x,\varrho))}.

The local upper bound provided by this lemma is in terms of a Sobolev seminorm
|\cdot|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q(x,\varrho))}
which is essentially the sum in the definition of the norm (Definition
4.1
) restricted to
|\alpha|=s
, see also (
B.2
).

Lemma
4.19
applies if condition (
4.13
) is satisfied, that is, if the covering radius
h_{P_{n},\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)}
with respect to the point set
P_{n}
and the domain
\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)
is small compared to the radius
\varrho
of the cube. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.20
.

We say that
Q(x,\varrho)
with
x\in\Omega
and
0<\varrho\leq r
is a good cube if it satisfies condition (
4.13
).

The smaller the radius of a good cube, the better the bound provided by Lemma
4.19

if

s-d/p>0
. Therefore, we would like to work for every
x\in\Omega
with a good cube
Q_{P_{n}}(x)
of smallest radius possible as given by the following definition for which we provide some justification below.

Definition 4.21
.

Define for each
P_{n}\subset\Omega
and
x\in\Omega
the radius
r_{P_{n}}(x)
as the infimum over all
\varrho\in(0,r)
such that
Q=Q(x,\varrho)
satisfies (
4.13
) and let
Q_{P_{n}}(x):=Q\big(x,r_{P_{n}}(x)\big)
.

The radius
r_{P_{n}}(x)
is well-defined since we may assume that, without loss of generality, for any
P_{n}\subset\Omega
and
x\in\Omega
there is some
\varrho\in(0,r)
such that (
4.13
) holds for the cube
Q(x,\varrho)
. Otherwise, we have
\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\geq c
for some
c>0
, see Lemma
B.7
in Appendix
B.4
. Then the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
a would become trivial.

The infimum in Definition
4.21
is in fact attained, see Lemma
B.8
in Appendix
B.4
. Consequently, also
r_{P_{n}}(x)
satisfies (
4.13
) and
Q_{P_{n}}(x)
is a good cube, the smallest of all good cubes centered at
x
. Note that we could also have chosen
r_{P_{n}}(x)
to be the minimizer up to a constant factor and the proof still works.

Subsequently, we consider only the collection
\{Q_{P_{n}}(x)\colon x\in\Omega\}
of good cubes of minimal radius and we will cover the domain by a suitable finite subset of them. The following proposition yields a suitable cover. It follows from a Besicovitch-type covering theorem for cubes as presented by de Guzman
[
dG75
, Thm. 1.1]
.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be bounded. There exist
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
depending only on
d
such that for any point set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
there are centers
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\in\Omega
such that the cubes
Q_{i}:=Q_{P_{n}}(y_{i})
, where
i=1,\dots,N
,

1.

form a cover, i.e.,
\Omega\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}Q_{i}
,

2.

can be distributed in at most
M_{1}
families, i.e.,
\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N}\}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{M_{1}}\mathcal{Q}_{j},
with each family
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
consisting of pairwise disjoint cubes,

3.

cover efficiently, i.e., no point of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in more than
M_{2}
of them.

To complete the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
, apply Lemma
4.19
to each of the cubes
Q_{i}
given by Proposition
4.22
to get, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
, a linear algorithm
S_{P_{n},i}\colon W^{s}_{p}(\Omega\cap Q_{i})\to L_{q}(\Omega\cap Q_{i})
. Then select for each point
x\in\Omega
a cube
Q_{i}
containing
x
and let
S_{P_{n}}(f)(x):=S_{P_{n},i}(f)(x)
. In this way, we form a global linear sampling algorithm
f\mapsto S_{P_{n}}(f)=\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)\in L_{q}(\Omega)
, defined on
\Omega
. In fact, since the components
S_{P_{n},i}(f)
are bounded, the algorithm returns a bounded function. It remains to combine the estimates given by Lemma
4.19
with Hölder’s inequality and the efficiency of the covering. A detailed proof will be provided in Appendix
B.4
. We have not used the second property of the covering in Proposition
4.22
; it will be needed for the lower bound described in the next section.

Remark 4.23
.

Note that Proposition
4.22
also works if the cubes are replaced by the familiy of all translations and rescalings of a fixed bounded set satisfying an interior cone condition, see
[
dG75
, Sec. I.1, Rem. (4)]
where this is attributed to A. P. Morse. It should be possible to replace everywhere in our proofs the cubes by such a family.

Remark 4.24
.

Concerning Proposition
4.22
, we proved in
[
KS20
]
the existence of a similar cover directly, see Lemma 11 and 12 as well as Proposition 2 there. During a seminar talk on our work by David Krieg in Linz, Simon Hackl suggested that the required cover may be obtained using
[
dG75
, Thm. 1.1]
. Together with D. Krieg, we succeeded in thus simplifying the proof in this way and the author wants
to
thank S. Hackl. This is one reason why the proof of the upper bound in the case
q<p
, which is given here, is slightly different from the one given in
[
KS20
]
.

4.4
Fooling functions and the integration problem

We come now to the lower bound of Theorem
4.2
a which relies on the well-known technique of fooling functions and also discuss the related equivalence between
L_{1}
-approximation and integration stated in Theorem
4.2
b, and its extension Theorem
4.10
.

Given a sampling set
P_{n}
, a
fooling function
forces any algorithm using information provided by
P_{n}
to
produce a large worst-case
error. Exhibiting such a function is a popular
approach
to lower bounds for sampling algorithms. To elaborate a bit, let
\Omega
be some domain and
f_{\ast}\in W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
with norm at most one and
f_{\ast}(x_{i})=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then an algorithm of the form
S_{P_{n}}\colon f\mapsto\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)
cannot distinguish
f_{\ast}
from
-f_{\ast}
, i.e., it satisfies
S_{P_{n}}(f_{*})=S_{P_{n}}(-f_{*})
. Therefore, the radius of information satisfies

\displaystyle r\big(W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L_{q}(\Omega),P_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\,=\,\inf_{S_{P_{n}}}\sup_{\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\|S_{P_{n}}(f)-f\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\geq\,\inf_{u\in L_{q}(\Omega)}\,\max\left\{\|u-f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)},\|u+f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\,\|f_{\ast}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}.

This reproves the lower bound in relation (
2.16
). Any
f_{\ast}
with sufficiently large
\|f_{\ast}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}
can be a fooling function. In particular, it is meaningful to rescale such that
\|f_{\ast}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}=1
. We describe how to construct such a function.

In the case
q\geq p
it is enough to consider a smooth non-negative function
f_{\ast}
which is supported
in a large hole
with radius close to
h_{P_{n},\Omega}
. Scaling properties of the Sobolev and the
L_{q}
-norm can then be used to obtain the required estimates.

Again, the case
q<p
demands a different approach. There, we have to take multiple disjoint holes in which the fooling function is supported. Intuitively, this is because we are interested in an average error. This distinction is well-known in the literature,
see, e.g., the proofs of the lower bounds in
[
BDS+15
]
and in
[
NT06
]
. To obtain the disjoint holes we will use the cover of Proposition
4.22
and select a family of disjoint cubes as in Definition
4.21
with a large sum of radii. The following lemma
then provides
a suitable hole of proportional size in every such cube and makes clear why we did choose good cubes of small radius.

Lemma 4.25
.

There exists a constant
c_{\rm hole}\in(0,\infty)
such that for every point set
P_{n}\subset\Omega
and any
x\in\Omega
the intersection
\Omega\cap Q_{P_{n}}(x)
, contains a ball of radius
c_{\rm hole}\,r_{P_{n}}(x)
.

To obtain the required estimates in the case
q<p
, we need some control over the Sobolev norm of a sum of functions with disjoint supports. Implicitly we used a property of Sobolev spaces, see for
example

[
Hei94
, eq. (9)]
, which states that the Sobolev norm of a sum of a linear combination of functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{M}
with pairwise disjoint support, which are obtained by scaling and translating a fixed function, can be described using an
\ell_{p}
-norm of the coefficients. This property is shared by other function spaces to a certain extent as discussed below.

Before this, we comment on the proof of Theorem
4.2
b which states that for the Sobolev spaces
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
the radius of information for the integration problem is asymptotically equivalent to the one for the
L_{1}
-approximation problem.

In order to obtain a suitable sampling algorithm to approximate the integral, we use the fact that if we have an algorithm
S_{P_{n}}
of the form
S_{P_{n}}\colon f\mapsto\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)
for the problem of
L_{1}
-approximation, then
S_{P_{n}}(f)\in L_{1}(\Omega)
and

\Big|\int_{\Omega}f(x)-\int_{\Omega}S_{P_{n}}(f)(x){\rm d}x\Big|\leq\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{1}(\Omega)}.

Regarding the lower bound, the above approach using fooling functions also applies to the integration problem if
f_{\ast}\geq 0
since then
\|f_{\ast}\|_{L_{1}(\Omega)}={\rm INT}(f_{\ast})
. Thus, Theorem
4.2
b is proven if we can find non-negative fooling functions.

Indeed, we shall use non-negative fooling functions in the proofs in Appendix
B
below. In Section
B.4
there, we employ techniques which will also be suitable for the extension to other function spaces mentioned in Theorem
4.10
. In our original work
[
KS20
]
we extended the lower bound for the
L_{q}
-approximation problem to Triebel-Lizorkin spaces, which do not behave as well as Sobolev spaces with respect
to
sums of functions of disjoint supports of different sizes, by using a wavelet characterization pointed out to us by W. Sickel.

However, the generalization of Theorem
4.2
b dealing with the integration problem did not succeed using the wavelets as these have to satisfy moment conditions which require them to have an integral of zero. Using atomic decompositions instead gives non-negative fooling functions and thus
proves
also the extension of Theorem
4.2
b to the Triebel-Lizorkin spaces, see also Remark
4.12
above. This will be done in the Appendix, Section
B.4
.

At this point, we want to say a few more words on the proof of the generalization to other spaces, that is the proof of Theorem
4.10
. As a crucial ingredient, we need a bound on polynomial approximation in terms of a seminorm that scales well. The work
[
NT06
]
contains the required tools, even for spaces on the more general bounded Lipschitz domains defined below. Further, our method requires that the seminorms on the cubes can be glued together in a suitable manner. In this regard, our attempt at an extension of Theorem
4.2
to Besov spaces failed. We believe that this is solely a shortcoming of our technique and that Theorem
4.10
holds also for Besov spaces, see also Question
4.2
. For a definition of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov spaces and details of the proof see Appendix
B.5
.

4.5
Optimal quantizers and the distortion of random points

In the following, we shall prove Proposition
4.5
on the expected average hole size of random points, which together with Theorem
4.2
, leads to the main result of Corollary
4.6
.

At the heart of the study of the radius of information of an arbitrary, and thus in particular also a typical or random, point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
for the problems considered in this chapter lies the quantity

D_{\mu,P_{n},\gamma}:=\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu)}^{\gamma}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}\mu(x),

(4.14)

where
0<\gamma<\infty
and
\mu
is a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which we assume to be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. In the context of quantization theory, which originated from signal processing, the number
D_{\mu,P_{n},\gamma}
is also called the
distortion
, see the monograph
[
GL00
]
by Graf and Luschgy for more information on this subject. Before, we considered only the Lebesgue measure restricted to a domain
\Omega
but in the following we shall provide some background from a more general perspective.
