Roughly speaking, quantization means replacing a continuous object by a discrete one. In quantization theory one seeks to replace a random vector
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with law
\mu
by a quantized version which assumes only finitely many values. For this, one uses a Borel measurable map
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with an image of cardinality
\#T(\mathbb{R}^{d})=n\in\mathbb{N}
and forms the random variable
T(X)
. Such a map is called an
n
-quantizer. To measure how well the quantized version
T(X)
represents
X
itself, one considers the quantization error
\mathbb{E}\|X-T(X)\|_{2}^{r}
, where
1\leq r<\infty
is such that
\mathbb{E}\|X\|_{2}^{r}<\infty
.

If the image of an
n
-quantizer is some
n
-point set
P_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the optimal quantizer
T_{P_{n}}
maps a realization of
X
to the closest point of
P_{n}
. This means that it is of the form

T_{P_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{C(x_{i},P_{n})},

(4.15)

where

C(x_{i},P_{n}):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\|x-x_{i}\|_{2}=\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})\}

are the Voronoi cells with respect to
P_{n}
. By deciding ties arbitrarily, the collection
\{C(x_{i},P_{n})\colon i=1,\ldots,n\}
becomes a disjoint partition of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A moment of thought reveals that the quantization error of
T_{P_{n}}
is exactly the distortion, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}\|X-T_{P_{n}}(X)\|_{2}^{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{C(x_{i},P_{n})}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{r}{\rm d}\mu(x)=D_{\mu,P_{n},r}.

(4.16)

In quantization theory, one is often interested in the minimal quantization error over all
n
-quantizers, or equivalently in the infimum of the distortion over all
n
-point sets
P_{n}
. In (
4.7
) we gave rough asymptotics for this quantity but in fact exact asymptotics are available. Also for random quantizers precise limit theorems have been obtained, for example, by Cohort
[
Coh04
]
and Yukich
[
Yuk08
]
who build on work of Zador
[
Zad82
]
. We deduce from
[
Coh04
, Thm. 1]
the following statement on random distortion.

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
0<\gamma<\infty
and
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be independent and uniformly distributed on a bounded measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying an interior cone condition. Consider the random
n
-point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
. Then, for every
p=2,3,\ldots
,

n^{\gamma/d}\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\,\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{L_{p}}\,\left(\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)}{\mathrm{vol}\bigl(\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}\bigr)}\right)^{\gamma/d}\Gamma\Bigl(1+\frac{\gamma}{d}\Bigr).

In order to deduce this from
[
Coh04
, Thm. 1]
, it remains to observe that the interior cone condition satisfied by
\Omega
yields a constant
c_{\Omega}>0
such that for every
x\in\Omega
and every
0<\varrho\leq 1+\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|x\|_{2}
we have
\mathrm{vol}\bigl(\Omega\cap B(x,\varrho)\bigr)\geq c_{\Omega}\mathrm{vol}\bigl(B(x,\varrho)\bigr)
.

We are now ready to give the proof of Proposition
4.5
. If
0<\gamma<\infty
, the lower bound is clear from the behaviour of optimal points as mentioned in (
4.7
). For the upper bound, an application of Jensen’s inequality shows that for
p=p(\alpha,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}
chosen large enough such that
p\geq\alpha/\gamma
we have

\Big(\mathbb{E}\,n^{\alpha/d}\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P)\|_{L_{\gamma}}^{\alpha}\Big)^{\gamma/\alpha}\leq\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[n^{\gamma/d}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P)^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\Big]^{p}\Big)^{1/p}.

By Lemma
4.16
the bounded convex domain
\Omega
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
4.26
, and thus the right-hand side converges to some finite limit. This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.5
for this choice of
\gamma
.

The case
\gamma=\infty
follows from asymptotics for the size of the largest hole in a random point set which were given by Reznikov and Saff
[
RS16
]
. More precisely, we obtain the statement of Proposition
4.5
in this case from Corollary 2.3 there applied to
\Phi(r)=r^{d}
together with the fact that convex domains satisfy an interior cone condition.

Remark 4.27
.

The form of the limit theorem in Proposition
4.26
is different from the one used in
[
KS20
, Sec. 3]
. There, we relied on
[
Coh04
, Thm. 2]
which gives an almost sure convergence together with convergence in
L_{2}
but we neglected that convergence of arbitrary high moments is necessary. The above proof remedies this.

Remark 4.28
.

Actually, we used only a special instance of Cohort’s result which is valid for different measures
\mu
, too. Further, the arguments behind the proof of Proposition
4.26
transfer also to points sampled from other measures which are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and have a density bounded from below. One probably needs to employ different methods and arrives at qualitatively distinct results if more general measures are considered.

Remark 4.29
.

Proposition
4.26
also holds in more general spaces. For example, a simplified proof working on general Riemannian manifolds was presented by us in
[
KS21
]
and shows that one may easily obtain convergence in
L_{p}
for
0<p<\infty
using Jensen’s inequality.

Remark 4.30
.

One can also show Corollary
4.6
without Theorem
4.2
or Proposition
4.26
by using techniques similar in spirit to
[
KNS22
]
.

Remark 4.31
.

Regarding the asymptotic constant in Proposition
4.26
, note that the quantity
\bigl(\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)/\mathrm{vol}\bigl(\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}\bigr)\bigr)^{1/d}
is related to the concept of volume ratio, which plays an important role in Banach space geometry, see, e.g., Szarek and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
ST80
]
. Further, as
\gamma\to\infty
the factor
\bigl(\Gamma(1+\frac{\gamma}{d})\bigr)^{1/\gamma}
tends to infinity, which corresponds to the fact that the covering radius of
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
is typically of larger order than
n^{-1/d}
.

Remark 4.32
.

On smoothly bounded domains or polytopes, exact asymptotics are also available for the covering radius of i.i.d. uniform random points. In
[
RS16
]
a proof may be found for the case of three-dimensional polytopes but in fact it generalizes to arbitrary dimension. Even more precise results such as a limiting Gumbel law, an important extreme value distribution showing up as the limit distribution of rescaled maxima, were derived by Penrose
[
Pen21
]
. Further, the covering radius of a random point set is connected to the coupon collector’s problem, which asks for the number of coupons (points) that a collector has to draw in order to obtain a complete collection (hit every set in a diameter-bounded equal volume partition of
\Omega
).

4.6
Open questions

Question 4.1
.

When discussing the moving least squares method, we started with general subspaces which we then replaced by the space of polynomials. It would be interesting to consider other subspaces and develop a more general formulation of our results.

Can Theorem
4.2
be generalized to other function spaces?

It seems worthwhile to pass from moving least squares to general weighted least squares in order to extend our results to other function spaces where smoothness is not determined via polynomials. In particular, it could be of interest to study the integration problem in Hilbert spaces of functions on which function evaluations are continuous. These widely used spaces are called reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces and the corresponding kernel allows for an analysis of sampling methods, see for example the book
[
BT04
]
by Berlinet and Thomas-Agnan or
[
Wen05
, Ch. 10]
.

Question 4.2
.

As mentioned in Section
4.4
, we could not extend our results to Besov spaces.
The following question is left open maybe due to our own incompetence or due to the lack of a suitable seminorm.

Does Theorem
4.10
also hold for Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p\tau}
?

Similar to the proof of Theorem 23 in
[
NT06
]
one may use interpolation arguments to prove an upper bound, but in this way an additional
\varepsilon>0
appears in the exponent of the
L_{\gamma}
-norm in the case
q<p
. Therefore, new ideas seem to be required.

Question 4.3
.

As explained in Section
4.3
, convexity is central to our proof technique and therefore new ideas seem necessary to answer the following question.

Can Theorem
4.2
be extended to general bounded Lipschitz domains?

A possible approach could consist in using a different cover adapted to the boundary of the domain, as well as to the point set used. However, caution is required to ensure
independence of the constants from the point set.

Question 4.4
.

We did not track the hidden constants in Theorem
4.2
(and the derived results). As the dependence of the involved constants on the dimension is important for tractability considerations, the following question comes to mind.

What is the dependence of the constants in Theorem
4.2
on the dimension?

We think that our proof technique leaves an exponentially growing gap with regard to this question. It appears easier to determine for fixed dimension the asymptotic constants in front of the expected radius of random information if
n
tends to infinity. For example, one could use a more elaborate fooling function such as a smoothed version of the distance function, as suggested to us by M. Ullrich, and employ the full strength of the limiting behaviour of random distortion given by Proposition
4.26
. Such questions are of interest also for the minimal radius and optimal points, see, e.g., Novak
[
Nov20
]
.

Question 4.5
.

One drawback of the linear algorithm attaining the upper bound in the integration problem is that we do not know the weights it uses.

Can the weights of a suitable linear algorithm achieving the asymptotic upper bound in Theorem
4.2
b be expressed geometrically?

It is clear that the weights of any linear algorithm (of the form (
4.4
)) achieving the asymptotic upper bound have to depend on the point set. In the upcoming Chapter
5
we will see that these weights can be given explicitly for Hölder spaces with small smoothness and this choice may be optimal for higher smoothness too.

5
\cdot
Interlude - optimal transport and cubature rules

This chapter connects the integration problem to the concept of discrepancy, which is related to equal-weight integration, and thus links Chapter
4
with the upcoming Chapter
6
. The focus will be on the weights used by (near-)optimal cubature rules for Hölder and Lipschitz functions. For these weights a geometric expression as volumes of Voronoi cells is shown, and further that equal weights can be optimal in high dimensions for uniform random points. Along the way, we pile dirt onto heaps and use classical results from probability to study Monte Carlo.

The main results of Chapter
4
show that we can assess the quality of any point set
P_{n}
for several approximation and integration problems in terms of the distortion, an average over the distance function to
P_{n}
defined in (
4.14
). Much alike, both the distortion and the discrepancy, which will be introduced in Chapter
6
, measure the distance between a measure
\mu
and a point set
P_{n}
, which can be represented by a sum of Dirac measures. Distances between measures have been studied in the field of optimal transport, which gave rise to the concept of the Wasserstein (or Kantorovich) distance, a well known way to metrize the space of measures. We refer to the book of Villani
[
Vil03
]
for a comprehensive account and limit ourselves to a brief introduction in the following.

To transport the unit mass from a Borel probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to another, say
\nu
, one can use a transference plan or coupling
\pi
which is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with marginals
\mu
and
\nu
. We collect all such transference plans between
\mu
and
\nu
into a set denoted by
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
. Optimal transport concerns itself with finding a transference plan with minimal transportation cost, that is, attaining the infimum in

\mathcal{T}_{r}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x-y\|_{2}^{r}{\rm d}\pi(x,y),

where we took the Euclidean distance raised to some power
0<r<\infty
as a cost function. By setting
W_{r}=\mathcal{T}_{r}^{1/r}
for
1\leq r<\infty
and
W_{r}=\mathcal{T}_{r}
for
0<r<1,
one can define a metric on the space of probability measures with finite
r
-th moments, the Wasserstein (or Kantorovich) distance, see
[
Vil03
, Thm. 7.3]
. The case
r=1
is especially interesting as we will see in a moment.

To prepare for the connection between Wasserstein distance and the integration problem, assume from now on that
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and that
\nu
is supported on a point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e., of the form

\nu_{P_{n},a}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}},\quad\text{where }a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ with }\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1,

(5.1)

where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the Dirac measure of any
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
equals one if
x\in A
and zero else.

The optimal transportation cost between
\mu
and
\nu_{P_{n},a}
as above attains a minimal value which is equal to the distortion, that is, for any
0<r<\infty
,

\inf_{a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{T}_{r}(\mu,\nu_{P_{n},a})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\min_{i=1,\dots,n}\|x-x_{i}\|_{2}^{r}{\rm d}\mu(x)\quad(=D_{\mu,P_{n},r})

(5.2)

and the infimum over the weights is in fact attained at

\mu\big(C(x_{1},P_{n})\big),\dots,\mu\big(C(x_{n},P_{n})\big)

(5.3)

where
C(x_{i},P_{n})
is the Voronoi cell of
x_{i}
as in (
4.15
). A proof can be deduced from
[
GL00
, Lem. 3.1]
and will be provided for completeness in Appendix
C
. The idea behind is to consider the coupling induced by the map
(x,y)\mapsto\big(x,T_{P_{n}}(x)\big)
, where
T_{P_{n}}
is the quantizer from (
4.15
) in Section
4.5
mapping
x
to the nearest point of
P_{n}
.

Let us recall the integration problem on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose we are given a normed space of functions
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the embedding
F\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
holds, see Section
2.3
. The goal is to find
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for every
f\in F
,
A(f)=\varphi\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)
approximates the integral
{\rm INT}_{\mu}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)
with respect to the absolutely continuous probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here, assuming absolute continuity ensures that the problem is not too easy.

As mentioned in the introduction to Chapter
4
the optimal
\varphi
can be chosen linear, that is, the optimal sampling operator is of the form

Q_{P_{n},a}\colon F\to\mathbb{R},\quad f\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f(x_{i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\nu_{P_{n},a}(x),

(5.4)

with
\nu_{P_{n},a}
as in (
5.1
). This is a cubature rule with points
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and weights
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that we mean in fact
P_{n}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to be able to associate the weights to the points but out of habit use the set notation which should not cause confusion. Then the worst-case error over the unit ball
F_{0}
of
F
is

e(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n},a})=\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\nu_{P_{n},a}(x)\Big|,

and can be seen as a distance between the measures
\mu
and
\nu_{P_{n},a}
. If we consider the special case
F=\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the space of Lipschitz functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, equipped with the seminorm

|f|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\|x-y\|_{2}},

then the Kantorovich-Rubinstein duality theorem (
[
Vil03
, Thm. 1.14]
) yields that, for any
\nu_{P_{n},a}
as in (
5.1
),

\sup_{|f|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1}\Big|\int f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\nu_{P_{n},a}(x)\Big|=W_{1}(\mu,\nu_{P_{n},a}).

(5.5)

This means that the worst-case error over Lipschitz functions with seminorm at most one can be expressed as the
W_{1}
-distance between the measure
\mu
according to which we want to integrate and the discrete measure
\nu_{P_{n},a}
representing the algorithm
Q_{P_{n},a}
.

It seems worth noting that the metric
W_{1}
is also called the earth mover’s distance for the following reason. If one imagines the density of
\mu
to be a distribution of dirt, which one wishes to collect into heaps of size
a_{i}
located at points
x_{i}
, and the work needed for transporting dirt is proportional to the distance it is moved, then the least amount of work is exactly the right-hand side of (
5.5
).

Optimal weights.
By means of the expression of the minimal distance
W_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1}
in (
5.2
) one can deduce that the normalized weights minimizing the worst-case error over the unit ball of the seminormed space
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are given as in (
5.3
) by the
\mu
-content of the Voronoi cells. This also holds more generally as we now explain.

Equality (
5.5
) extends to the Hölder classes
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),0<s<1
, with seminorm
|\cdot|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
defined in (
4.9
). It can be deduced for example from the more general Theorem 5 in Gruber
[
Gru04
]
that

\inf_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{|f|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1}\Big|\int f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|=D_{\mu,P_{n},s},

(5.6)

where the optimal weights are again as in (
5.3
). This holds true for
s=1
if
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is replaced by
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For convenience we provide a proof in Appendix
C
.

Strictly speaking, since we take the supremum over a set of seminorm at most one and not over the unit ball of a normed space, we are not in the setting considered before. However, the space
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be equipped by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}+|\cdot|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, see the line below (
4.9
), and one can proceed analogously for
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. As the seminorm is bounded by the norm thus defined, one can get an upper bound for the radius of information of
P_{n}
for integration in these spaces.

More can be said if
\mu
has bounded support
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then the space of Lipschitz functions
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
will be denoted by
{\rm Lip}(D)
and becomes a normed space when equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\rm Lip}(D)}
which is the restriction of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. For
{\rm Lip}(D)
and the Hölder spaces
C^{s}(D)
defined in Section
4.2
we have the following known result.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
\mu
be a probability measure supported on a bounded set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for any point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset D
,

\inf_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{\|f\|_{C^{s}(D)}\leq 1}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|\asymp D_{\mu,P_{n},s},

where the implicit constants depend only on
D
and the weights given in (
5.3
) attain the bound. The statement remains true for
s=1
if
C^{s}(D)
is replaced by
{\rm Lip}(D)
.

Since linear algorithms are optimal for this integration problem this proposition implies that the radius of information given by the point set
P_{n}
satisfies

r\big(C^{s}(D),\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},P_{n}\big)\asymp D_{\mu,P_{n},s},

which extends Theorem
4.10
for these spaces. An analogous result holds for
{\rm Lip}(D)
.

Proposition
5.1
provides us with (near-)optimal weights and the cubature rule

Q_{P_{n},\mu}(f):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu\big(C(x_{i},P_{n})\big)f(x_{i}),

(5.7)

which is asymptotically optimal in the sense of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(C^{s}(D),\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n},\mu})\leq C\,r\big(C^{s}(D),\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},P_{n}\big),}

where the constant
C
is independent of
P_{n}
.

In general, it is a question of interest to compute (near-)optimal weights for general point sets and in most cases they are unknown. It would be interesting to have an analogon of Proposition
5.1
for other classes such as Sobolev spaces, see also Question
4.5
and Remark
5.3
below.

Before we turn to the case of equal weights, we show that, under minor assumptions, normalized weights
cannot
be much worse than general real weights. Since we were not able to locate a proof of this known fact, we provide one in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure supported on a set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
F
be a normed space of functions with
F\hookrightarrow C_{b}(D)
. Suppose that the constant function
\mathbf{1}_{D}
belongs to
F
(and thus all constant functions). If
Q_{P_{n},a}
is a cubature rule as in (
5.4
), then the normalized weights
a_{i}^{*}=a_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i},i=1,\ldots,n,
satisfy

\sup_{\|f\|_{F}\leq 1}\Big|\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{*}f(x_{i})\Big|\leq(1+C\|\mathbf{1}_{D}\|_{F})\sup_{\|f\|_{F}\leq 1}\Big|\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f(x_{i})\Big|.

The assumptions on the function space
F
are satisfied for all function spaces considered until now if the set
D
is bounded. This provides some justification for studying equal weights
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{n}=1/n
.

Remark 5.3
.

In the case of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, one can compute optimal weights explicitly by solving a linear system, see, e.g.,
[
NW10
, Ch. 10.2]
. For example, this can be done for the Sobolev spaces
W^{s}_{2}(\Omega)
, where a kernel can be derived from Novak, M. Ullrich, Woźniakowski and Zhang
[
NUWZ18
]
, see also Section 2.3 in
[
KS20
]
. However, there does not seem to be a convenient geometric expression of near-optimal weights.

In the following, we will discuss equal-weight rules in Hölder spaces and discover that these are in fact asymptotically optimal, provided the dimension is large enough. This motivates the study of discrepancy in the upcoming Chapter
6
.

Equal weights.

Define, for
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the equal-weight cubature rule

Q_{P_{n}}(f):=Q_{P_{n},(1/n,\ldots,1/n)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i}).

(5.8)

Thus we allow only the reconstruction map
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi(y)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}
and we may interpret the worst-case error of
Q_{P_{n}}
, which is

e(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n}})=\sup_{f\in F_{0}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|,

as a measure of
the
quality of information given by sampling at
P_{n}
.

At first sight, it seems that by restricting to equal weights much flexibility is lost and only special kinds of point sets may yield good equal-weight rules. This is true, for example, if one requires exact integration on subspaces as in the case of spherical designs, surveyed e.g. by Brauchart and Grabner in
[
BG15
]
.

However, if we are interested in asymptotically optimal equal-weight rules with an error allowed to exceed the minimal error

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},n)}=\inf_{\#P_{n}=n}r(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},P_{n})

by a proportional amount, then in some cases typical information is asymptotically optimal. As in Section
2.3
, we use for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the random point set
P_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}:=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
with random points
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
drawn independently and uniformly according to the probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Given random information, the corresponding random equal-weight rule associating to each
f
the random number
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})
is the prototypical example of a Monte Carlo method, whereas the algorithm in (
5.8
) using the deterministic points above is often called a quasi-Monte Carlo method.
Monte Carlo methods are very popular among practioners in fields such as physics or mathematical finance where high-dimensional integrals have to be evaluated and common cubature rules based on polynomial interpolation become computationally expensive or numerically unstable. In a moment, we shall give some intuition why it is so effective in these situations. We refer to
[
Nie92
]
(see also the survey
[
Nie78
]
) for further information.

The law of large numbers states that, almost surely,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})\to\mathbb{E}f(X_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty

provided that
f(X_{1})
is integrable. Further, the central limit theorem implies that the fluctuations around this limit are asymptotically of order
n^{-1/2}
, that is, we have the convergence in distribution

\sqrt{n}\,\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big)\to\mathcal{N}\big(0,{\rm Var}_{\mu}(f)\big)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

to a centered Gaussian with variance
{\rm Var}_{\mu}(f)=\mathbb{E}f(X_{1})^{2}-\big(\mathbb{E}f(X_{1})\big)^{2}
provided that
f(X_{1})
is square-integrable.

Thus we make an error on individual functions which is of the dimension- but also smoothness-independent rate
n^{-1/2}
. For obtaining a bound on the worst-case error over a function class, one can employ empirical process theory or entropy estimates, see, e.g., van der Vaart and Wellner
[
vW96
]
. However, this necessarily increases the error, if only by a factor.

In high dimensions the rate
n^{-1/2}
can be optimal for functions of low effective smoothness
s/d
. For example, it follows from Theorem
4.10
and (
4.7
) (see also Proposition
5.1
for
s\in(0,1)
) that for the Hölder spaces
C^{s}(D)
, where
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded convex set and
s>0
, the
n
-th minimal radius of information satisfies

r\big(C^{s}(D),{\rm INT},n\big)\asymp n^{-s/d},

where integration is with respect to the Lebesgue measure. In fact, using the Monte Carlo method above one can achieve this optimal rate if
s/d<1/2
at least on the unit cube as the following discussion shows.

Among others, Kloeckner
[
Klo20
]
studied the related topic of (Wasserstein) distances between a measure
\mu
and its empirical measure
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
which corresponds to the Monte Carlo method. Theorem 1.4 in
[
Klo20
]
shows that, see also related work by Fournier and Guillin
[
FG15
]
and van der Vaart and Wellner
[
vW96
, Sec. 2.7.1]
, for any
s\geq 1
and any probability measure
\mu
on
[0,1]^{d}
,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{\|f\|_{C^{s}([0,1]^{d})}\leq 1}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|\lesssim\begin{cases}n^{-s/d},&\text{if }s<d/2,\\
n^{-1/2}\log n,&\text{if }s=d/2,\\
n^{-1/2},&\text{if }s>d/2.\\
\end{cases}

Note that here for
s\in\mathbb{N}
, in
contrast
to our definition, the space
C^{s}
consists of functions whose derivatives of order
s-1
are Lipschitz continuous and is normed slightly differently. This means that in the low smoothness regime
s/d<1/2
the Monte Carlo method achieves on average the optimal rate
n^{-s/d}
. At the threshold
s/d=1/2
an additional logarithm comes into play and for higher smoothness we arrive at the standard Monte Carlo rate of
n^{-1/2}
which cannot be improved in general.

The special case of Lipschitz functions (
s=1
) leads to the case distinction into
d\geq 3
, where we have the optimal rate,
d=2
, where we loose a logarithm, and
d=1
, where we have the rate
n^{-1/2}
. Since Lipschitz functions are related to Wasserstein distance
W_{1}
, this case received particular attention, see, e.g., Weed and Bach
[
WB19
]
for some references on recent progress.

It is no coincidence that also in the related field of optimal matching, where one tries to find a bijection between two random point sets minimizing a sum of distances, the two-dimensional case is especially interesting, see for example
[
Tal14
, Ch. 4]
. There, the author calls the worst-case error of such a Monte Carlo method a discrepancy as it measures the distance between the uniform measure and its associated empirical measure. This is related to but different from the discrepancy we will study in the following Chapter
6
.

Remark 5.4
.

It is a curious phenomenon that we have a threshold as above. According to Bobkov and Ledoux
[
BL20
]
, who investigated optimal matchings in one dimension, this is connected to the fact that periodic functions of smoothness
s>1/2
can be expanded into absolutely convergent Fourier series, which is no longer true for
s=1/2
.

6
\cdot
Isotropic discrepancy of lattice point sets

In this final chapter we will consider the isotropic discrepancy, a quantity related to the radius of information for certain integration problems, where we restrict to equal-weight cubature algorithms using information either at specific point sets or random ones. As motivated in Chapter
5
, the findings here complement the study of the radius of (random) standard information in Chapter
4
. More precisely, we study the isotropic discrepancy of a special type of structured point sets, called lattice point sets, and show that in dimension
d\geq 2
they do not attain the optimal behaviour exhibited by random constructions.

After a rather general introduction, we present in Section
6.2
the results obtained together with F. Pillichshammer in the publications
[
PS20
]
and
[
SP21
]
. The subsequent Sections
6.3
and
6.4
contain the ideas behind the proofs and Section
6.5
ends this chapter with open questions and opportunities for subsequent work.

6.1
Introduction and motivation

We feel that the motivation for the study of istropic discrepancy, which is a special kind of discrepancy related to convex sets, benefits from a more general perspective. Thus, consider a measure space
(D,\Sigma,\mu)
with
\mu(D)=1
and a set system
\mathcal{D}\subset\Sigma
containing
D
as well as the empty set. Then, for any point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset D
, the corresponding equal-weight algorithm
Q_{P_{n}}\colon f\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})
as in (
5.8
) incurs an error on the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{A}\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
which can be bounded via

\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{A}(x_{i})-\int_{D}\mathbf{1}_{A}(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|\leq D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n}),\quad A\in\mathcal{D},

(6.1)

where

D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n}):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{D}}\Big|\frac{\#(A\cap P_{n})}{n}-\mu(A)\Big|

(6.2)

is the discrepancy of the point set
P_{n}
with respect to the system
\mathcal{D}
(and the measure
\mu
which is omitted in the notation).

Analytically, the discrepancy
D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n})
quantifies the uniform error made by the algorithm
Q_{P_{n}}
on the set of characteristic functions belonging to
\mathcal{D}
, and geometrically it compares the maximal deviation between the fraction of points of
P_{n}
belonging to such a set and its
\mu
-volume. There is a whole theory related to this notion of irregularity of distribution, see, e.g., the books by Beck and Chen
[
BC08
]
, Drmota and Tichy
[
DT97
]
, and Kuipers and Niederreiter
[
KN74
]
.

In the following, we would like to construct a space of functions built from indicator functions such that the discrepancy
D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n})
is equal to the quality of
Q_{P_{n}}
for this space. For this purpose, we follow Pausinger and Svane
[
PS15
]
and start with the class of simple functions

\Sigma(\mathcal{D}):=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\mathbf{1}_{A_{j}}\colon m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},A_{j}\in\mathcal{D}\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,m,\Big\}.

(6.3)

The complexity of a simple function
f\in\Sigma(\mathcal{D})
is captured by the sum of its coefficients, minimized over all representations, i.e.,

V_{\mathcal{D}}(f):=\inf\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}|a_{j}|\colon f=a_{0}\mathbf{1}_{D}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\mathbf{1}_{A_{j}}\text{ with }m,a_{j}\text{ and }A_{j}\text{ as in \eqref{eq:simple}}\Big\}

which is called the
\mathcal{D}
-variation of
f
. Note that the constant part does not contribute.

By direct calculation, we can extend (
6.1
) to simple functions and obtain

\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})-\int_{D}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|\leq D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n})V_{\mathcal{D}}(f),\quad f\in\Sigma(\mathcal{D}).

(6.4)

It is important to note that this bound decomposes into a part depending only on the point set and another depending only on the function. For this reason one may call (
6.4
) a Koksma-Hlawka inequality in the spirit of a theorem of the same name, for which we refer to e.g.
[
KN74
, Ch. 2, Thm. 5.5]
.

To extend (
6.4
) further, we use uniform limits of simple functions to find a suitable function space containing
\Sigma(\mathcal{D})
. Let
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function such that there exists a sequence of simple functions
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Sigma(\mathcal{D})
converging uniformly to
f
. Then define the
\mathcal{D}
-variation of
f
by

V_{\mathcal{D}}(f):=\inf\big\{\liminf_{n\to\infty}V_{\mathcal{D}}(f_{n})\colon f_{n}\in\Sigma(\mathcal{D}),f_{n}\to f\text{ uniformly}\big\}.

If we denote by
V(\mathcal{D})
the space of all functions with finite
\mathcal{D}
-variation, then it defines a seminorm on it which is zero for the constant functions. We obtain

\sup_{V_{\mathcal{D}}(f)\leq 1}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})-\int_{D}f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|=D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n}),

(6.5)

where the upper bound follows from (
6.4
) and for the lower bound a sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}
with
V_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{1}_{A_{n}})\to D_{\mathcal{D}}(P_{n})
works.

The identity (
6.5
) states that the worst-case error of any equal-weight algorithm based on a point set over the functions with
\mathcal{D}
-variation at most one is equal to the discrepancy with respect to
\mathcal{D}
. This should be compared with (
5.5
) relating the worst-case error of a cubature rule on functions with
|f|_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
with the Wasserstein distance. Admittedly, the set
\{f\in V(\mathcal{D})\colon V_{\mathcal{D}}(f)\leq 1\}
may seem less intuitive, as it is constructed by completing simple functions in the uniform norm. Thus, we will be more concrete and specialize to the case which interests us in this chapter.

Let now
D=[0,1)^{d}
be equipped with the Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{vol}
and consider the set system
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{K}
, the class of convex sets contained in
[0,1)^{d}
. Then we call

D_{\mathcal{K}}(P_{n}):=\sup_{K\in\mathcal{K}}\Big|\frac{\#(K\cap P_{n})}{n}-\mathrm{vol}(K)\Big|

(6.6)

the isotropic discrepancy of the point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. It is also denoted by
J_{n}(P_{n})
instead, which is the notation used in
[
PS20
,
SP21
]
. Its definition dates back to Hlawka
[
Hla64
]
and its name
was coined by
Zaremba
[
Zar70
]
who apparently based it on the isotropy, that is the rotation invariance, of the family of convex sets. This is in
contrast
to the family
\mathcal{A}
of boxes with sides parallel to the coordinate axes used for estimating integration errors in anisotropic function spaces of dominating mixed smoothness.

The relation (
6.5
) expresses the isotropic discrepancy as a worst-case error of the corresponding equal-weight or quasi-Monte Carlo rule. In this sense, it measures the quality of the information given by a point set for a specific integration problem. It can be deduced from
[
PS15
, Thm. 3.12]
that the twice continuously differentiable functions
C^{2}((0,1)^{d})
are continuously embedded into the space
V_{\mathcal{K}}
if it is equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}+V_{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot)
. Additionally, the class
\mathcal{K}
encompasses many other set systems used for studying worst-case errors, as for example
\mathcal{A}
which is
related to Hardy-Krause variation and worst-case errors in spaces of mixed smoothness. Therefore, it bounds the integration error of equal-weight rules in related function spaces.

Concerning further applications, Aistleitner, Brauchart and Dick
[
ABD12
]
used an area-preserving map
\Phi\colon[0,1)^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
to relate the isotropic discrepancy to the spherical cap discrepancy, where we equip
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with its normalized rotation invariant measure
\sigma^{(2)}
and consider the system
\mathcal{C}
of all spherical caps

{\rm Cap}(x,t):=\{y\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\colon\langle x,y\rangle\geq t\},\quad x\in\mathbb{S}^{2},t\in[-1,1],

that is, intersections of the sphere with
half-spaces
. Through elaborate computations, they proved in
[
ABD12
, Thm. 6]
an upper bound for the spherical cap discrepancy of the mapped point set in terms of the isotropic discrepancy of the original point set
P_{n}\subset[0,1)^{2}
, which is of the form

D_{\mathcal{C}}\big(\Phi(P_{n})\big)\leq 11\,D_{\mathcal{K}}(P_{n}).

(6.7)

This was then applied to Fibonacci lattice points and certain digital nets, which are both classes of point sets with low discrepancy with respect to
\mathcal{A}
. Discussing their results, they stated on p. 1001 in
[
ABD12
]
:

“Whether
(0,m,2)
-nets and/or Fibonacci lattices achieve the optimal rate of convergence for the isotropic discrepancy is an open question.”

This served as our original motivation to study the isotropic discrepancy of more general lattice point sets as introduced below. In the above question, the optimal rate is meant to be the rate of decay of the minimal isotropic discrepancy given by

D_{\mathcal{K}}(n):=\inf_{\#P_{n}=n}D_{\mathcal{K}}(P_{n}),

(6.8)

where the infimum is over all point sets
P_{n}\subset[0,1)^{d}
with
n
points. In
[
Sch75
]
Schmidt proved the lower bound

D_{\mathcal{K}}(n)\gtrsim n^{-2/(d+1)}

(6.9)

using a now famous technique. This bound is attained up to a power of a logarithm as shown by Stute
[
Stu77
]
for
d\geq 3
and Beck
[
Bec88
]
for
d=2
. The proofs rely on the probabilistic method and thus one does not know of an explicit point set achieving this upper bound. Interestingly enough, the proof of Stute shows that i.i.d. random points with density absolutely continuous to the Lebesgue measure satisfy this bound and are thus optimal up to a logarithmic factor. Note that this cannot hold for
d=2
as the central limit theorem yields a rate of at best
n^{-1/2}
, see Chapter
5
.

We investigate the isotropic discrepancy of lattice-point sets, defined as follows.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N},d\geq 2,
and let

L=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}b_{i}\colon k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,d\Big\}

be a
d
-dimensional lattice spanned by linearly independent basis vectors
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If the lattice
L
contains
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, it is called an integration lattice and generates the lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L):=L\cap[0,1)^{d}
.

The Fibonacci lattice point set is a special case which can be generated by a single vector and thus is of rank one, see, e.g.,
[
Nie92
, Ch. 5.3]
. To define it, let
F_{1}=F_{2}=1
and
F_{m}=F_{m-1}+F_{m-2}
for
m>2
be the Fibonacci numbers. Then the Fibonacci lattice point set with
F_{n}
points is given by

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\Big\{\Big(\frac{i}{F_{n}},\Big\{\frac{iF_{n-1}}{F_{n}}\Big\}\Big),\quad i=0,\dots,F_{n}-1\Big\},

where
\{\cdot\}\in[0,1)
is the fractional part. For an illustration of
\mathcal{F}_{8}
with
F_{8}=21
points see Figure
6.1
in Section
6.3
below.

Lattice point sets are used in numerical integration for constructing good equal weight rules, see, e.g.,
[
Nie92
, Ch. 5]
as well as Sloan and Joe
[
SJ94
]
. In particular, the Fibonacci lattice point set achieves the best order for discrepancy with respect to
\mathcal{A}
. It also has optimal dispersion, which measures the size of the largest empty axis-parallel box in the cube, see Breneis and Hinrichs
[
BH20
]
as well as Lachmann and Wiart
[
LW21
]
. Let us mention that also for the dispersion uniform random points are the best point sets known in certain (high) dimensions, see, e.g., Litvak and Livshyts
[
LL21
]
.

However, the results presented in the next section will show that the special structure of lattice point sets compared to random points is a disadvantage with respect to isotropic discrepancy.

Remark 6.2
.

The Voronoi cells of a lattice point set are of equal volume, at least when it is considered on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}\simeq[0,1)^{d}
. Therefore, equal weights are optimal for the integration of (periodic) Hölder and Lipschitz functions as Proposition
5.1
shows. This
justifies considering
only equal weights for the integration of functions from
V_{\mathcal{K}}
when using lattice point sets.

6.2
Discussion of results

Among the results we present here are a lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy of lattice point sets (Theorem
6.3
), a characterization of their isotropic discrepancy in terms of the spectral test of the underlying lattice (Theorem
6.5
) and an equivalence for the radius of information of lattice point sets for
L_{q}
-approximation,
1\leq q\leq\infty
, in the Sobolev spaces
W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})
(Theorem
6.12
).

In
[
PS20
, Thm. 1]
we derived a lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy of lattice-point sets. We present a corrected version as follows, see Remark
6.9
.

Theorem 6.3

(
[
PS20
, Thm. 1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}(L)
be an
n
-element lattice point set in
[0,1)^{d}
. Then

D_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\geq\min\Big\{\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}+1},\frac{c_{d}}{n^{1/d}}\Big\},

where

c_{d}:=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{d}+1}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)\right)^{-1/d}.

If additionally
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
, then
c_{d}
may be replaced by
c_{d}^{\prime}=c\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)\right)^{-1/d}
for some
c>0
that is independent of
d
.

Stirling’s formula for the Gamma function
\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{x-1}{\rm e}^{-t}{\rm d}t,x>0
, see (
D.5
), implies

c_{d}^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{\pi\,{\rm e}}{2}}\frac{1}{d}\to 1\quad\text{as }d\to\infty.

Theorem
6.3
shows that the isotropic discrepancy of lattice point sets decays slower than the minimal isotropic discrepancy (
6.8
) and answers the question from
[
ABD12
]
mentioned near the end of the introduction to this chapter in the negative. Therefore, the upper bound of
[
ABD12
, Lem. 17]
on the spherical discrepancy of mapped Fibonacci lattice points, which is of order
n^{-1/2}
cannot be improved. We remark that Larcher
[
Lar89
]
has shown the lower bound of order
n^{-1/d}
already for the class of lattice point sets of rank one by using a different approach than ours. In dimension
d\geq 3
, the lower bound of Theorem
6.3
implies together with the discussion on the minimal isotropic discrepancy that random information prevails over information given by lattice point sets.

We obtained the lower bound in Theorem
6.3
using a characterization of the isotropic discrepancy of
\mathcal{P}(L)
in terms of the spectral test
\sigma(L)
of the lattice given as follows.

Definition 6.4
.

The spectral test of a
d
-dimensional lattice
L
is given by

\sigma(L):=\frac{1}{\min\big\{\|h\|_{2}\colon h\in L^{\bot}\setminus\{0\}\big\}},

where the dual lattice is given by

L^{\bot}:=\left\{h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\langle h,x\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }x\in L\right\}.

We present the corrected version of Theorem 2 in
[
PS20
]
, see again Remark
6.9
.

Theorem 6.5

(
[
PS20
, Thm. 2]
,
[
SP21
, Thm. 1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}(L)
be an
n
-element lattice point set in
[0,1)^{d}
. Then

\frac{\sigma(L)}{\sqrt{d}+\sigma(L)}\leq D_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\leq d\,2^{2(d+1)}\sigma(L).

If
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
, then the lower bound can be replaced by
c\,\sigma(L)
, where
c>0
does not depend on the dimension
d
.

To give some background, let us mention that the spectral test originated as a means to evaluate the randomness of linear congruential pseudorandom number generators, see Knuth
[
Knu98
, Ch. 3.3.4]
and
[
Nie92
, Ch. 7.2]
. It is a well known numerical quantity to assess the coarseness of (integration) lattices and admits a convenient geometric interpretation which is developed in Section
6.3
, see also
[
SJ94
, p. 29]
.

Example 6.6
.

Consider
n=m^{d}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the lattice point set generated by the scaled integer lattice
(1/m)\mathbb{Z}^{d}
contains
n
points. Its dual is given by
m\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and thus
\sigma\big((1/m)\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big)=\|(m,0,\dots)\|_{2}=n^{-1/d}
.
Its isotropic discrepancy appears to be unknown.

Example 6.7
.

Let
n=m^{d-1}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the lattice point set generated by
L=\mathbb{Z}\times(1/m)\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
has
n
points and is contained in the hyperplane
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. Its dual is
L^{\bot}=\mathbb{Z}\times m\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
and
\sigma(L)=\|(1,0,\dots,0)\|_{2}=1
. Its isotropic discrepancy is one.

If
\sigma(L)>1/2
, then the lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L)
is concentrated on a few hyperplanes, whereas the case
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
corresponds to a more evenly distributed
\mathcal{P}(L)
. We have the following lower bound on the spectral test of an
n
-element lattice point set, which together with Theorem
6.5
implies Theorem
6.3
.

Proposition 6.8

(
[
PS20
, Prop. 3]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}(L)
be an
n
-element lattice point set in
[0,1)^{d}
. Then

\sigma(L)\geq\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)\right)^{-1/d}\frac{1}{n^{1/d}}.

We did not find a proof of this result in the literature and thus provide a proof in Section
6.3
using Minkowski’s theorem which is commonly used for bounding the length of the shortest vector in a lattice.

In Proposition
6.8
the rate
n^{-1/d}
can be achieved by rank-one lattices when
n
is prime, see Dick, Larcher, Pillichshammer and Woźniakowski
[
DLPW11
, Lem. 2]
who bound a figure of merit equivalent, up to constants in
d
, to the inverse of the spectral test.

Remark 6.9
.

Theorem
6.3
and Theorem
6.5
are the corrected versions of
[
PS20
, Thm. 1 and Thm. 2]
respectively. The upper bound in the latter has been already repaired by
[
SP21
, Thm. 1]
; details are discussed in Remark
6.21
in Section
6.4
. Recently, we discovered also an error in the lower bound of
[
PS20
, Thm. 2]
which affects also
[
PS20
, Thm. 1]
without the restriction of
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
, see Remark
6.15
in Section
6.3
.

Remark 6.10
.

The characterization in Theorem
6.5
is true for more general point sets
of the form
(L+a)\cap[0,1)^{d}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which
contain
n
points, but one has to adjust the constants. This follows from the fact that by volume comparison the volume of the fundamental domain is roughly the inverse of the number of points.

Remark 6.11
.

Since, in dimension two, the minimal spherical cap discrepancy and isotropic discrepancy are of order
n^{-3/4}
and
n^{-2/3}
up to logarithmic factors, respectively, the bound of
[
ABD12
]
stated in (
6.7
) cannot be used to prove that the mapped Fibonacci lattice point set has optimal spherical discrepancy as the numerics suggest. However, it may be used to
improve
on the current rate of
n^{-1/2}
for the best known deterministic constructions, see also Etayo
[
Eta21
]
for such a sequence of point sets apart from the mapped Fibonacci lattice point set. Note that the upper bound on the minimal spherical cap discrepancy was obtained by Beck using the Probabilistic method, more precisely, jittered sampling, see, e.g.,
[
BC08
, Thm. 24D]
. See also the author’s master thesis
[
Son19
]
for more information on spherical discrepancy.

The following application of Theorem
6.5
characterizes the radius of information given by a lattice point set with respect to the problems of approximation and integration of Sobolev functions on the cube.

Theorem 6.12

(
[
SP21
, Thm. 2]
)
.

Let
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
as in (
4.1
)
. For every lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L)
in
[0,1)^{d}
it holds that

r\big(W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{q}([0,1)^{d}),\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\,\asymp\,\sigma(L)^{s-d(1/p-1/q)_{+}}.

Here, the asymptotic notation conceals constants independent of the integration lattice
L
.

Note that the Sobolev space
W^{s}_{p}\big([0,1)^{d}\big)
is defined via restriction, see (
4.10
), and an analogous result holds for the space defined on
(0,1)^{d}
. Theorem
6.12
is a consequence of Theorem
4.2
applied to the Sobolev space
W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})
and the following equivalence, which may be interesting on its own.

Proposition 6.13

(
[
SP21
, Prop. 8]
)
.

For every lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L)
in
[0,1)^{d}
and every
0<\gamma\leq\infty
, we have

\sigma(L)\lesssim_{d,\gamma}\bigl\|\mathrm{dist}\bigl(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\bigr)\bigr\|_{L_{\gamma}([0,1)^{d})}\lesssim_{d}\sigma(L).

The proof and thus the proof of Theorem
6.12
will be given in Appendix
D.3
. The distortion of sets arising from lattices has been also studied in the context of lattice quantizers, see, e.g., Conway and Sloane
[
CS82
]
as well as
[
GL00
, Ch. 8]
.

Given a sequence of lattice point sets, we can interpret Theorem
6.12
as follows.

Corollary 6.14
.

A sequence of lattice point sets
\big(\mathcal{P}(L_{k})\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is asymptotically optimal for the
L_{q}
-approximation of Sobolev functions from
W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})
if and only if the spectral test behaves optimally, i.e.,

r\big(W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{q}([0,1)^{d}),\mathcal{P}(L_{k})\big)\asymp r\big(W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{q}([0,1)^{d}),n_{k}\big)

if and only if

\quad\sigma(L_{n_{k}})\asymp n_{k}^{-1/d},

where
n_{k}:=\#\mathcal{P}(L_{k})
.

By Theorem
6.5
one can replace the spectral test by isotropic discrepancy in Theorem
6.12
and Corollary
6.14
.
The latter shows
that lattice point sets with small spectral test may attain the optimal rates for the integration problem in Sobolev spaces but fail to attain the optimal rate for the isotropic discrepancy. Perhaps, this may be deduced from the function spaces
W^{s}_{p}([0,1)^{d})
and
V(\mathcal{K})
themselves.

6.3
Hyperplane coverings and lower bounds

We will give more details on the geometric interpretation of the spectral test
\sigma(L)
of a
d
-dimensional integration lattice
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and then use it to obtain lower bounds.

\sigma(L)

Figure 6.1
:
The first row depicts a Fibonacci lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L)\subset[0,1)^{d}
together with two different hyperplane coverings, whose distance is marked by an arrow.
Its length is
inverse to the vector in the dual lattice
L^{\bot}
, shown in the second row, which generates the hyperplane covering. The lattice in grey depicts
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\supset L
.

Following the presentation in Hellekalek
[
Hel98
]
, we call a family
\mathcal{H}
of parallel hyperplanes a covering of
L
if
L\subset\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H
and every
H\in\mathcal{H}
contains some lattice point of
L
. Then the distance between any two adjacent hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}
is equal to some number
d(\mathcal{H})\geq 0
. In fact, every primitive vector
x
in the dual
L^{\bot}
gives rise to a covering of
L
by parallel hyperplanes all
orthogonal
to
x
. The distance between adjacent hyperplanes in this covering is exactly
\|x\|_{2}^{-1}
. The spectral test is equal to the supremum
\sup_{\mathcal{H}}d(\mathcal{H})
over all coverings of
L
by parallel hyperplanes, which is attained by the covering of hyperplanes
\mathcal{H}^{*}=\mathcal{H}^{*}(L)
for which all hyperplanes are
orthogonal
to the shortest vector in the dual lattice
L^{\bot}
.
See Figure
6.1
for an illustration.
Let us note that here a lattice vector is called primitive if it is non-zero and the line connecting it to the origin does not contain another lattice vector.

In the following, we will only consider the covering
\mathcal{H}^{*}
and use it to explain the idea behind the lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy, Theorem
6.3
, which follows from the lower bound in Theorem
6.5
together with Proposition
6.8
. Detailed proofs can be found in Appendix
D.1
. The proof of the lower bound of Theorem
6.5
is divided into two cases depending on whether we suppose
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
or not.

In the case without any assumption on
\sigma(L)
we will find a convex set which has no volume and contains some fraction of the points. This is achieved by intersecting
[0,1)^{d}
with a hyperplane
H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
containing sufficiently many points. The existence of such
a hyperplane
will be a consequence of the pigeonhole principle and the fact that not too many hyperplanes of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
have a non-empty intersection with the unit cube as they are separated by a distance of
at least

\sigma(L)
.

Remark 6.15
.

The proof of
[
PS20
, Thm. 1]
contains a flawed estimate of the number of hyperplanes from
\mathcal{H}^{*}
intersecting
[0,1)^{d}
. There, the upper bound
\lfloor\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)\rfloor
was claimed but there can be in fact as many as
\lceil\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)\rceil\leq\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)+1
. The correction performed in this thesis gives a slightly worse bound but retains the asymptotic behaviour. Here, for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lceil a\rceil
is the smallest integer not smaller than
a
.

The lower bound with the assumption
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
relies on a recent result due to König and Rudelson
[
KR20
]
who have shown the following.

If an affine subspace has distance less than
1/2
to the center of the unit cube, then the volume of their intersection is at least a positive constant only depending on the codimension of the subspace.

We apply this to the continuum of parallel hyperplanes between two adjacent hyperplanes from
\mathcal{H}^{*}
whose convex hull contains the center of the cube. In this way, we get a lower bound on the volume of the cube intersected with the interior of this convex hull, which contains no points of
\mathcal{P}(L)
, and thus also a lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy.

6.4
Short lattice vectors and an upper bound

For the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
we have to show that for any convex set
K\subset[0,1)^{d}
the fraction of points of
\mathcal{P}(L)
contained in
K
is not too far from its volume. For this purpose, we rely on the lattice structure to form a partition into cells of volume
1/n
containing exactly one point. Then the discrepancy of the set
K
depends only on cells or points near the boundary of
K
.

That kind of argument appears throughout the discrepancy literature, even though sometimes superficially (especially in high dimensions). In particular, this applies to jittered or stratified sampling where in each cell a point is uniformly distributed and which can be used to derive upper bounds on the discrepancy with respect to various set systems, see, e.g., Chapter 3 in Matoušek’s book
[
Mat10
]
or
[
BC08
, Ch. 8]
. In equation (3) in the latter, an estimate on the number of intersecting cells was given, however with a hidden constant. Confer also Drmota and Tichy
[
DT97
, Sec. 2.1.2]
for another presentation. In
[
BCC+19
, Sec. 8]
, Brandolini, Chen, Colzani, Gigante and Travaglini extend this method to metric measure spaces, a key hypothesis being a bound on the measure of the neighbourhood of the boundary of the involved sets. Similar to Corollary 8.3 there, we shall derive such a bound for convex sets and give an explicit constant (see Corollary
6.20
). This discussion motivates a more detailed description of the proof technique of the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
; remaining proofs may be found in Appendix
D.2
.

Consider a tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by disjoint translations of the following set.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
L
be a
d
-dimensional lattice with basis
b_{1},\dots,b_{d}
. Then the fundamental domain or cell with respect to this basis is given by

\mathfrak{P}:=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}b_{i}\colon 0\leq\lambda_{i}<1\Big\}.

(6.10)

Its definition implies that for every
x\in L
the set
\mathfrak{P}_{x}:=\mathfrak{P}+x
, which is a shifted fundamental domain, contains no other point of
L
besides
x
. Also, the sets
\mathfrak{P}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{y}
are disjoint for
x\neq y
both in
L
. We have the disjoint union

\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup_{x\in L}\mathfrak{P}_{x}
.

The volume of
\mathfrak{P}
and thus any such cell is independent of the chosen basis and called the determinant
\det(L)
of
L
. A peculiar property of
L
being an integration lattice is that
\det(L)=1/n
, where
n=\#\mathcal{P}(L)
.

For an arbitrary convex set
K\subset[0,1)^{d}
, its signed discrepancy

D^{\pm}(\mathcal{P}(L),K):=\frac{\#(\mathcal{P}(L)\cap K)}{n}-\mathrm{vol}(K)

can be decomposed into the contributions made by each cell, that is,

D^{\pm}(\mathcal{P}(L),K)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in L}\Big(\mathbf{1}_{K}(x)-n\,\mathrm{vol}(K\cap\mathfrak{P}_{x})\Big).

(6.11)

If a cell is completely contained in
K
, then it does not contribute to the sum as it contains exactly one point of
\mathcal{P}(L)
and its intersection with
K
has exactly volume
1/n
. Also a cell not intersecting
K
does not contribute, since then the corresponding lattice point is not in
K
and the volume of
K
intersected with the cell is zero.

In order to estimate the right-hand side of (
6.11
), consider the union of cells inside of
K
and of those intersecting
K
, that is, define

U_{\rm in}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in L:\\
\mathfrak{P}_{x}\subset K\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{P}_{x}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{\rm out}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in L:\\
\mathfrak{P}_{x}\cap K\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{P}_{x}.

Then we have the inclusions
U_{\rm in}\subset K\subset U_{\rm out}
.

We get a lower bound by ignoring lattice points not belonging to
U_{\rm in}
and thus

D^{\pm}(\mathcal{P}(L),K)\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in L\cap U_{\rm in}}\big(\mathbf{1}_{K}(x)-n\,\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{P}_{x})\big)-\mathrm{vol}(K\setminus U_{\rm in})=\mathrm{vol}(K\setminus U_{\rm in}).

Note that
\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{P}_{x})=\frac{1}{n}
for all
x\in L
. Similarly, we get an upper bound by adding lattice points whose cells intersect
K
and thus

D^{\pm}(\mathcal{P}(L),K)\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in L\cap U_{\rm out}}\big(\mathbf{1}_{U_{\rm out}}(x)-n\,\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{P}_{x})\big)+\mathrm{vol}(U_{\rm out}\setminus K)=\mathrm{vol}(U_{\rm out}\setminus K).

Taken together, they imply for the absolute value of the signed discrepancy, the discrepancy of
K
, that

D(\mathcal{P}(L),K):=|D^{\pm}(\mathcal{P}(L),K)|\leq\max\{\mathrm{vol}(K\setminus U_{\rm in}),\mathrm{vol}(U_{\rm out}\setminus K)\}.

Therefore, the discrepancy is bounded in terms of the cells intersecting the boundary of
K
. In the following, we will obtain an upper bound of the volumes in the maximum by the diameter of the cell
\mathfrak{P}
and bound the latter in terms of the spectral test. Taking the supremum over all convex
K\subset[0,1)^{d}
then proves the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
.

The sets
K\setminus U_{\rm in}
and
U_{\rm out}\setminus K
are contained in
K_{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})}^{-}
and in
K_{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})}^{+}
, respectively, where, for any
\varrho\in\mathbb{R}
,

K_{\rho}^{-}:=\{x\in K\colon\mathrm{dist}(x,K^{C})\leq\rho\}\quad\text{and}\quad K_{\rho}^{+}:=\{x\in K^{C}\colon\mathrm{dist}(x,K)\leq\rho\}

are the inner and the outer
\rho
-neighborhood, respectively. Thus, we have the bound

D(\mathcal{P}(L),K)\leq\max\big\{\mathrm{vol}\big(K_{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})}^{-}\big),\mathrm{vol}\big(K_{\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})}^{+}\big)\big\},

which is more effective if the diameter of the cells is small.

We continue with the help of the following lemma.

Lemma 6.17
.

For any
\rho\in[0,1]
we have
\max\{\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+}),\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{-})\}\leq 2^{d+3}\rho
.

The proof of this bound on the volume of a neighborhood of a convex set will be provided in Appendix
D.2
. It relies on Steiner’s formula which expresses, for every
\rho>0
, the volume of the Minkowski sum
K+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\mathrm{dist}(x,K)<\rho\}
of
K
with a ball of radius
\rho>0
as a polynomial in
\rho
. For the proof of Lemma
6.17
one needs suitable bounds on its coefficients.

We shall apply Lemma
6.17
for
\rho=\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})
, where
\mathfrak{P}
is the fundamental domain of a suitable basis
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
of
L
consisting of short vectors such that the diameter is close to smallest possible. We will argue later why we can assume that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq 1
.

To find such a basis of short vectors, we use the LLL-lattice basis reduction algorithm, which was originally derived by Lenstra, Lenstra and Lovász in
[
LLL82
]
to factorize polynomials but then received widespread attention in many other fields. For more information we refer to Galbraith
[
Gal12
, Ch. 17]
. The LLL-algorithm computes an LLL-reduced basis given as follows.

Definition 6.18
.

A basis
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
of a lattice
L
is called LLL-reduced (with parameter
\delta=3/4
) if

1.

|\mu_{i,j}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
for
1\leq j\leq i\leq d
,

2.

\|b_{i}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\mu_{i,i-1}^{2})\|b_{i}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}
for
2\leq i\leq d
,

where

b_{1}^{*}=b_{1},\quad b_{i}^{*}=b_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\mu_{i,j}b_{j}^{*}\text{ for }i=2,\dots,d\quad\text{with }\mu_{i,j}:=\langle b_{i},b_{j}^{*}\rangle/\|b_{j}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

is the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
.

Then we have the following lemma for the corresponding fundamental cell
\mathfrak{P}
via Definition
6.16
.

Lemma 6.19
.

The diameter of a fundamental domain
\mathfrak{P}
of a
d
-dimensional lattice
L
with respect to an LLL-reduced basis
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
as in Definition
6.18
is bounded by

\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq d\,2^{d-1}\sigma(L)

Inserting this into Lemma
6.17
completes the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
. It remains to justify why it is sufficient to consider the case
\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq 1
. This is because in any case the isotropic discrepancy is at most one and thus we can assume that
\sigma(L)\leq d^{-1}\,2^{-2(d+1)}
, since else the upper bound is trivial. By Lemma
6.19
this translates to

\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq d\,2^{d-1}\sigma(L)\leq 2^{-d-3}<1.

\hfill\square

We note the following corollary of Lemma
6.17
giving a bound on the neighbourhood of the boundary of a convex set.

Corollary 6.20
.

Let
K\subset[0,1)^{d}
be non-empty and convex. Then, for any
\rho\in[0,1]
we have

\mathrm{vol}\big(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial K)\leq\rho\}\big)\leq 2^{d+4}\rho.

Remark 6.21
.

As explained in
[
SP21
]
, the proof of
[
PS20
, Thm. 2]
contained an erroneous version of Lemma
6.17
in
[
PS20
, page 5, lines 19-21]
, where the argument used in the proof of
[
ABD12
, Lem. 17]
was incorrectly extended to higher dimensions, although the asymptotic result itself remains valid with adjusted constants in
d
.

Remark 6.22
.

The dependence of the upper bound of Lemma
6.17
, and thus Corollary
6.20
, on the dimension
d
can be improved to be sub-exponential in
d
. Essentially, it is of order of magnitude
{\rm e}^{cd^{2/3}}
for
c=\frac{3}{2}(2\pi)^{1/3}
. A proof is given in Appendix
D.2
.

6.5
Open questions

Question 6.1
.

The first question arises canonically.

Can the unit cube be replaced by a more general set in Theorem
6.5
?

Thinking of the geometry behind, it appears that the cube
[0,1)^{d}
can be replaced by any convex set. It would be interesting how
[
KR20
, Thm. 1.1]
may be extended to more general convex sets. This is somewhat related to the famous hyperplane conjecture, see, e.g., the book of Brazitikos, Giannopoulos, Valettas and Vritsiou
[
BGVV14
]
.

Question 6.2
.

The second question is about the constants involved.

What is the correct dependence on
d
in Theorem
6.5
?

The exponential upper bound is essentially due to volume arguments involved and we do not know if it can be improved or other methods have to be employed.

Question 6.3
.

In the introduction to this chapter we related the isotropic discrepancy to a worst-case error in a function space of functions of bounded
\mathcal{K}
-variation denoted by
V(\mathcal{K})
and into which
C^{2}((0,1)^{d})
is embedded. This is already a partial answer to the following question.

Can the function space
(V(\mathcal{K}),\|\cdot\|_{V(\mathcal{K})})
be characterized in terms of smoothness?

Note that
V(\mathcal{K})
contains indicator functions of convex sets. In general, relating discrepancies to worst-case errors of cubature rules is of much interest and may lead to a better understanding of the best possible behaviour of
n
-point sets. For
L_{2}
-averaged discrepancy there is a connection to reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, see, e.g.,
[
NW10
, Ch. 9]
. On the sphere, this becomes a beautiful connection to a sum of distances, which is known from
[
Sto73
]
as Stolarsky’s invariance principle, see also Brauchart and Dick
[
BD13
]
.

Appendix A
Additional material for Chapter 3

A.1
Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators

In what follows, we collect useful knowledge about the Gelfand numbers of diagonal operators. At the end of this section we will state and prove Corollary
A.4
from which the expression in (
3.9
) on the rate of polynomial decay of the minimal radius for an
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid with polynomial semiaxes is deduced.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
\sigma=(\sigma_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
. To this sequence we associate the diagonal operator

D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q},\quad x=(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto(\sigma_{j}x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}},

which, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, can be considered as an operator from
\ell_{p}^{m}
to
\ell_{q}^{m}
. Recall that

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)=c_{n+1}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{2}^{m})\quad\text{for all }0\leq n<m,

(A.1)

and an analogous result holds if 2 is replaced by
q
and we measure the radius in
\|\cdot\|_{q}
. Although we will need only the case
q=2
, we state the following result in a more general form.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
0<q\leq p\leq\infty
and
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
. Then, for any
1\leq n\leq m
, we have

c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m})=\Big(\sum_{j=n}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{r}\Big)^{1/r},

where
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}
if
q<p
and
r=\infty
if
q=p
.

A proof can be found in
[
Pie80
, Sec. 11.11]
for the case
q\geq 1
but is in fact also valid if
q<1
. In addition to Proposition
A.1
, we have for all
0<p,q\leq\infty
that

c_{1}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m})=\|D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m}\|=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{r}\Big)^{1/r},

where
\frac{1}{r}=(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}
. This shows that
\|\sigma\|_{r}<\infty
is necessary to ensure that the operators
D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m},m\in\mathbb{N},
are uniformly bounded and explains the condition
\lambda>(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}
in Section
3.3
.

All of the above extends to the infinite-dimensional case in a canonical way and we now state an equivalence between the Lorentz quasi-norm of an infinite sequence
\sigma
and the Gelfand numbers of the corresponding diagonal operators. It is taken from Buchmann
[
Buc99
]
and one implication can be extracted from Linde
[
Lin85
, Thm. 5]
. The technique behind makes use of well-known results on the Gelfand numbers of the identity operator from
\ell_{p}
to
\ell_{q}
by, to name a few, Garnaev and Gluskin
[
GG84
]
, Gluskin
[
Glu81
,
Glu84
]
, Ismagilov
[
Ism74
]
and Kashin
[
Kas77
]
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
r>0
with
\frac{1}{r}>(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}
as well as
0<t\leq\infty
. Then

\sigma\in\ell_{r,t}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\big(c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell_{u,t},

where

1.

if
1\leq q\leq p\leq\infty
, then
\frac{1}{u}=\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}
,

2.

if
1\leq p<q\leq 2
, then
\frac{1}{u}=\begin{cases}\frac{p^{*}}{2r}&\text{if }\frac{1}{r}<\frac{1}{p^{*}}\frac{1/p-1/q}{1/p-1/2},\\
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}&\text{if }\frac{1}{r}>\frac{1}{p^{*}}\frac{1/p-1/q}{1/p-1/2},\end{cases}

3.

if
1\leq p<2<q\leq\infty
, then
\frac{1}{u}=\begin{cases}\frac{p^{*}}{2r}&\text{if }\frac{1}{r}<\frac{1}{p^{*}},\\
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}&\text{if }\frac{1}{r}>\frac{1}{p^{*}},\end{cases}

4.

if
2\leq p<q\leq\infty
, then
\frac{1}{u}=\frac{1}{r}
.

By means of equality (
A.1
), the above Propositions
A.1
and
A.2
apply to the minimal radius for
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids. Note that in the latter proposition some cases are missing; for example, if
q=2
, there is a gap for
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p^{*}}
. In this case, we can deduce
the following corollary from Theorem
3.4
.

Corollary A.3
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{2})\lesssim\begin{cases}n^{-1/2}\sup\limits_{k\leq j\leq\infty}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}&\text{if }p=1,\\
\sqrt{p^{*}}n^{-1/2}\Big(\sum\limits_{j=k}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}}&\text{if }1<p\leq\infty,\end{cases}

where
k\asymp\frac{n}{p^{*}}
for
p>1
, while
k\asymp n
for
p=1
. In particular,
c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{2})\in\ell_{2,\infty}
if
1<p\leq\infty
and
\sigma\in\ell_{p^{*}}
.

Proof.

We use (
A.1
) to state Theorem
3.4
for Gelfand numbers. Then, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
D_{\sigma}^{m}
be the restriction of the operator
D_{\sigma}
to the first
m
coordinates. This gives

c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m})=c_{n}(D_{\sigma}^{m}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q}),

and further, by continuity and Hölder’s inequality,

\big|c_{n}(D_{\sigma}^{m}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q})-c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q})\big|\leq\Big(\sum_{j=m+1}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}^{s}\Big)^{1/s},

with
\frac{1}{s}=(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}
. Letting
m\to\infty
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
completes the proof.
∎

Let us note that bounding the Gelfand numbers of operators into
\ell_{2}
via
M^{*}
-estimates has been done before, e.g., in
[
PT86
]
.

As a corollary to Proposition
A.2
we obtain the following result on the order of decay of the minimal radius of
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoids with polynomially decaying semiaxes. For
p\geq 2
it also follows from Proposition
A.1
which shows that
\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\lesssim n^{-\lambda+1/2-1/p}
for all
m>n
with a matching lower bound for
m
, say, larger than
2n
.

Corollary A.4
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
. If
\sigma_{j}=j^{-\lambda}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, for some
\lambda>(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}
, then

\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big)=\begin{cases}\lambda\cdot\frac{p^{*}}{2}&\text{if }1\leq p<2\text{ and }\lambda<\frac{1}{p^{*}},\\
\lambda+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We only prove the first case since the other case does not require additional ideas. To show that
\mathrm{decay}\big(\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},n)\big)\geq\lambda p^{*}/2
, it is sufficient by (
A.1
) to find
C>0
such that, for all
\varrho<\lambda p^{*}/2
large enough,

c_{n,m}:=c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}^{m}\to\ell_{q}^{m})\leq Cn^{-\varrho}\quad\text{for all }m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }1\leq n\leq m.

(A.2)

This is satisfied if the sequence of Gelfand numbers
c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q})\geq c_{n,m}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) belongs to
\ell_{u,\infty}
with
u=1/\varrho
. By Proposition
A.2
, this holds if
\sigma\in\ell_{r,\infty}
with a certain
r>1/\lambda
, which is true by assumption.

For the other inequality we assume that (
A.2
) holds for some
\varrho>\lambda p^{*}/2
. Choosing
m
large enough compared to
n
, see the proof of Corollary
A.3
, we deduce from (
A.2
) that for some
\varrho>\lambda p^{*}/2
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

c_{n}:=c_{n}(D_{\sigma}\colon\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q})\leq 2Cn^{-\varrho}.

(A.3)

For every
r<1/\lambda
and
0<t<\infty
, it follows from
\sigma\not\in\ell_{r,t}
and Proposition
A.2
that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}c_{n}^{t}n^{tp^{*}/2r}=\infty,

which implies
c_{n}n^{p^{*}/2r}\log^{2/t}n\to\infty
, contradicting (
A.3
).
∎

A.2
Proofs for the convex case

In this section we complete the proof ideas for Sections
3.4
and
3.5
, in particular the upper bound of Theorem
3.4
and the lower bound of Proposition
3.17
.

We start with the proof of Proposition
3.15
on the mean width of a rounded
\ell_{p}
-ellipsoid which is then used to derive Theorem
3.4
. The following elementary estimate for
\ell_{q}
-norms of structured Gaussian random vectors will be useful.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq q<\infty
. If
b=(b_{j})_{j=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
X=(b_{j}g_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
with independent standard Gaussian random variables
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}
, then

\gamma_{1}\|b\|_{q}\leq\mathbb{E}\|X\|_{q}\leq\gamma_{q}\|b\|_{q},\quad\text{where }\quad\gamma_{q}:=\big(\mathbb{E}|g_{1}|^{q}\big)^{1/q}\asymp\sqrt{q}.

Further,

\mathbb{E}\|X\|_{\infty}\asymp\sup_{1\leq j\leq k}b_{j}^{*}\sqrt{\log(j)+1},

where
(b_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{k}
is the non-increasing rearrangement of
(|b_{j}|)_{j=1}^{k}
(see Definition
3.6
).

Proof.

For
1\leq q<\infty
the upper bound follows from Jensen’s inequality and the lower bound follows from
\|\mathbb{E}X^{\prime}\|_{q}\leq\mathbb{E}\|X^{\prime}\|_{q}=\mathbb{E}\|X\|_{q}
, where
X^{\prime}=(|b_{j}g_{j}|)_{j=1}^{k}
. The asymptotics for
q=\infty
are taken from van Handel
[
van17
, Lem. 2.3 and 2.4]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.15
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq k<m
and
\varrho>0
. We shall use the representation (
3.13
), which translates to

M^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m})=\frac{1}{c_{m}}\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}t_{j}g_{j},

where
c_{m}\asymp\sqrt{m}
. In order to bound the supremum, fix some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}
. Then it follows from Hölder’s inequality that, for every
0\leq k<m
,

\langle x,y\rangle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}y_{j}|+\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}|x_{j}y_{j}|\leq\varrho\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}+\Big(\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}|x_{j}|^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}},

where the first, and consequently the third, sum is empty if
k=0
.
Combining this estimate with Lemma
A.5
, we obtain that if
p>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{y\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{j}g_{j}\leq\varrho\,a_{k}+\gamma_{p^{*}}\Big(\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}},

where we used that
a_{k}=\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}g_{i}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
, see (
3.14
). By the previously stated asympotics for
a_{k}
and for
\gamma_{p^{*}}
in Lemma
A.5
, we obtain the statement for
p>1
.

If
p=1
, then we deduce from Lemma
A.5
that, for some suitable
C>0
,

\mathbb{E}\,\sup_{y\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{j}g_{j}\leq\varrho\sqrt{k}+C\sup_{k+1\leq j\leq m}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\log(j)+1}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

For any
\varrho>0
, it follows from Gordon’s
M^{*}
-estimate (Proposition
3.14
) applied to the convex body
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}
that, with probability as claimed, a random subspace
E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}
of codimension
n
satisfies

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}\cap E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}}}\|x\|_{2}\leq 2\frac{a_{m}}{a_{n}}M^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}).

We start with the case
p>1
. Inserting the bound obtained in Proposition
3.15
, we obtain a constant
C>0
such that, for any
0\leq k<m
and
1\leq n<m
,

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\frac{\sqrt{p^{*}}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\varrho\sqrt{k}+\Big(\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}}\Big).

In accordance with relation (
3.15
), we seek to choose
\varrho
suitably small and
k
large such that this radius is bounded by
\varrho
.

First, let
\frac{n}{p^{*}}>4C^{2}
. Setting
\varrho:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\Big(\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}}
with
k:=c\frac{n}{p^{*}}
, where the constant
c>0
is chosen (sufficiently small) such that
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq k<m
and

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\cap\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})<\varrho

and so in particular that

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})<\varrho

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n<m
. Noting that

\varrho\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{p^{*}}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}},

proves the result for
p>1
in this case. If
\frac{n}{p^{*}}\leq 4C^{2}
, then let
k=0
and let
\varrho>0
be large enough such that
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}\subset\varrho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{m}
and thus

\mathrm{rad}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},E_{n}^{\mathrm{ran}})\leq C\frac{\sqrt{p^{*}}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{p^{*}}\Big)^{1/p^{*}}.

In both cases,
k+1\asymp\frac{n}{p^{*}}
. The proof for
p=1
is carried out analogously.
∎

Next, we prove the general version of the lower bound in Proposition
3.11
.

Proof of Proposition
3.17
.

By Lemma
3.16
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
, we find
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with

x_{1}^{2}\geq 1-\frac{n}{\varepsilon m},\quad\|x\|_{2}=1,\quad\text{and}\quad G_{n,m}x=0.

We estimate

\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{|x_{j}|^{p}}{\sigma_{j}^{p}}\Big)^{1/p}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}}+\Big(\sum_{j=2}^{m}\frac{|x_{j}|^{p}}{\sigma_{j}^{p}}\Big)^{1/p}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}}+\frac{1}{\sigma_{m}}\Big(\sum_{j=2}^{m}|x_{j}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

and by means of Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

\frac{1}{\sigma_{m}}\Big(\sum_{j=2}^{m}|x_{j}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\leq\frac{m^{1/p-1/2}}{\sigma_{m}}\Big(\sum_{j=2}^{m}x_{j}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}=\frac{m^{1/p-1/2}}{\sigma_{m}}\big(1-x_{1}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

Since
1-x_{1}^{2}\leq\frac{n}{\varepsilon m}
, we have

\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{|x_{j}|^{p}}{\sigma_{j}^{p}}\Big)^{1/p}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}}+1

if
n\leq\varepsilon m^{2p^{*}}\sigma_{m}^{2}
. In this case, we can normalize such that
\tilde{x}:=x/(1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}})
satisfies

\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},\quad G_{n,m}\tilde{x}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\|\tilde{x}\|_{2}=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{1+\sigma_{1}},

which completes the proof.
∎

A.3
Proofs for the non-convex case

We continue with the results leading to the proof of Theorem
3.9
. It is a special case of Theorem
3.24
from Section
3.6
. The proof relies on Lemma
3.23
which is proven first.

Proof of Lemma
3.23
.

Approximating every
x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
by the
s
-sparse vector consisting of its
s
largest entries we obtain

\sigma_{s}(x)_{q}\leq\Big(\sum_{j=s+1}^{m}(x_{j}^{*})^{q}\Big)^{1/q},

where
(x_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{m}
is the non-increasing rearrangement as in Definition
3.6
. If
q=\infty
, then take the maximum norm instead and analogously for
p
in the following. Recall that in this thesis we set
a/\infty=0
for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

Using that
x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
, we can write

1\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{|x_{j}|^{p}}{\sigma_{j}^{p}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{(x_{j}^{*})^{p}}{\sigma_{\pi(j)}^{p}}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{k}^{*})^{p}}{\sigma_{\pi(j)}^{p}},

for any
1\leq k\leq m
, where
\pi:\{1,\ldots,m\}\to\{1,\ldots,m\}
is a suitable permutation. Thus,

(x_{k}^{*})^{p}\leq\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\sigma_{\pi(j)}^{p}}\Big)^{-1}\leq\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}^{p}}\Big)^{-1}\asymp k^{-\lambda p-1},

where the notation
\asymp
(and
\lesssim
below) means in this proof that the implicit constants do not depend on
k,s
or
m\geq 2s
but may depend on
p,q
and
\lambda
. Inserting this bound above yields

\sigma_{s}(x)_{q}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{k=s+1}^{m}k^{-\lambda q-q/p}\Big)^{1/q}\asymp s^{-\lambda+1/q-1/p}

as
\lambda>(1/q-1/p)_{+}
.

The lower bound is achieved by a vector on the boundary of
\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
having its support on the first
2s
coordinates which are all equal to

\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{2s}\sigma_{i}^{-p}\Big)^{-1/p}\asymp s^{-\lambda-1/p}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.24
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq n<m
,
N_{n}:=n^{-1/2}G_{n,m}
and set
r=\min\{1,q\}
. By Proposition
2.9
, see also Remark
3.5
,

\mathrm{rad}_{q}(\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m},\ker G_{n,m})\leq 2^{(1/q-1)_{+}}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}}\|x-\Delta_{r}(N_{n}x)\|_{q},

(A.4)

where we specified
\varphi(y)=\Delta_{r}(n^{-1/2}y)=
arg min
\|z\|_{r}
subject to
N_{n}z=n^{-1/2}y
for
y=G_{n,m}x
.

We follow the proof of
[
FPRU10
, Thm. 3.2]
in order to obtain an upper bound. To this end, let
D\in(0,\infty)
be large enough such that

D/2>{\rm e}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{D/2}{1+\log(D/2)}>C_{1},

where
C_{1}\in(0,\infty)
is the constant from Proposition
3.22
with
\delta=1/3
. Also choose
s\geq 1
as the largest integer smaller than
