n/D\log({\rm e}m/n)
such that

\frac{n}{2D\log({\rm e}m/n)}\leq s<\frac{n}{D\log({\rm e}m/n)}.

Then, if
n\geq D\log({\rm e}m/n)
it holds that
n>C_{1}(2s)\log({\rm e}m/2s)
. This can be seen as follows. Put
t=2s
such that
t/n<2/D<1/{\rm e}
. As the function
x\mapsto x\log(x)
is decreasing on
[0,1/{\rm e}]
, we obtain

n\geq\frac{D}{2}t\log({\rm e}m/n)=\frac{D}{2}t\log({\rm e}m/t)+\frac{D}{2}n(t/n)\log({\rm e}t/n)>\frac{D}{2}t\log({\rm e}m/t)-n\log(D/2)

and therefore

n\geq\frac{D/2}{1+\log(D/2)}t\log({\rm e}m/t)>C_{1}\,t\log({\rm e}m/t).

Then, by Proposition
3.22
the matrix
N_{n}
satisfies
\delta_{2s}(N_{n})\leq\delta
with probability at least
1-2\exp(-C_{2}n)
. Assume from now on that
N_{n}
is replaced by a realization satisfying this.

Let
x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}
be arbitrary. Then
v:=x-\Delta_{r}(N_{n}x)
belongs to the kernel and we will apply the restricted isometry property to a decomposition of
v
in order to bound
\|v\|_{q}
. Decompose the set
\{1,\dots,m\}
into disjoint sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{M}
which are of size
s
, except for possibly
S_{M}
, such that
|v_{i}|\geq|v_{j}|
for all
i\in S_{k-1}
and
j\in S_{k}
, where
k\geq 2
. Write
v=\sum_{k=1}^{M}v_{S_{k}}
, where
v_{S_{k}}
is a vector whose coordinates coincide with
v_{i}
if
i\in S_{k}
and with zero else. Then
r\leq 2
and Hölder’s inequality imply

\|v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\leq s^{1/2-1/r}\|v_{S_{k-1}}\|_{r},\quad k=2,\dots,M,

(A.5)

which will be used a few lines below. Together with Hölder’s inequality we deduce from the lower bound in the definition (
3.17
) of the restricted isometry property for the
s
-sparse (and also
2s
-sparse) vector
v_{S_{k}}
that

\|v\|_{q}^{r}\leq\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{M}v_{S_{k}}\Big\|_{q}^{r}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{M}\|v_{S_{k}}\|_{q}^{r}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{M}(s^{1/q-1/2}\|v_{S_{k}}\|_{2})^{r}\leq\Big(\frac{s^{1/q-1/2}}{\sqrt{1-\delta}}\Big)^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\|N_{n}v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}^{r}.

Since
v\in\ker N_{n}
, it holds that
N_{n}v_{S_{1}}=-\sum_{k=2}^{M}N_{n}v_{S_{k}}
and further, since
r\leq 2
,

\|N_{n}v_{S_{1}}\|_{2}^{r}=\Big\|\sum_{k=2}^{M}N_{n}v_{S_{k}}\Big\|_{2}^{r}=\Big(\sum_{k=2}^{M}\|N_{n}v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}^{2}\Big)^{r/2}\leq\sum_{k=2}^{M}\|N_{n}v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}^{r}.

Thus, we can continue our estimate with

\|v\|_{q}^{r}\leq 2\Big(\frac{s^{1/q-1/2}}{\sqrt{1-\delta}}\Big)^{r}\sum_{k=2}^{M}\|N_{n}v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}^{r}.

Applying now the upper bound in the restricted isometry property of (
3.17
) yields that

\|v\|_{q}^{r}\leq 2\Big(\frac{\sqrt{1+\delta}}{\sqrt{1-\delta}}s^{1/q-1/2}\Big)^{r}\sum_{k=2}^{M}\|v_{S_{k}}\|_{2}^{r}.

Using the inequality (
A.5
) and that
\delta=1/3
yields

\|v\|_{q}\leq 2^{1/r+1/2}s^{1/q-1/r}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{M}\|v_{S_{k}}\|_{r}^{r}\Big)^{1/r}=2^{1/r+1/2}s^{1/q-1/r}\|v\|_{r}

and inserting now for
s
yields for some constant
C>0
depending on
r
and
C_{1}
that

\|v\|_{q}\leq C\,\Big(\frac{\log({\rm e}m/n)}{n}\Big)^{1/r-1/q}\|v\|_{r}.

After inserting for
v=x-\Delta_{r}(N_{n}x)
, we can apply Proposition
3.21
to get a bound in terms of the error of best
s
-term approximation of
x
. Then taking the supremum shows that there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
p,q
and
C_{1}
, such that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}}\|x-\Delta_{r}(N_{n}x)\|_{q}\leq C\,\Big(\frac{\log({\rm e}m/n)}{n}\Big)^{1/r-1/q}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}_{p,\sigma}^{m}}\sigma_{s}(x)_{r}.

Until now we considered a specific realization of
N_{n}
and therefore this holds with probability at least
1-2\exp(-C_{2}n)
for the random matrix
N_{n}
.

With Lemma
3.23
the proof of Theorem
3.24
is complete if we can show that
s\leq m/2
. Indeed, since the function
n\mapsto n/\log({\rm e}m/n)
is increasing for
1\leq n\leq m
, we have
s\leq m/D<m/2{\rm e}
.
∎

Appendix B
Additional material for Chapter 4

We provide the proofs for the statements claimed in Chapter
4
and in particular follow the ideas given in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
to complete the proof of Theorem
4.2
and its extension, Theorem
4.10
.

First, in Section
B.1
, we provide the proofs for the asymptotics of the optimal distortion (
4.7
) and for Example
4.4
.

In Section
B.2
we continue with technical preparations regarding the approximation of Sobolev functions by polynomials (Lemma
B.1
) and their extension from bounded Lipschitz domains to the whole space (Lemma
B.3
). We also give a proof of the known fact that a bounded convex domain has Lipschitz boundary (Lemma
B.4
).

Section
B.3
contains the proof of Theorem
4.2
for the case
q\geq p
. As a preparation for the upper bound, Lemma
4.14
is deduced from
[
Wen05
, Thm. 4.7]
and the fact that
the
interior cone condition is preserved by the closure (Lemma
4.17
) is proven. The lower bound relies on finding a suitable large hole which is provided by Lemma
B.6
.

In Section
B.4
we give the proof of Theorem
4.2
for the case
q<p
. For this, we commence by proving the local cone condition (Lemma
4.18
) and the error bound of moving least squares applied to a cube (Lemma
4.19
). Afterwards, Lemmas
B.7
and
B.8
justify the Definition
4.21
of the smallest good cube. Then the existence of an efficient covering (Proposition
4.22
) and of a hole of proportional size in every smallest good cube (Lemma
4.25
) are proven. With these preparations at hand the proof of the upper and lower bound in the case
q<p
is given.

Finally, in Section
B.5
we prove the extension, Theorem
4.10
, of our results to more general function spaces. There, we shall also provide a definition of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.

In this appendix we will make use of the asymptotic notations
\lesssim,\gtrsim,\asymp
as given in Definition
2.13
. Additionally, whenever an arbitrary but fixed function
f
occurs, the implicit constants will not depend on it.

B.1
Proofs for Section 4.2

Let us prove the asymptotics for the optimal points on a bounded set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof of (
4.7
).

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since

\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\infty}(\Omega)}\geq\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)^{1/\gamma}\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\text{ for every }0<\gamma<\infty,

(B.1)

it suffices to prove the upper bound for
\gamma=\infty
and the lower bound for
0<\gamma<\infty
.

For the upper bound we construct an
\varepsilon
-net, with
\varepsilon>0
to be fixed later, by iteratively selecting
y_{1}\in\Omega
,
y_{2}\in\Omega\setminus B(y_{1},\varepsilon)
and
y_{k+1}\in\Omega
not contained in
\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}B(y_{j},\varepsilon)
until the process stops after finitely many steps. Then we are left with
N
balls
B(y_{1},\varepsilon),\dots,B(y_{N},\varepsilon)
which cover
\Omega
and the balls
B(y_{1},\varepsilon/2),\dots,B(y_{N},\varepsilon/2)
are disjoint.

If
\varepsilon<1
, we have the volume estimate

\mathrm{vol}\big(\Omega+B(0,1/2)\big)\geq\mathrm{vol}\Big(\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B(y_{j},\varepsilon/2)\Big)=N(\varepsilon/2)^{d}\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}).

We refer to Definition
D.1
in Appendix
D.2
for the sum of sets.

If we choose, for sufficiently large
n
,

\varepsilon=2\,\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d})^{-1/d}\mathrm{vol}\big(\Omega+B(0,1/2)\big)^{1/d}n^{-1/d},

it follows that
N\leq n
and therefore
P_{N}=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{N}\}
has at most
n
points and every point of
\Omega
is not more than
\varepsilon
from the nearest point of
P_{N}
. This proves the upper bound. For completeness, adding arbitrary points ensures that
\#P_{N}=n
.

For the proof of the lower bound, let
0<\gamma<\infty
and fix

\delta=\big(\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)/2\,\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d})\big)^{1/d}n^{-1/d}\quad\text{ such that }\quad n\,\mathrm{vol}\big(B(0,\delta)\big)=\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)/2.

Then for any
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the set

\Omega_{P_{n}}:=\Omega\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B(x_{i},\delta)

has volume at least
\mathrm{vol}(\Omega)/2
and
\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}\geq\delta^{\gamma}
for each
x\in\Omega_{P_{n}}
. Integrating over
\Omega_{P_{n}}
yields

\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}^{\gamma}\geq\int_{\Omega_{P_{n}}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\gtrsim n^{-\gamma/d},

concluding the proof.
∎

Next, we provide the proof for the claimed necessary condition in Example
4.4
for the optimality of a sequence of point sets.

Proof of condition (
4.8
).

Assume that for each
n
the ball
B_{n}:=B(y_{n},r_{n})
with
y_{n}\in\Omega
and
r_{n}>0
does not contain any points of
P_{n}
and that the points of
P_{n}
cover
\Omega\setminus B_{n}
nicely, i.e., the covering radius of
P_{n}
in
\Omega\setminus B_{n}
is of order
n^{-1/d}
. Then we have

\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}\penalty\ {\rm d}x\,\lesssim\,n^{-\gamma/d}+r_{n}^{\gamma+d}\quad\text{for all }0<\gamma<\infty.

Rearranging, this means that if
r_{n}\lesssim n^{-1/d+1/(\gamma+d)}
, then
\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\lesssim n^{-1/d}
for the sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

To show that this condition is also necessary, note that
\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})\geq r_{n}/2
for every
x\in\Omega\cap B(y_{n},r_{n}/2)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The interior cone condition satisfied by
\Omega
implies that there is a constant
c>0
depending only on
\Omega
such that
\mathrm{vol}\big(\Omega\cap B(y_{n},r_{n}/2)\big)\geq c\,r_{n}
, and thus

\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}\penalty\ {\rm d}x\,\gtrsim\,r_{n}^{\gamma+d}.

Letting
1\leq q<p\leq\infty
and
\gamma
as above, we obtain from Corollary
4.3
that
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is asymptotically optimal for
L_{q}
-approximation on
W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)
if and only if condition (
4.8
), that is,

r_{n}\lesssim n^{-1/d+1/(\gamma+d)}

is satisfied.
∎

B.2
Approximation and extension of Sobolev functions

In the following, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a domain as well as
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
. Recall from Definition
4.1
that the Sobolev norm of a function
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
is given by

\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}=\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq s}\|D^{\alpha}f\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}\Big)^{1/p},

where the sum is over all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with sum of entries
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}
at most
s
. The seminorm

|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}:=\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|=s}\|D^{\alpha}f\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

(B.2)

is zero on polynomials of degree at most
s-1
and will be used to bound the error by polynomial approximation.

Variants of the following result about optimal polynomial approximation on cubes are well known, see, e.g., the references provided in
[
Nov88
, Sec. 1.3.12]
. The proof given here is not adapted from
[
KS20
, Lem. 9]
, where we stated it originally, but from the similar
[
KNS22
, Lem. 4]
.

Lemma B.1
.

For any
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}

as in (
4.1
)
, there is a constant
c_{\rm poly}>0
such that the following holds. For any
0<\varrho\leq 1
, any cube
Q(x,\varrho)
with center
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and radius
\varrho
, and any
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there is a polynomial
\pi
of degree at most
s
such that

\sup_{x\in Q(x,\varrho)}|(f-\pi)(x)|\leq c_{\rm poly}\,\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q(x,\varrho))}.

Proof.

We use an affine map
T
from
Q=Q(x,\varrho)
to the reference cube
Q_{0}=Q(0,1)
with
T(y)=\varrho^{-1}(y-x)
for every
y\in Q
. Applied to the function
f\circ T^{-1}\colon Q_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
, the generalized Poincaré inequality from
[
Maz85
, 1.1.11]
implies that there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
independent of
f
and
Q
such that we find a polynomial
\pi
of degree at most
s-1
with

\|f\circ T^{-1}-\pi\|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{0})}\,\leq\,c_{1}\,|f\circ T^{-1}|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{0})}.

(B.3)

A change of variables, together with the chain rule and integration by substitution, gives another constant
c_{2}>0
such that

|f\circ T^{-1}|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{0})}\,\leq\,c_{2}\,\varrho^{s-d/p}\,|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q)}.

(B.4)

The continuous embedding of
W^{s}_{p}(Q_{0})
into
C_{b}(Q_{0})
, see the introduction to Chapter
4
, yields
c_{3}>0
with

\|f-\pi^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(Q)}\,=\,\|f\circ T^{-1}-\pi\|_{L_{\infty}(Q_{0})}\,\leq\,c_{3}\,\|f\circ T^{-1}-\pi\|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{0})},

where
\pi^{\prime}=\pi\circ T
is again a polynomial of degree at most
s-1
. Combining this with the estimates (
B.3
) and (
B.4
) yields

\|f-\pi^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(Q)}\,\leq\,c_{\rm poly}\,\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q)},

where
c_{\rm poly}:=c_{1}\,c_{2}\,c_{3}
is independent of
f
and
Q
. The continuity of
f-\pi^{\prime}
concludes the proof.
∎

The reason that Lemma
B.1
is stated for functions defined on the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is that we can extend the functions from
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
to
W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, when
\Omega
is a bounded convex domain, which is the content of well-known Sobolev extension theorems. See, e.g., the historical comments in
[
Maz85
, Sec. 1.1.19]
. To state a version suitable for our needs, we introduce the notion of a bounded Lipschitz domain, which is
a popular
regularity condition under which Sobolev embedding and extension theorems hold. The following definition was used in
[
KS20
]
and taken from
[
NT06
]
.

Definition B.2
.

A bounded Lipschitz domain
\Omega
is a domain with the following property. There are points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\in\partial\Omega
on the boundary and radii
r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}>0
such that
\partial\Omega
is covered by the balls
B(x_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,B(x_{N},r_{N})
and

\Omega\cap B(x_{i},r_{i})=\Omega_{i}\cap B(x_{i},r_{i}),\quad i=1,\ldots,N,

where
\Omega_{i}
is a suitable rotation of a special Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here, a special Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
, is the collection of all points
x=(x^{\prime},x_{d})
with
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
such that

h(x^{\prime})<x_{d}<\infty,

where
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
is some Lipschitz function, i.e., there exists a constant
C>0
with
|h(x^{\prime})-h(y^{\prime})|\leq C\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|_{2}
for all
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.

The following extension theorem is taken from Stein
[
Ste71
, Sec. VI.3]
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain,
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is a bounded linear operator
{\rm ext}\colon W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\to W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
{\rm ext}(f)|_{\Omega}=f
for all
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
.

Note that Stein uses the notion of a minimally smooth domain which, however, entails bounded Lipschitz domains. Further, the following lemma shows that a bounded convex domain is a bounded Lipschitz domain. As we could not find a suitable proof of this intuitive fact, we provide one ourselves. A similar result may be found in Dekel and Leviatan
[
DL04
, Lem. 2.3]
.

Lemma B.4
.

Every bounded convex domain is a bounded Lipschitz domain.

Proof.

If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded convex domain, we find a ball
B(x_{0},r)\subset\Omega
. As
\partial\Omega
is compact, there are points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
such that the balls
B(x_{i},r/2)
,
i\leq N
, cover
\partial\Omega
. For all
i\leq N
we show that

\Omega\cap B(x_{i},r/2)=\Omega_{i}\cap B(x_{i},r/2),

(B.5)

where
\Omega_{i}
is a rotation of a special Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Applying a suitable rotation (and translation), we may assume that
x_{0}=0
and that
x_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,a)
for some
a\leq-r
. Consider the open ball
B^{\prime}=B^{d-1}(0,r)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. For
x^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
, we define the set
A(x^{\prime})=\{x_{d}\in\mathbb{R}\colon(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in\Omega\}
. Since
\Omega
is convex and open,
A(x^{\prime})
is an open interval. Moreover,
A(x^{\prime})
is non-empty since
0\in A(x^{\prime})
. We define
h(x^{\prime})
to be the infimum of
A(x^{\prime})
. The convexity of
\Omega
implies that the function
h\colon B^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex. Since every convex function on a convex domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
is Lipschitz on every compact subset of the domain, see
[
Uni72
]
, the function
h
is Lipschitz on the
d-1
-dimensional ball
B^{*}=B^{d-1}(0,r/2)
. This Lipschitz continuity carries over to the whole
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
if we set
h(\lambda x^{\prime})=h(x^{\prime})
for all
x^{\prime}\in\partial B^{*}
and
\lambda\geq 1
(thereby redefining
h
on
B^{\prime}\setminus B^{*}
). It remains to note that for every
x=(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in B(x_{i},r/2)
it holds that

x\in\Omega\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x_{d}\in A(x^{\prime})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x_{d}>h(x^{\prime}),

proving (
B.5
) for the special Lipschitz domain
\Omega_{i}=\{(x^{\prime},x_{d})\colon x_{d}>h(x^{\prime})\}
.
∎

Moreover, let us mention that a bounded Lipschitz domain satisfies an interior cone condition. This seems to be well known and is used for example in
[
NT06
]
, see also
[
AF03
, 4.11]
. However we could not find a proof of this fact. To convince ourselves, we gave a proof of it in
[
KS20
, Lem. 5]
which we will not repeat here.

B.3
The proof of the characterization if
q\geq p

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex domain and
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\Omega
. Further, let the parameters
p,q,s
be as in the statement of Theorem
4.2
with
q\geq p
and fix
r\in(0,1]
such that
\Omega
contains a closed ball of radius
r
. By Lemma
4.16
the set
\Omega
satisfies an interior cone condition as in Definition
4.15
with radius
r>0
and some angle
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
. In the following, all constants are allowed to depend on the parameters mentioned and in particular on the domain
\Omega
and its dimension
d
.

For the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
we apply Lemma
4.14
to the point set
P_{n}
and the closure
\overline{\Omega}
. To this end, we first deduce Lemma
4.14
and then give a proof of the fact that the closure of a set inherits the interior cone condition.

Proof of Lemma
4.14
.

We will show that for any point set
P_{n}\subset K
there is a subset
X\subset P_{n}
with
h_{X,K}\leq 2h_{P_{n},K}
and separation distance
q_{X}\geq\frac{1}{2}h_{P_{n},K}
. Then the condition (
4.11
) is satisfied for
X
and we can apply
[
Wen05
, Thm. 4.7]
to the subset
X
. We then set the functions
u_{i}
for every
x_{i}\in P_{n}\setminus X
equal to the zero function.

Take
y_{1}=x_{1}\in P_{n}
and iteratively choose
y_{i}\in P_{n}\setminus\bigcup_{j<i}B(y_{j},h_{P_{n},K})
until this set difference is empty. In this way, we obtain a subset
X
of
P_{n}
which satisfies
q_{X}\geq\frac{1}{2}h_{P_{n},K}
. Moreover, for any
x\in K
, there is some
x_{i}\in P_{n}
with
\|x-x_{i}\|_{2}\leq h_{P_{n},K}
. Since
P_{n}\setminus\bigcup_{y\in X}B(y,h_{P_{n},K})=\emptyset
, there is some
y\in X
with
\|x_{i}-y\|_{2}\leq h_{P_{n},K}
. The triangle inequality gives
\|x-y\|_{2}\leq 2\,h_{P_{n},K}
and therefore
h_{X,K}\leq 2\,h_{P_{n},K}
.
∎

The following proof ensures that the closure
\overline{\Omega}
satisfies an interior cone condition with
r
and
\theta
as above.

Proof of Lemma
4.17
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies an interior cone condition with radius
r
and angle
\theta
. For each limit point
x
in the closure
\overline{\Omega}
consider a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Omega
converging to it and let
C(x_{n}):=C(x_{n},\xi(x_{n}),r,\theta)\subset\Omega
, where
n\in\mathbb{N},
be the corresponding sequence of cones given by the interior cone condition. As the sphere is a compact metric space when equipped with the geodesic distance, the sequence
\xi(x_{n})\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of directions contains a convergent subsequence. Upon passing to this subsequence we may assume that
\xi(x_{n})
is convergent itself and define
\xi(x)
to be its limit point. Then the cone
C(x):=C(x,\xi(x),r,\theta)
is contained in
\overline{\Omega}
. To give a proof by contradiction, suppose that this does not hold, i.e., there is a point
y\in C(x)
with positive distance from
\overline{\Omega}
. The convergence of
\xi(x_{n})\to\xi(x)
and of
x_{n}\to x
implies that every point of
C(x)
will be arbitrarily close to some point from
C(x_{n})
whenever
n
is large enough. This is a contradiction to
y\in C(x)
having a positive distance from
\overline{\Omega}
and thus of every
C(x_{n})
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

After these preparations, we prove the already known part of our main result.

Proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q\geq p
.

Let
m>s
be an integer and let
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}>0
be as in Lemma
4.14
. In order to apply it to the point set
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\overline{\Omega}
we need to have
h_{P_{n},\overline{\Omega}}\leq c_{1}r
. Without loss of generality we can assume that this is satisfied as the upper bound becomes trivial if this does not hold; compare with Lemma
B.7
in Appendix
B.4
below.

We consider the linear algorithm

S_{P_{n}}\colon W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)\to L_{q}(\Omega),\quad S_{P_{n}}(f)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}}f(x_{i})u_{i},

with
u_{i}
provided by Lemma
4.14
for the compact set
\overline{\Omega}
.

For technical reasons we need a suitable covering of the domain by pieces of radius of order
h_{P_{n},\Omega}
. In order to find such a covering, consider the balls
B(y,h_{P_{n},\Omega})
,
y\in\Omega
. By compactness we can extract a finite subset
I\subset\Omega
such that the balls with
y\in I
form a covering of
\overline{\Omega}
. Because of a well-known finite covering lemma due to G. Vitali we can select from this finite covering a subset of pairwise disjoint balls
B_{i}:=B(y_{i},h_{P_{n},\Omega})
such that the balls
3B_{i}:=B(y_{i},3h_{P_{n},\Omega})
, where
i=1,\dots,N
, cover
\overline{\Omega}
.

Let
f\in W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)
with
\|f\|_{W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)}\leq 1
. By Lemma
B.3
, we can replace
f
with its extension and assume that
f\in W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|f\|_{W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{1}
, where
C_{1}>0
is independent of
f
. Fix some
i\leq N
and consider the cube
Q_{i}
centered at
y_{i}
with radius
(3+c_{2})h_{P_{n},\Omega}
. By Lemma
B.1
, we find a polynomial
\pi_{i}
of degree at most
s
such that

\sup_{y\in Q_{i}}\big|(f-\pi_{i})(y)\big|\leq c_{\rm poly}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{s-d/p}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})}.

For each
y\in\Omega_{i}:=\Omega\cap 3B_{i}
, we note that
u_{i}(y)=0
for
x_{i}\not\in Q_{i}
, and obtain

\displaystyle\big|(f-S_{P_{n}}f)(y)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|(f-\pi_{i})(y)-S_{P_{n}}(f-\pi_{i})(y)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|(f-\pi_{i})(y)\big|+\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f-\pi_{i})(x_{i})u_{i}(y)\Big|\leq C_{2}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{s-d/p}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})},

(B.6)

where
C_{2}=(1+c_{0})c_{\rm poly}
. In particular, this yields

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{2}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{s-d/p}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

which proves the case
q=\infty
. For
p\leq q<\infty
, we use
\Omega_{i}\subset Q_{i}
and (
B.6
) to get

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega_{i}}\big|\big(f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\big)(y)\big|^{q}{\rm d}y\leq C_{2}^{q}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{sq-dq/p}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})}^{q}\mathrm{vol}(\Omega_{i}),

and thus, as a continuation,

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\leq C_{3}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{sq-dq/p+d}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})}^{q}\leq C_{3}\,h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{sq-dq/p+d}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})}^{p}\Big)^{q/p}.

It remains to bound the sum on the right-hand side by a constant. A volume argument shows that there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
P_{n}\subset\Omega
every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in at most
M\in\mathbb{N}
of the
N
cubes
Q_{i}
. To see this, note that the balls
B_{i}\subset Q_{i}
are disjoint and of radius
h_{P_{n},\Omega}
. Therefore,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(Q_{i})}^{p}=\sum_{|\alpha|=s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(|D^{\alpha}f(x)|^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}}(x)\Big){\rm d}x\leq M\,|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\leq MC_{1}^{p}

(B.7)

and we arrive at the desired inequality.
∎

As described in Section
4.4
, the lower bound will be proven with the help of a fooling function
f_{\ast}
from the unit ball of
W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)
which has a large
L_{q}
-norm and is supported in the largest hole of the point set.

Proof of the lower bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q\geq p
.

Let
P_{n}\subset\Omega
be arbitrary and choose
x_{0}\in\Omega
such that

\mathrm{dist}(x_{0},P_{n})\geq r_{0}:=\min\{r,h_{P_{n},\Omega}/2\}.

(B.8)

Then the ball
B(x_{0},r_{0})
does not contain any point of
P_{n}
and is a large hole in which we can place a fooling function. However, this ball need not be contained in
\Omega
. To remedy this, we will find a ball in the intersection
\Omega\cap B(x_{0},r_{0})
with radius proportional to
r_{0}
. This is the content of Lemma
B.6
below, for which we need the following special case of
[
Wen05
, Lem. 3.7]
.

Lemma B.5
.

Every cone
C(x,\xi,r,\theta)
contains a closed ball of radius
c_{\theta}r
with
c_{\theta}:=\frac{\sin\theta}{1+\sin\theta}
.

With this geometric fact we obtain the following simple but useful consequence.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy an interior cone condition with parameters
r
and
\theta
. If
B(x,\varrho)
is a ball with center
x\in\Omega
and radius
0<\varrho\leq r
, there is a ball
B(y,c_{\theta}\varrho)
contained in
\Omega\cap B(x,\varrho)
with
c_{\theta}
as in Lemma
B.5
.

Proof.

By the interior cone condition, there is a cone with apex
x
, radius
\varrho
and angle
\theta
such that its interior is contained in
\Omega\cap B(x,\varrho)
. Now Lemma
B.5
completes the proof.
∎

Returning to the proof of the lower bound of Theorem
4.2
, consider again the empty ball
B(x_{0},r_{0})
, where
r_{0}
is as in (
B.8
). By Lemma
B.6
we find a ball
B(y,\varrho)
with
\varrho:=c_{\theta}r_{0},
which is contained in
\Omega\cap B(x_{0},r_{0})
. Now, take a smooth non-negative function
\varphi
supported in
B(0,1)
with
\varphi(0)=1
and consider the function
x\mapsto f_{*}(x)=\varphi\big(\varrho^{-1}(x-y)\big)
, which is supported in
B(y,\varrho)
. One can easily compute the scaling properties

\|f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}\asymp\varrho^{d/q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\lesssim\varrho^{-s+d/p}

(B.9)

by taking into account that
\varrho\leq 1
and
s\geq d/p
, see also the proof of
[
HKN+20
, Thm. 4.11]
. Here, the implicit constants are independent of
\varrho
, but depend on the choice of
\varphi
. Replacing
f_{*}
by its normalization
f_{*}/\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}
, it satisfies

\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq 1,\quad f_{*}|_{P_{n}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\|f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}\gtrsim h_{P_{n},\Omega}^{s-d(1/p-1/q)}.

This follows after inserting for
\varrho
in (
B.9
) and noting that
\varrho=c_{\theta}\min\{r,h_{P_{n},\Omega}/2\}
as well as that
h_{P_{n},\Omega}
is bounded from above. Since
f_{*}\geq 0
, this completes the proof also for the integration problem.

\hfill\square

B.4
The proof of the characterization if
q<p

Let
\Omega,P_{n},p,q,s,r
and
\theta
as in the previous section but now with
q<p
. Before we give the proof of Theorem
4.2
a, we prove the auxilary results Lemma
4.18
, Lemma
4.19
and Proposition
4.22
from Section
4.3
.

For the proof of Lemma
4.18
we shall need that a full-dimensional convex set satisfies an interior cone condition. This has been stated in Lemma
4.16
and is a consequence of
[
Wen05
, Prop. 11.26]
which, in fact, holds for more general sets, which are star-shaped with respect to a ball. Here, a set is called star-shaped with respect to a ball if the line connecting any point in this ball with any point in the set itself is fully contained in it. In particular, every convex set is star-shaped with respect to any ball inside it.

Proof of Lemma
4.18
.

Let
x\in\Omega
and
0<\varrho\leq r
. By Lemma
4.16
, the set
\Omega
contains a cone with radius
\varrho
, apex
x
and angle
\theta
. Clearly, this cone is contained in
\overline{\Omega\cap B(x,\varrho)}
, which in turn is a subset of
A(x,\varrho):=\overline{\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)}
. By Lemma
B.5
, there is a closed ball of radius
c_{\theta}\varrho
in this cone and thus in
A(x,\varrho)
. The proof is finished if we apply Lemma
4.16
to the convex set
A(x,\varrho)
since its diameter is at most
2\varrho\sqrt{d}
and therefore we can set
\theta^{\prime}=2\arcsin(c_{\theta}/4\sqrt{d})
.
∎

Together with Lemma
B.6
from Section
B.3
we can deduce the proof of the local approximation result which gave rise to the notion of a good cube.

Proof of Lemma
4.19
.

Let
Q=Q(x,\varrho)
for some
x\in\Omega
and
0<\varrho\leq r
. By Lemma
4.18
the set
A(x,\varrho):=\overline{\Omega\cap Q}
satisfies an interior cone condition with radius
\varrho^{\prime}=c_{\theta}\varrho
and angle
\theta^{\prime}
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer greater than
s
and let
c_{0},c_{1}>0
be as in Lemma
4.14
for the parameters
\theta^{\prime}
and
m
. We can assume without loss of generality that
c_{1}<1
. We set

c_{\rm good}:=c_{\theta}c_{\theta^{\prime}}c_{1}/2,

(B.10)

where
c_{\theta^{\prime}}=\frac{\sin\theta^{\prime}}{1+\sin\theta^{\prime}}
is from Lemma
B.5
. Then assumption (
4.13
) implies that every ball
B(y,2c_{\rm good}\varrho)
,
y\in\Omega\cap Q
, contains a point of
P_{n}
. By Lemma
B.6
applied to
A(x,\varrho)
every ball
B(x,2c_{\theta^{\prime}}^{-1}c_{\rm good}\varrho),x\in\Omega\cap Q
, contains a ball
B(y,2c_{\rm good}\varrho)\subset A(x,\varrho)
and therefore a point of
P_{n}\cap Q
. We thus have

h_{P_{n}\cap Q,\Omega\cap Q}=\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q}\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n}\cap Q)\leq 2c_{\theta^{\prime}}^{-1}c_{\rm good}\varrho=c_{1}\varrho^{\prime}.

Thus, we may apply Lemma
4.14
to the point set
P_{n}\cap Q
within the compact set
A(x,\varrho)
and obtain bounded continuous functions
u_{i}\colon\Omega\cap Q\to\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\dots,n
with

\sum_{i=1}^{n}|u_{i}(y)|\leq c_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(x)u_{i}(y)

for all
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{d}
and
y\in\Omega\cap Q
, where we set
u_{i}=0
for
x_{i}\in P_{n}\setminus Q
. For any
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by Lemma
B.1
there is a polynomial
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{d}
with

\sup_{y\in Q}\big|(f-\pi)(y)\big|\,\leq\,c_{\rm poly}\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q)}.

Here, we used that
\varrho\leq r\leq 1
. Similar to the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q\geq p
in Section
B.3
, we get for all
y\in\Omega\cap Q
that

\displaystyle\Big|f(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})u_{i}(y)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|(f-\pi)(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f-\pi)(x_{i})u_{i}(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\big|(f-\pi)(y)\big|+c_{0}\,\max_{x_{i}\in P_{n}\cap Q}\big|(f-\pi)(x_{i})\big|

\displaystyle\,\leq\,(1+c_{0})c_{\rm poly}\,\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q)},

as it was to be proven.
∎

We state and prove the following justification for
r_{P_{n}}(x)\leq r
for all
x\in\Omega
, which ensures the integrity of Definition
4.21
.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
P_{n}\subset\Omega
be a non-empty finite point set. If, for some
x\in\Omega
, we can not find
\varrho\in(0,r)
such that (
4.13
), i.e.,

\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho)}\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})\leq c_{\rm good}\varrho

holds, then
\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}\geq c
for any
0<\gamma\leq\infty
and some constant
c>0
independent of the point set
P_{n}
.

Proof.

Because of (
B.1
) in Appendix
B.1
it is sufficient to consider finite
\gamma
. By assumption we find
y\in\Omega
with
\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})>c_{\rm good}r/2
, that is the ball
B(y,c_{\rm good}r/2)
is empty of
P_{n}
. By Lemma
B.6
we find a ball
B(z,r^{\prime})
of radius
r^{\prime}=c_{\theta}c_{\rm good}r/2
contained in
\Omega\cap B(y,c_{\rm good}r/2)
. Hence, the distance function satisfies
\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})\geq r^{\prime}/2
for all
x\in B(z,r^{\prime}/2)
. Raising this inequality to the power of
\gamma
and integrating over
\Omega
yields the desired result.
∎

The following lemma is used to show that
Q_{P_{n}}(x)
is a good cube for every
x\in\Omega
.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
x\in\Omega
. Then

\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,r_{P_{n}}(x))}\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})\leq c_{\rm good}\,r_{P_{n}}(x).

Proof.

Take a sequence
(\varrho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,r)
converging from above to
r_{P_{n}}(x)
such that
Q(x,\varrho_{n})
satisfies (
4.13
), that is,

\sup_{y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,\varrho_{n})}\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})\leq c_{\rm good}\,\varrho_{n}.

As
Q\big(x,r_{P_{n}}(x)\big)\subset Q(x,\varrho_{n})
for every
n
, taking the infimum completes the proof.
∎

Before we come to the main part of the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q<p
, we need to validate the covering by good cubes given in Proposition
4.22
. As mentioned, we will make use of a Besicovitch-type covering result in
[
dG75
, Ch. 1,Thm. 1.1]
. Remark (2) directly below it allows us to consider open cubes instead of closed ones. For convenience we rephrase the statement here.

Consider a bounded set
D
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for each point
x\in D
a cube
Q\big(x,r(x)\big)
centered at this point with some radius
r(x)>0
. Then one can choose a (possibly finite) sequence among the given cubes which is a cover of
A
, can be distributed in finitely many families, each consisting of pairwise disjoint cubes, and every point of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in at most a constant many cubes of the sequence. These constants depend only on the dimension.

Similar to the proof we provided in
[
KS20
]
to find a suitable covering, the idea behind the proof is to iteratively subtract from
D
a cube of maximal size.

Proof of Proposition
4.22
.

For each point set
P_{n}
, we will apply
[
dG75
, Ch. 1,Thm. 1.1]
to the bounded convex domain
\Omega
and the collection
Q_{P_{n}}(x)
of small good cubes. In this way, we obtain a sequence
y_{1},y_{2},\dots
of points belonging to
\Omega
such that (i), (ii) and (iii) in Proposition
4.22
are satisfied with possibly infinite
N
. The proof is complete if we can show that the sequence is indeed finite. This is what we show in the following using a volume argument.

Consider the families
\mathcal{Q}_{j},j=1,\dots,M_{1}
of pairwise disjoint cubes into which the set
Q_{P_{n}}(y_{i}),i\in\mathbb{N},
is distributed. We show that an arbitrary such family
\mathcal{Q}_{j_{0}}
is finite and thus the whole collection
Q_{P_{n}}(y_{i}),i\in\mathbb{N}
. All the cubes
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{j_{0}}
are pairwise disjoint and contained in the neighborhood
\Omega_{r}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\exists x\text{ with }\|x-y\|_{\infty}\leq r\}
. It is sufficient to show that there is a constant
c>0
such that
\mathrm{vol}(Q)\geq c
for all of these cubes since then

\#\mathcal{Q}_{j_{0}}\,c\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{j_{0}}}\mathrm{vol}(Q)\leq\mathrm{vol}(\Omega_{r})

implies that the family has to be of finite cardinality and that the sequence
y_{1},y_{2},\dots
is in fact finite and of length
N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will show that in fact that there exists
c>0
such that
\mathrm{vol}\big(Q_{P_{n}}(x)\big)\geq c
for every
Q_{P_{n}}(x)
with
x\in\Omega
. For this, note that any such good cube contains at least two points of
P_{n}
. In particular, for any
x\in\Omega
,

0<\frac{q_{P_{n}}}{\sqrt{d}}<r_{P_{n}}(x)\leq r,

where
q_{P_{n}}=\frac{1}{2}\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}
is the separation distance of
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. This gives a uniform lower bound of the form
\mathrm{vol}\big(Q_{P_{n}}(x)\big)=\big(2\,r_{P_{n}}(x)\big)^{d}\geq c:=(2\,q_{P_{n}}/\sqrt{d})^{d}
.
∎

The following proof is similar to that of
[
KS20
, Prop. 2]
.

Proof of Lemma
4.25
.

Let
x\in\Omega
and
Q_{P_{n}}(x)=Q\big(x,r_{P_{n}}(x)\big)
. By definition,
r_{P_{n}}(x)
is the infimum over all
\varrho\in(0,r)
such that (
4.13
) holds. In particular, we find a point
y\in\Omega\cap Q(x,r_{P_{n}}(x)/2)
with
\mathrm{dist}(y,P_{n})\geq c_{\rm good}r_{P_{n}}(x)/4
meaning that the ball
B(y,c_{\rm good}r_{P_{n}}(x)/4)\subset Q_{P_{n}}(x)
is empty of
P_{n}
. By Lemma
B.6
applied to
\Omega
we obtain a ball of radius
c_{\rm hole}r_{P_{n}}(x)
with
c_{\rm hole}:=c_{\theta}c/4
which is contained in
\Omega\cap B(y,c_{\rm good}r_{P_{n}}(x)/4)
. As
c_{\rm good}<1
(see (
B.10
)), this ball is also contained in
\Omega\cap Q_{P_{n}}(y)
.
∎

Let us conclude this section with the proof of our main result.

Proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q<p
.

We choose
y_{i}\in\Omega
,
r_{i}:=r_{P_{n}}(y_{i})
,
Q_{i}=Q(y_{i},r_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq N
as in Proposition
4.22
. To get a well-defined algorithm, we will make the covering disjoint by letting

\Omega_{i}:=(Q_{i}\cap\Omega)\setminus\bigcup_{j<i}\Omega_{j}

for all
i=1,\dots,N
. That is,
\Omega
is the disjoint union of the sets
\Omega_{i}\subset Q_{i}
.

Then, for any
x_{j}\in P_{n}
and
y\in\Omega_{i}
, we define
u_{j}(y)
according to Lemma
4.19
, applied to the cube
Q_{i}
. This yields bounded functions
u_{j}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and a linear algorithm

S_{P_{n}}\colon W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)\to L_{q}(\Omega),\quad S_{P_{n}}(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(x_{j})u_{j}.

We proceed by showing that this algorithm which uses function samples at the point set
P_{n}
satisfies the required bound on the worst-case error. To this end, let
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
with
\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq 1
. As in the proof for the case
q\geq p
, using Lemma
B.3
, we may assume that
f\in W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C
. With the help of Lemma
4.19
we derive the estimate

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega_{i})}^{q}\lesssim r_{i}^{(s-d/p)q+d}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{i})}^{q},\quad i=1,\dots,N.

Using this we obtain

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega_{i})}^{q}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{(s-d/p)q+d}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{i})}^{q}.

If
p=\infty
, we use that
|f|_{W^{s}_{\infty}(Q_{i})}\leq|f|_{W^{s}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C
to obtain

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{sq+d}.

And if
p<\infty
, we have by means of
(s-d/p)q+d=(\gamma+d)(1-q/p)
and Hölder’s inequality that

\|f-S_{P_{n}}(f)\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\lesssim\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{(\gamma+d)(1-q/p)}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{i})}^{q}\,\leq\,\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\bigg)^{1-q/p}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N}|f|_{W^{s}_{p}(Q_{i})}^{p}\bigg)^{q/p}.

Since the cubes
Q_{i}
form an efficient covering, we can proceed as in (
B.7
) to obtain that the second factor in the previous estimate is bounded by a constant. We will show that the first factor is bounded by
\|\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})\|_{L_{\gamma}(\Omega)}^{qs}
.

To this end, let
B_{i}:=B(z_{i},c_{\rm hole}r_{i})\subset\Omega\cap Q_{i}
be the ball of radius
c_{\rm hole}r_{i}
given by Lemma
4.25
for each
i=1,\ldots,N
. Write, for every
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}=c\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma}\mathrm{vol}(B_{i}^{\prime}),

(B.11)

where
B_{i}^{\prime}:=B(z_{i},c_{\rm hole}r_{i}/2)
is the ball of half the radius which is concentric with
B_{i}
and
c>0
depends on
d
and
c_{\rm hole}
. Since
B_{i}
is empty of
P_{n}
, the distance function satisfies the lower bound
\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})\geq c_{\rm hole}r_{i}/2
for every
x\in B_{i}^{\prime}
. Therefore, integrating over
B_{i}^{\prime}
shows that

(c_{\rm hole}r_{i}/2)^{\gamma}\mathrm{vol}(B_{i}^{\prime})\leq\int_{B_{i}^{\prime}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x.

(B.12)

Combining the estimates (
B.11
) and (
B.12
) yields

\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{i}^{\prime}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\leq\int_{\Omega}\Big(\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}}(x)\Big){\rm d}x.

Again the efficiency of the cover
Q_{i},i=1,\ldots,N
, yields the required bound. Noting that
(1-q/p)=qs/\gamma
, this completes the proof.
∎

We give now the proof of the corresponding lower bound. For this purpose we use a fooling function supported in a disjoint subset of the holes given by Proposition
4.22
together with Lemma
4.25
.

Proof of the lower bound of Theorem
4.2
in the case
q<p
.

To begin, choose a cover by good cubes
Q_{i}=Q_{P_{n}}(y_{i})=Q(y_{i},r_{i})
, where
i=1,\ldots,N
, as given by Proposition
4.22
. Let, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
, the ball
B_{i}:=B(z_{i},c_{\rm hole}r_{i})\subset\Omega\cap Q_{i}
be as in Lemma
4.25
.

Then, for every
j=1,\ldots,M_{1}
, we collect the balls
B_{i},i=1,\ldots,N
, such that
Q_{i}
belongs to the family
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
into the family
\mathcal{B}_{j}
. Thus, each ball
B_{i},i=1,\ldots,N,
belongs to exactly one of the families
\mathcal{B}_{j},j=1,\ldots,M_{1}
and the families themselves are pairwise disjoint. To select a suitable family, note that
since
a sum over some family must be at least the average over the sums over all families, there must be an index
1\leq j_{0}\leq M_{1}
with

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N\\
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{j_{0}}\end{subarray}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\geq\frac{1}{M_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N\\
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{j}\end{subarray}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}=\frac{1}{M_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}.

(B.13)

We shall use this bound later for estimating the norm of the fooling function we consider. Consider the disjoint balls belonging to the family
\mathcal{B}_{j_{0}}
, each of which will contain part of the support of the fooling function. Let
I_{0}\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
be the subset of indices with
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{j_{0}}
. To define the fooling function we use atomic decompositions as discussed by Triebel
[
Tri11
, Ch. 13]
, see Section
4.4
.

Let
K>s
be an integer and
Q_{0}=Q(0,1/2)
be the cube of sidelength
1
which is centered at the origin. Further, let
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be non-negative with
\mathrm{supp}\,\psi\subset Q_{0}
such that
\psi(0)>0
and
\|D^{\alpha}\psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\leq K
. The exact choice of the function
\psi
is not important for our argument. Then according to Definition 13.3 in
[
Tri11
]
the function

\psi_{\nu m}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\psi_{\nu m}(x)=2^{-\nu(s-d/p)}\psi(2^{\nu}x-m),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\text{where }\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

is an
(s,p)_{K,-1}
-atom supported in
Q_{\nu m}
, the cube with center
2^{-\nu}m
and sidelength
2^{-\nu}
.

Choose, for every
i\in I_{0}
, a point
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\nu_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
2^{-\nu_{i}}\leq\sqrt{d}\,c_{\rm hole}r_{i}\leq 2^{-\nu_{i}+1}
such that the dyadic cube
Q_{i}^{*}=Q_{\nu_{i}m_{i}}
is contained in
B_{i}
. Define the fooling function

f_{*}:=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}\lambda_{i}\psi_{i},\quad\text{where }\lambda_{i}:=2^{-\nu_{i}\beta}\text{ with }\beta=(\gamma+d)/p,

which is supported in
\Omega
and satisfies
f_{*}|_{P_{n}}=0
. The choice
\lambda_{i}
will become clear in a moment.

To estimate
\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}
from above, we use Theorem 13.8 in
[
Tri11
]
, which yields a constant
C>0
depending only on
d,s,p
such that

\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\leq C\,\|\lambda\|_{f_{pp}}^{p},\quad\text{where }\|\lambda\|_{f_{pp}}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\sum_{i\in I_{0}}|\lambda_{i}2^{\nu_{i}d/p}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}^{*}}(x)|^{p}\Big){\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/p}.

(B.14)

Here, the symbol
f_{pp}
denotes a (quasi-)normed space of sequences which are indexed by
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, see
[
Tri11
, Def. 13.5]
. In the sum in (
B.14
), for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, only one summand is not equal to zero and since
\mathrm{vol}(Q_{i}^{*})=2^{-\nu_{i}d}
, we have

\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(2^{-\nu_{i}})^{\beta p-d}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}^{*}}(x){\rm d}x=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}(2^{-\nu_{i}})^{\beta p}.

As
\beta p=\gamma+d
and
2^{-\nu_{i}}\asymp r_{i}
and
\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}=\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, we arrive at

\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}.

Since the balls
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{j_{0}}
containing the cubes
Q_{i}^{*}
are pairwise disjoint, a substitution shows that

\|f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}\int_{Q_{i}^{*}}(2^{-\nu_{i}})^{\beta q}\psi_{i}(x)^{q}{\rm d}x=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}(2^{-\nu_{i}})^{\beta q+sq-dq/p+d}\int_{Q}\psi(x)^{q}{\rm d}x.

Since
\gamma=s(1/q-1/p)^{-1}
we have
\beta q+sq=\gamma+dq/p
and thus

\|f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}^{q}\asymp\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}.

From this it follows that if we replace
f_{*}
by the normalized function
f_{*}/\|f_{*}\|_{W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)}
, it satisfies

\|f_{*}\|_{L_{q}(\Omega)}\gtrsim\Big(\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\Big)^{1/q-1/p}.

(B.15)

We now employ the bound (
B.13
) for the specific choice of the family
\mathcal{B}_{j_{0}}
to estimate

\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\geq\frac{1}{M_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\geq\frac{1}{2^{d}M_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_{i}^{\gamma}\mathrm{vol}(\Omega\cap Q_{i}),

(B.16)

where
Q_{i}
is as above. By definition, we have
\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})\leq c_{\rm good}r_{i}
for every
x\in\Omega\cap Q_{i}
. Integrating this inequality yields

\int_{\Omega\cap Q_{i}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\leq c_{\rm good}^{\gamma}r_{i}^{\gamma}\mathrm{vol}(\Omega\cap Q_{i}),\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

(B.17)

Thus, combining the bounds (
B.16
) and (
B.17
) gives

\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}^{\gamma+d}\gtrsim\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega\cap Q_{i}}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x\geq\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{\gamma}{\rm d}x

since the cubes
Q_{i},i=1,\dots,N
, cover
\Omega
. Inserting this into (
B.15
) yields the lower bound for the
L_{q}
-norm of the fooling function and thus the lower bound for the
L_{q}
-approximation problem. Since
f_{\ast}\geq 0
, also the lower bound for the integration problem is proven.

∎

B.5
Other function spaces

In this section we give a proof of Theorem
4.10
. For the convenience of the reader we will give a definition of Triebel-Lizorkin spaces and also Besov spaces on bounded Lipschitz domains. We will mostly follow
[
NT06
]
. For more details see, e.g., Triebel
[
Tri92
]
or DeVore and Sharpley
[
DS93
]
.

The definition of Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.
We first define the Besov space
B^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the Triebel-Lizorkin space
F^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the help of a suitable dyadic resolution of unity of the Fourier domain. The Fourier transform maps a function
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
belonging to
S(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing smooth functions, to the function
\widehat{\psi}
defined by

\widehat{\psi}(\xi)=F(\psi)(\xi):=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-i\langle x,\xi\rangle}\psi(x){\rm d}x,\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The inverse
F^{-1}
of
F
acts the same way but with
-i
replaced by
i
.

Choose
\psi\in S(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\psi(x)=1\quad\text{if}\quad\|x\|_{2}\leq 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi(x)=0\quad\text{if}\quad\|x\|_{2}\geq\frac{3}{2}.

If we put
\psi_{0}:=\psi
and

\psi_{j}(x):=\psi(2^{-j}x)-\psi(2^{-j+1}x)\quad\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }j\in\mathbb{N},

then the system
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
forms a dyadic resolution of unity, that is
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\psi_{j}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any tempered distribution
f\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
its Fourier and inverse Fourier transform may be defined and the function
F^{-1}(\psi_{j}\widehat{f})
can be evaluated pointwise. Thus, the following definition makes sense.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<q\leq\infty
. For
0<p\leq\infty
define the Besov space
B^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the collection of all
f\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\psi}:=\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}\|F^{-1}(\psi_{j}\widehat{f})\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{1/q}

is finite. If
q=\infty
we modify as usual by taking the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm. For
0<p<\infty
the Triebel-Lizorkin space
F^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given as the collection of all
f\in S^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with finite

\|f\|_{F^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\psi}:=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}|F^{-1}(\psi_{j}\widehat{f})(\cdot)|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\Big\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The expression
\|\cdot\|_{A^{s}_{pq}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\psi}
, where
A
stands for either
B
or
F
, is a quasi-norm and becomes a norm if both
p,q\geq 1
. A different choice of
\psi
yields an equivalent (quasi-)norm, which is why
\psi
is omitted in the notation.

On a bounded Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
one can define the space
A^{s}_{pq}(\Omega)
via restriction as in (
4.10
). By definition, the space
A^{s}_{pq}(\Omega)
contains distributions but an embedding theorem shows that if
s>d/p
we have the continuous embedding
A^{s}_{pq}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\Omega)
. Further, extension of these functions onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is possible if
s>d/p
. Thus, Lemma
B.3
extends to these spaces. For further information consult for example Triebel’s book
[
Tri92
]
and see also Rychkov
[
Ryc99
]
for the extension theorem.

The proof of Theorem
4.10
.
Let us first note that the analogues of Corollary
4.3
and Corollary
4.6
immediately follow if we can extend Theorem
4.2
, which is what we prove in the following.

For the space
C^{s}(\Omega)
with
s\in\mathbb{N}
the extension of Theorem
4.2
a is already included in Section
B.3
. Namely, the upper bound is immediate from the continuous embedding
C^{s}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\infty}^{s}(\Omega)
. For this note that all norms on the finite-dimensional space of multi-indices are equivalent. The lower bound holds since our fooling functions
f_{\ast}
for
W_{\infty}^{s}(\Omega)
are smooth and thus contained also in
C^{s}(\Omega)
with an equivalent norm.

For the remaining cases, we follow the lines of the proof of Theorem
4.2
as given in the previous Sections
B.3
and
B.4
. We will discuss the necessary changes and refrain from copying the proof.

Let
0<p,q,\tau\leq\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
s>d/p
. We first give the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
4.10
in the remaining cases, where we replace
W^{s}_{p}(\Omega)
by

•

C^{s}(\Omega)
and
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
if
p=\infty
,

•

F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
if
p<\infty
.

Note that it suffices to consider the case
\tau=\infty
since we have the continuous embedding
F^{s}_{p\tau_{1}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p\tau_{2}}(\Omega)
for
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. Everywhere in the proof of Theorem
4.2
we replace
|f|_{W_{p}^{s}(\Omega)}
by the following (quasi-)seminorms.

In the case
p=\infty
and
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the seminorm
|f|_{C^{s}(\Omega)}
as defined in (
4.9
).

If
p<\infty
, we use a (quasi-)seminorm which is defined via the averaged means

(d_{t}^{M,\Omega}f)(x):=t^{-d}\int_{V^{M}_{\Omega}(x,t)}\bigl|(\Delta_{h,\Omega}^{M}f)(x)\bigr|\,\text{d}h,

where
M:=\lfloor s+1\rfloor
,
\Delta_{h,\Omega}^{M}
is an
M^{\rm{th}}
-order difference operator restricted to
\Omega
and
V^{M}_{\Omega}(x,t)
is the set of directions
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of length less than
t>0
with
x+ah\in\Omega
for all
0\leq a\leq M
. More precisely,

\Delta_{h,\Omega}^{M}(f):=\begin{cases}\Delta_{h}^{M}(f)&\text{if }x+lh\in\Omega\text{ for }l=0,\ldots,M,\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where, for every
x,h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Delta_{h}^{M}(f)=\Delta_{h}^{1}\Delta_{h}^{M-1}(f)\quad\text{with}\quad\Delta_{h}^{1}(f):=f(x+h)-f(x).

Choosing then in the case
p<\infty
the (quasi-)seminorm

|f|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)}:=\bigg\|\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\frac{(d_{t}^{M,\Omega}f)(\cdot)}{t^{s}}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\Omega)},

it is known that then

F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)=\{f\in L_{\infty}(\Omega)\colon|f|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty\}

with

\|\cdot\|_{L_{\max\{p,1\}}(\Omega)}+|\cdot|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)}

being an equivalent quasi-norm, see Proposition 6 in
[
NT06
]
and set
u=1
as well as
r=\infty
. The same is true for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

It is readily verified that these (quasi-)seminorms have the following scaling property. If
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is of the form
T(y)=\varrho^{-1}(y-x)
with
\varrho\leq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f\in F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)
or
f\in C^{s}(\Omega)
, then

|f\circ T^{-1}|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(Q_{0})}\leq\varrho^{s-d/p}|f|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(Q)},\qquad|f\circ T^{-1}|_{C^{s}(Q_{0})}=\varrho^{s}|f|_{C^{s}(Q)},\quad\text{respectively.}

(B.18)

As mentioned above the extension theorem in Lemma
B.3
holds without changes for the spaces
F^{s}_{p\tau}(\Omega)
and
C^{s}(\Omega)
, where we note that
C^{s}(\Omega)=B^{s}_{\infty\infty}(\Omega)
for
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
. If we use
[
NT06
, Cor. 11]
(for
p<\infty
) and
[
DS80
, Thm. 6.1]
(for
p=\infty
) instead of
[
Maz85
, Lem. 1.1.11]
and the scaling properties (
B.18
), we see that Lemma
B.1
concerning polynomial approximation on cubes remains valid under the modifications of Theorem
4.10
. Thus, also Lemma
4.19
defies our modifications.

To complete the proof of the upper bound in both cases
q\geq p
and
q<p
, it only remains to note that the seminorm
|\cdot|_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(\Omega)}
behaves equally well with respect to an efficient covering. Analogous to (
B.7
), we have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}|f|^{p}_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(Q_{i})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\frac{(d_{t}^{M,Q_{i}}f)(x)}{t^{s}}\right)^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}}(x){\rm d}x\leq c_{7}|f|^{p}_{F^{s}_{p\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq c_{8}

(B.19)

since
d_{t}^{M,Q_{i}}f(x)\leq d_{t}^{M,\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With these preparations at hand, we may copy the proof of the upper bound in both cases
q\geq p
and
q<p
.

Let us now discuss the lower bound. In case of
q\geq p
we can use the same fooling function supported in a large hole. The scaling properties (
B.9
) for
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
require a straightforward computation, and for
F^{s}_{p\tau}
they may be obtained e.g. from Proposition 2.3.1/1 in Edmunds and Triebel
[
ET96
]
and the translation-invariance of the (quasi-)seminorm.

Let us therefore consider the case
q>p
where we used an atomic decomposition which is also valid for Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. If, for
p<\infty
, we want to replace
W^{s}_{p}
by
F^{s}_{p\tau}
, we have to substitute the bound (
B.14
) by

\|f_{*}\|_{F^{s}_{p\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\leq C\,\|\lambda\|_{f_{p\tau}}^{p},\quad\text{where }\|\lambda\|_{f_{p\tau}}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\sum_{i\in I_{0}}|\lambda_{i}2^{\nu_{i}d/p}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}^{*}}(x)|^{\tau}\Big)^{p/\tau}{\rm d}x\bigg)^{1/p}.

Here,

\|\cdot\|_{f_{p\tau}}
is another sequence quasi-norm. Otherwise the proof can remain unchanged.

In the case
q<p=\infty
, where we replace
W^{s}_{p}
by
C^{s}
, we use the convention that
a/\infty=0
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, whence
\beta=0
and
\lambda_{i}=1
for all
i\in I_{0}
. In this case, the fooling function is equal to

f_{*}:=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}2^{-s\nu_{i}}\psi(2^{\nu_{i}}x-m_{i}).

The norm of this function can be bounded as follows. If
s\in\mathbb{N}
, one can use the disjoint supports of the summands to see that
\|f_{*}\|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi(x)|\leq 1
, and if
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
we use that Theorem 13.8 in
[
Tri11
]
is also valid for Besov spaces and the bound

\|f_{*}\|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\,\|\lambda\|_{b_{\infty\infty}},\quad\text{where }\|\lambda\|_{b_{\infty\infty}}:=\max_{i\in I_{0}}|\lambda_{i}|=1

instead of
(
B.14
)
. Here we used that if
s\not\in\mathbb{N}
, then
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{\infty\infty}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with equivalent norms. The remainder of the proof of the lower bound requires only obvious modifications.

Note that also Theorem
4.2
b extends to the spaces since
\psi
is non-negative. See also the discussion in Section
4.4
.

Appendix C
Additional material for Chapter 5

In the following, we prove equality (
5.2
) between the minimal Wasserstein distance to a measure supported on a point set and the distortion, identity (
5.6
) for the worst-case error for Hölder functions, the extension to the normed spaces given in Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.2
stating that weights can be assumed to be normalized.

The following proof is inspired by the one for
[
GL00
, Lem. 3.4]
. Recall that
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and that its distortion is given by (
4.14
).

Proof of (
5.2
).

For the upper bound we will exhibit an explicit transference plan between the absolutely continuous measure
\mu
and the discrete measure
\mu_{P_{n},a}
given by (
5.1
). To this end, let
T_{P_{n}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the quantizer from (
4.15
) in Section
4.5
which maps
x
to the closest point of
P_{n}
and is of the form

T_{P_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{C(x_{i},P_{n})},

where
C(x_{i},P_{n})
is as in (
5.3
).

Define the map
G_{P_{n}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
G_{P_{n}}(x)=\big(x,T_{P_{n}}(x)\big)
. Then the pushforward measure
\pi_{P_{n}}:=\mu\circ G_{P_{n}}^{-1}
satisfies, for any Borel sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\pi_{P_{n}}(A\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle=\mu(x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon x\in A,T_{P_{n}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mu(A)

\displaystyle\pi_{P_{n}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times B)

\displaystyle=\mu(x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon T_{P_{n}}(x)\in B)=\nu_{P_{n}}(B),

where
\nu_{P_{n}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu\big(C(x_{i},P_{n})\big)\delta_{x_{i}}
. Thus,
\pi_{P_{n}}
has marginals
\mu
and
\nu_{P_{n}}
and belongs to
\pi(\mu,\nu_{P_{n}})
. Then, by (
4.16
), the distortion is equal to

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x-T_{P_{n}}(x)\|^{r}{\rm d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x-y\|^{r}{\rm d}\pi_{P_{n}}(x,y)\geq T_{r}(\mu,\nu_{P_{n}})\geq\inf_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}}T_{r}(\mu,\nu_{P_{n},a}),

which completes the upper bound.

For the lower bound we note that, for every
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and every transference plan
\pi\in\pi(\mu,\nu_{P_{n},a})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x-y\|^{r}{\rm d}\pi(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times P_{n}}\|x-y\|^{r}{\rm d}\pi(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times P_{n}}\min_{i=1,\dots,n}\|x-x_{i}\|^{r}{\rm d}\pi(x,y)=D_{\mu,P_{n},r}

Taking the infimum over all couplings and then over all weights completes the proof.
∎

Next, we deduce the expression for the minimal worst-case error of weighted algorithms for Hölder and Lipschitz functions from the proof of Theorem 5 in
[
Gru04
]
.

Proof of (
5.6
).

Let
s\in(0,1)
. For the lower bound consider the fooling function
f_{\ast}:=\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})^{s}
which vanishes on
P_{n}
and has integral
\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f_{\ast})=D_{\mu,P_{n},s}
. It is a consequence of the triangle and Hölder’s inequality that
|f_{\ast}|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
. The method of fooling functions described in Section
4.4
then establishes the lower bound.

For the upper bound we note that the infimum over all weights can be bounded from above if we insert the optimal cubature rule from (
5.7
), and then

\displaystyle\inf_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{|f|_{C^{s}}\leq 1}\Big|\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})a_{i}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|f|_{C^{s}}\leq 1}\Big|\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)-Q_{P_{n},\mu}(f)\Big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{|f|_{C^{s}}\leq 1}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{C(x_{i},P_{n})}f(x)-f(x_{i}){\rm d}\mu(x)\Big|

Using the triangle inequality and that
|f|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
implies
|f(x)-f(x_{i})|\leq\|x-x_{i}\|_{2}^{s}
, we see that this bounded from above by the distortion
D_{\mu,P_{n},s}
completing the proof of the identity for
s\in(0,1)
. The modifications necessary for
s=1
and
\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are straightforward.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
. For the lower bound we again choose the fooling function
f_{\ast}:=\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,P_{n})^{s}
which satisfies
|f_{\ast}|_{C^{s}(D)}\leq 1
. To normalize, we divide by
1+\|f_{\ast}\|_{\infty}=1+h_{P_{n},D}^{s}
, which yields the lower bound

e(C^{s}(D),\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n},a})\geq\frac{1}{1+h_{P_{n},D}^{s}}\int_{D}\mathrm{dist}(x,P_{n})^{s}{\rm d}\mu(x),

where we can replace
C^{s}(D)
by
{\rm Lip}(D)
for
s=1
. To have a constant independent of the point set
P_{n}
, and also of
s
, one can use that

h_{P_{n},D}^{s}\leq\max\{1,h_{P_{n},D}\}\leq\max\{1,\mathrm{diam}(D)\}.

For the proof of the upper bound we use the just proven identity (
5.6
) and that optimal weigths are given by (
5.3
). It remains to note that the infimum over arbitrary real weights is bounded from above by the infimum over weights summing to one and that the worst-case error over functions with
|f|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
is at least as large as the worst-case error over all functions with
\|f\|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
, and analogously for
s=1
and
{\rm Lip}(D)
.
∎

Let us conclude this appendix with the following proof.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Let
f\in F
with
\|f\|_{F}\leq 1
be arbitrary and
Q_{P_{n},a}
a cubatrue rule with points
P_{n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset D
and weights
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Then the cubature rule
Q_{P_{n},a^{*}}
using the normalized weights
a^{*}
with
a_{i}^{*}=a_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i},i=1,\ldots,n,
satisfies

\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{*}f(x_{i})-\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)\Big|\leq\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f(x_{i})-\mathrm{INT}_{\mu}(f)\Big|+\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}^{*}-a_{i})f(x_{i})\Big|.

The first term on the right-hand side is bounded by
e(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n},a})
and the second term is bounded in absolute value by

\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}^{*}-a_{i})f(x_{i})\Big|\leq C\,\Big|1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\Big|\leq C\,\|\mathbf{1}_{D}\|_{F}\,e(F,\mathrm{INT}_{\mu},Q_{P_{n},a})

as the normalized function
\mathbf{1}_{D}/\|\mathbf{1}_{D}\|_{F}
belongs to the unit ball of
F
.
∎

Appendix D
Additional material for Chapter 6

In this appendix we provide the missing proofs for Chapter
6
. In Section
D.1
we prove the lower bound of its characterization in terms of the spectral test (Theorem
6.5
) and the lower bound on the spectral test (Proposition
6.8
), thus completing the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
6.3
.

Section
D.2
contains the proofs of Lemmas
6.17
and
6.19
to fill in the gaps of the proof sketch for the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
given in Section
6.4
. Additionally, we shall give the proof behind the asymptotics in Remark
6.22
. Finally, Section
D.3
contains the proof of Proposition
6.13
.

Throughout, let
L
be a
d
-dimensional lattice and
\mathcal{P}(L)=L\cap[0,1)^{d}
be the corresponding lattice point set. Further, let
\mathcal{H}^{*}
be a hyperplane covering of
L
as in Section
6.3
, where the distance between adjacent hyperplanes is maximal and equal to the spectral test
\sigma(L)
.

D.1
Lower bounds

Proof of the lower bound of Theorem
6.5
.

By the pigeonhole principle we find a hyperplane from
\mathcal{H}^{*}
which contains sufficiently many points of
\mathcal{P}(L)
. Since the unit cube
[0,1)^{d}
has diameter
\sqrt{d}
, it can be intersected by no more than
\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)+1
hyperplanes from
\mathcal{H}^{*}
. If all of them contained strictly less than

n_{-}:=\frac{\sigma(L)}{\sqrt{d}+\sigma(L)}n

points of the lattice point set
\mathcal{P}(L)
, this would be a contradiction to the fact that there are at most
\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)+1
of them and any point of
\mathcal{P}(L)
must lie on one of these hyperplanes. Therefore, we may find at least
n_{-}
points of
\mathcal{P}(L)
lying on some hyperplane. This implies the lower bound.

Suppose now that additionally
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
.
In order to find a suitable pair of hyperplanes such that their convex hull contains the center
(1/2,\dots,1/2)
of the cube
[0,1)^{d}
, consider the one-dimensional space orthogonal to all hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}^{*}
which is spanned by some
h\in L^{\bot}
. The rays emanating from the center of the cube into the opposite directions
\pm h
hit
a hyperplane
of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
at distance at most
\sigma(L)
from the center of the cube. In this way, we get a pair of adjacent hyperplanes
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
sandwiching the center of the cube with possibly one of them containing the center. Denote the collection of all hyperplanes which lie between
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
such that the interior of the convex hull of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
satisfies

\widetilde{C}={\rm int}\,{\rm conv}(H_{1}\cup H_{2})=\bigcup_{H\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}H.

Then
C:=\widetilde{C}\cap[0,1)^{d}
does not contain any point from
\mathcal{P}(L)
. Since all hyperplanes in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
have distance at most
\sigma(L)\leq 1/2
from the center,
[
KR20
, Thm. 1.1]
yields a constant independent of the dimension such that

\mathrm{vol}(C)\geq\sigma(L)\inf_{H\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}\text{vol}_{d-1}(H\cap[0,1)^{d})\geq c\,\sigma(L).

(D.1)

Consequently, the isotropic discrepancy of
\mathcal{P}(L)
is at least
c\,\sigma(L)
.
∎

We briefly explain how the lower bound on the isotropic discrepancy in Theorem
6.3
follows. If
\sigma(L)>1/2
, then we use the fact that the function
f\colon x\mapsto x/(\sqrt{d}+x),x\geq 1/2,
is increasing and satisfies
f(1/2)=1/(2\sqrt{d}+1)
. In the other case we apply the lower bound on the spectral test in Proposition
6.8
which we prove next.

Before doing that, let us recall the content of Minkowski’s fundamental theorem, which states the following:

Let
L
be a lattice in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then any convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is symmetric with respect to the origin and with volume greater than
2^{d}\det(L)
contains a non-zero lattice point of
L
.

See, e.g.,
[
HW79
, Thm. 447]
in the book by Hardy and Wright.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

By the definition of the spectral test we need an upper bound on the shortest vector in the dual lattice
L^{\bot}
. To this end, we will apply Minkowski’s theorem to
L^{\bot}
and the ball
r\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}
of radius
r>0
. According to Sloan and Kachoyan
[
SK87
, Sec. 3 and 4]
(see also
[
Nie92
, Thm. 5.30]
) we have
\det(L^{\bot})=n
. The volume of
r\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}
is

\mathrm{vol}(r\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d})=r^{d}\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}.

Hence, by Minkowski’s theorem, if

r^{d}\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)}>2^{d}\det(L^{\bot})=2^{d}n,

i.e., if

r>\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+1)\big)^{1/d}n^{1/d}=:\widetilde{r}(d,n)

then
r\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}
contains a non-zero point from
L^{\bot}
. In other words,
L^{\bot}
contains a non-zero lattice point of length at most
\widetilde{r}(d,n)
and therefore the length of the shortest vector of
L^{\bot}
is at most
\widetilde{r}(d,n)
and the spectral test at least
\widetilde{r}(d,n)^{-1}
.
∎

D.2
The upper bound for the characterization

In the following, we introduce useful concepts from convex geometry, see, e.g., the book of Schneider
[
Sch14
]
for an introduction.

Definition D.1
.

Given non-empty
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define the Minkowski addition and the Minkowski difference by

A+B:=\bigcup_{b\in B}(A+b)\quad\text{and}\quad A\div B:=\bigcap_{b\in B}(A-b),\text{ respectively,}

where
A\pm b=\{a\pm b\colon a\in A\}
for every
b\in B
.

Then, for all
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle K+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\mathrm{dist}(x,K)<\rho\},

\displaystyle K\div\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}

\displaystyle=\{x\in K\colon\mathrm{dist}\big(x,K^{C}\big)\geq\rho\}.

We define a family of convex parallel sets by

K_{\rho}:=\begin{cases}K+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}&\text{for }\rho\geq 0,\\
K\div(-\rho)\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}&\text{for }\rho<0.\end{cases}

The largest
\rho>0
such that
K\div\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}\neq\emptyset
is given by the inradius of
K
, which is defined by
r(K):=\sup\{\rho\geq 0\colon x+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}\subseteq K\text{ for some }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. As a consequence,
\mathrm{vol}(K_{-r(K)})=0
and
\rho<-r(K)
implies
K_{\rho}=\emptyset
. For
\rho>0
we have
K_{\rho}=K+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}
and
K_{-\rho}=K\div\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d}
. Further,
K_{0}=K
.

Comparing the definitions we see that for any
\rho\geq 0

\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})=\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho})-\mathrm{vol}(K)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{-})=\mathrm{vol}(K)-\mathrm{vol}(K_{-\rho}).

(D.2)

We will use Steiner’s formula (see, e.g., Schneider
[
Sch14
, eq. (4.8)]
) stating that, for every
\rho\geq 0
,

\mathrm{vol}(K+\rho\mathbb{B}_{2}^{d})=\sum_{j=0}^{d}\binom{d}{j}W_{j}(K)\rho^{j},

(D.3)

where
W_{j}(K)
is the
j
-th quermassintegral of
K
. As a mixed volume, it is
monotone with respect to
set inclusion, i.e., it satisfies
W_{j}(K_{1})\leq W_{j}(K_{2})
for
j=0,\ldots,d
, whenever
K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}
are convex bodies. Note that
W_{0}(K)=\mathrm{vol}(K)
and
d\,W_{1}(K)
is the surface area of
K
.

We shall need a result noted by Hadwiger in his book
[
Had57
, Eq. (30), page 207]
; compare also to
[
RS22
, Prop. 2.6]
by Richter and Saorín Gómez who give additional references.

Lemma D.2
.

The function
v(\rho):=\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho})
is differentiable on
(-r(K),\infty)
and its derivative satisfies
v^{\prime}(\rho)=d\,W_{1}(K_{\rho})
.

From this we derive the following inequality, which will enable us to prove Lemma
6.17
.

Lemma D.3
.

For all
\rho\geq 0
we have
\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})\geq\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{-}).

Proof.

Using (
D.2
), this inequality can be written as
v(\rho)-v(0)\geq v(0)-v(-\rho)
. Suppose first that
0<\rho\leq r(K)
. Lemma
D.2
and the mean value theorem yield some
\rho_{1}\in(0,\rho)
and
\rho_{2}\in(-\rho,0)
such that

\frac{v(\rho)-v(0)}{\rho}=v^{\prime}(\rho_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{v(0)-v(-\rho)}{\rho}=v^{\prime}(\rho_{2}).

Since
K_{\rho_{2}}\subseteq K_{\rho_{1}}
and the quermassintegral
W_{1}(\cdot)
is
monotone
, we have
v^{\prime}(\rho_{1})\geq v^{\prime}(\rho_{2})
. This completes the proof in this case.

If
\rho=0
, we have equality by definition, and if
\rho>r(K)
, the monotonicity of the volume yields
v\big(r(K)\big)\leq v(\rho)
, and thus from the previously established case
\rho=r(K)
it follows that

v(\rho)-v(0)\geq v\big(r(K)\big)-v(0)\geq v(0)-v\big(-r(K)\big)=v(0)-v(-\rho)

since
v\big(-r(K)\big)=v(-\rho)=0
. By means of
(
D.2
) this completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.17
.

If
\rho=0
, there is nothing to show and thus assume
\rho\in(0,1]
from now on. Lemma
D.3
implies that
\max\{\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+}),\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{-})\}=\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})
and it remains to estimate the latter. By Steiner’s formula (
D.3
) we have

\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})=\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho})-\mathrm{vol}(K)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}W_{j}(K)\rho^{j}.

The monotonicity of the quermassintegrals yields

\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}W_{j}([0,1]^{d})\rho^{j}

with equality for
K=[0,1]^{d}
. According to e.g. Lotz, McCoy, Nourdin, Peccati and Tropp
[
LMN+20
, Ex. 6.1.3]
it is a classical fact that for
j=0,1,\ldots,d
the
j
-th intrinsic volume
V_{j}([0,1]^{d})
of the unit cube equals
\binom{d}{j}
. The relation to the quermassintegrals is given by
\binom{d}{j}W_{j}([0,1]^{d})=\kappa_{j}V_{d-j}([0,1]^{d}),
where
\kappa_{j}
is the
j
-dimensional volume of the unit ball of
(\mathbb{R}^{j},\|\cdot\|_{2})
. Together with the symmetry of the binomial coefficients, this implies
W_{j}([0,1]^{d})=\kappa_{j}
. Therefore, as
\rho\leq 1
, we have

\mathrm{vol}(K_{\rho}^{+})\leq\rho\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\kappa_{j}\rho^{j-1}\leq\rho\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\kappa_{j}.

Using the fact that
\kappa_{j}\leq\kappa_{5}=8\pi^{2}/15\leq 2^{3}
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
and that
\sum_{j=0}^{d}\binom{d}{j}=2^{d}
completes the proof of Lemma
6.17
.
∎

We finish the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
with the following proof of the estimate on the diameter of a cell with respect to an LLL-reduced basis.

Proof of Lemma
6.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}b_{i}\colon 0\leq\lambda_{i}<1,i=1,\ldots,d\}
be the fundamental cell with respect to an LLL-reduced basis
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
of
L
as given by Definition
6.18
. Then, from the properties of a reduced basis it can easily be deduced, see, e.g.,
[
Gal12
, Lem. 17.2.8]
, that

(a)

\|b^{\ast}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{i-j}\|b^{\ast}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
for
1\leq j\leq i\leq d
and

(b)

\|b_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{d-1}\|b^{\ast}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
for
1\leq i\leq d
.

Together these estimates imply

\|b^{\ast}_{d}\|_{2}\geq 2^{-(d-1)/2}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\|b^{\ast}_{i}\|_{2}\geq 2^{-d+1}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\|b\|_{2}.

Using this, we bound the diameter of
\mathfrak{P}
by

\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|b_{i}\|_{2}\leq d\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\|b_{i}\|_{2}\leq d\,2^{d-1}\,\|b_{d}^{\ast}\|_{2}.

By the Gram-Schmidt algorithm we have

\|b_{d}^{\ast}\|_{2}=\left\|b_{d}-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\mu_{d,j}b^{\ast}_{j}\right\|_{2}.

That is, the length of the last vector in the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization is the length of the projection of the vector
b_{d}
onto the orthogonal complement of the subspace span
\{b^{\ast}_{1},\dots,b^{\ast}_{d-1}\}=\text{span}\{b_{1},...,b_{d-1}\}
spanned by the other basis vectors. This is exactly the distance between two adjacent hyperplanes of the family of parallel hyperplanes

k\,b_{d}+\text{span}\{b_{1},...,b_{d-1}\},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

which covers the entire lattice
L
. Therefore, since this distance cannot be larger than the spectral test, we have

d\,2^{d-1}\,\sigma(L)\geq d\,2^{d-1}\,\|b^{\ast}\|_{2}\geq\text{diam}(\mathfrak{P}).

∎

This completes the proof of the upper bound of Theorem
6.5
.

We give now the proof behind Remark
6.22
. It was suggested by us by an anonymous reviewer when the paper
[
SP21
]
was submitted and improved our previous asymptotic estimates. The improvement of Remark
6.22
can be achieved because the above upper estimate of the sum

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\kappa_{j}\quad\text{with the volumes of the unit balls }\kappa_{j}=\frac{\pi^{j/2}}{\Gamma(1+j/2)}

can be replaced with the following asymptotics

\sum_{j=1}^{d}{d\choose j}\frac{\pi^{j/2}}{\Gamma(1+j/2)}={\rm e}^{\frac{3}{2}(2\pi)^{1/3}d^{2/3}+\mu(d)},

(D.4)

where
|\mu(d)|\leq c\,d^{1/3}
for some constant
c>0
and all
d
.

Proof of (
D.4
).

Setting

a_{j}:={d\choose j}\frac{\pi^{j/2}}{\Gamma(1+j/2)}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,d,

it holds that
a_{j}\leq a_{j+2}
if and only if

(j+1)(j+2)^{2}\leq 2\pi(d-j)(d-j-1).

From this one can deduce that there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for each
d
the maximal index
j_{d}
, i.e.,
a_{j_{d}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}a_{j}
, satisfies
j_{d}=(2\pi)^{1/3}d^{2/3}+\mu_{0}(d)
with
|\mu_{0}(d)|\leq C\,d^{1/3}
.

Using Stirling’s formula for the Gamma function stating that

\Gamma(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{x}}\left(\frac{x}{{\rm e}}\right)^{x}{\rm e}^{\mu_{1}(x)},\quad\text{where }0<\mu_{1}(x)<\frac{1}{12x}\text{ for all }x>0,

(D.5)

and the asymptotics

d(d-1)\cdots(d-j+1)=d^{j}{\rm e}^{-(j^{2}/2d)\big(1+\mu_{2}(j/d)\big)}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,d,

where
\mu_{2}(x)\to 0
if
x\to 0
, we compute

a_{j}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\pi j}\frac{d^{j}(2\pi)^{j/2}}{j^{3j/2}}{\rm e}^{3j/2-(j^{2}/2d)\big(1+\mu_{2}(j/d)\big)-\mu_{1}(j)-\mu_{1}(j/2)}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,d.

Inserting
j=j_{d}
gives

a_{j_{d}}={\rm e}^{\frac{3}{2}(2\pi)^{1/3}d^{2/3}+\mu_{3}(d)}

with
|\mu_{3}(d)|\leq C^{\prime}\,d^{1/3}
for some
C^{\prime}>0
and all
d
. Finally, using
a_{j_{d}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}\leq d\,a_{j_{d}}
proves the statement.
∎

D.3
Proof of Proposition 6.13

Hölder’s inequality implies

\|\mathrm{dist}\big(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\|_{L_{\gamma}([0,1)^{d})}\leq\|\mathrm{dist}\big(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\|_{L_{\infty}([0,1)^{d})},

and therefore it suffices to prove the lower bound for
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
and the upper for
\gamma=\infty
.

We start with the proof of the lower bound and let
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
be arbitrary. Take a hyperplane covering
\mathcal{H}^{*}
of
L
as in the beginning of Section
6.3
with distance
\sigma(L)
between adjacent hyperplanes. For
t\in(0,1/2)
consider the set

A_{t}:=\bigg\{x\in[0,1)^{d}\ \colon\ \mathrm{dist}\bigg(x,\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}H\bigg)\geq t\,\sigma(L)\,\bigg\}.

As the family
\mathcal{H}^{*}
covers the lattice
L
, we have
\mathcal{P}(L)\subseteq\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}H
, and thus for
x\in A_{t}
it holds that
\mathrm{dist}\big(x,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\geq t\,\sigma(L)
. Taking powers and integrals on both sides yields

\int_{[0,1)^{d}}\mathrm{dist}\big(x,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)^{\gamma}\,{\rm d}x\geq t^{\gamma}\,\sigma(L)^{\gamma}\,\mathrm{vol}(A_{t}).

For establishing the lower bound it suffices to find
t_{d}>0
such that
\mathrm{vol}(A_{t_{d}})\geq 1/2
. To this end, we show that for some
t_{d}>0
the complement
B_{t}:=[0,1)^{d}\backslash A_{t}
satisfies
\mathrm{vol}(B_{t_{d}})\leq 1/2
.

For any
t>0
, we first decompose the set
B_{t}
into the disjoint union
B_{t}=\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}S_{t}(H),

where we let

S_{t}(H):=\{x\in[0,1)^{d}\ \colon\ \mathrm{dist}(x,H)<t\,\sigma(L)\}.

Consequently, we have
\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}}\mathrm{vol}\big(S_{t}(H)\big)
.

For any
t>0
, at most
\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)+2
of the sets
S_{t}(H),H\in\mathcal{H}^{*},
are non-empty, and thus only finitely many terms of the sum are non-zero. This is because the cube
[0,1)^{d}
has diameter
\sqrt{d}
and can therefore be intersected by no more than
\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)
hyperplanes contained in
\mathcal{H}^{*}
. The volume of a set
S_{t}(H)
is bounded by its width, which is at most
2t\,\sigma(L)
times the quantity
\sup_{H}\mathrm{vol}_{d-1}(H\cap[0,1)^{d})
, where the supremum is extended over all hyperplanes
H
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
[0,1)^{d}
is bounded, this supremum is bounded by some constant only depending on the dimension, call it
v_{d}
. This implies, since
H\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
can be arbitrary,

\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})\leq(\sqrt{d}/\sigma(L)+2)\,2t\,\sigma(L)\,v_{d}=\big(2\sqrt{d}+4\sigma(L)\big)\,v_{d}\,t.

Using that
\sigma(L)\leq\sqrt{d}
we can choose
t_{d}=(12\sqrt{d}v_{d})^{-1}
such that
\mathrm{vol}(B_{t_{d}})\leq 1/2
. This completes the proof of the lower bound.

We turn to the proof of the upper bound for which we have to find
C_{d}>0
such that

\|\mathrm{dist}\big(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\|_{L_{\infty}([0,1)^{d})}\leq C_{d}\,\sigma(L).

If we choose

r_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathrm{dist}\big(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\|_{L_{\infty}([0,1)^{d})},

then there exists a ball
B(y,r_{0})
with center
y\in[0,1)^{d}
and radius
r_{0}>0
which is empty of points from
\mathcal{P}(L)
. We now use the fact that by Lemma
4.16
in Section
4.3
the cube
[0,1)^{d}
satisfies an interior cone condition. Then Lemma
B.6
implies that there exists a ball
B(z,r_{0}^{\prime})
contained in
B(y,r_{0})\cap[0,1)^{d}
with
r_{0}^{\prime}=u_{d}r_{0}
, where the quantity
u_{d}>0
only depends on
d
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be the fundamental parallelotope with respect to a LLL-reduced basis of
L
(see Section
6.4
) and fix
x\in L
such that the translate
\mathfrak{P}_{x}=x+P
contains the center
z
of the ball. By Lemma
6.19
, we have
\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq d\,2^{d-1}\sigma(L)
.

Thus, if
\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})\leq r_{0}^{\prime}
, then we must have the inclusions
\mathfrak{P}_{x}\subset B(z,r_{0}^{\prime})\subset[0,1)^{d}
and consequently
x\in\mathcal{P}(L)\cap B(z,r_{0}^{\prime})
. This is a contradiction to
B(y,r_{0})\cap\mathcal{P}(L)=\emptyset
. Therefore, we must have
\mathrm{diam}(\mathfrak{P})>r_{0}^{\prime}\geq u_{d}r_{0}
and

\|\mathrm{dist}\big(\cdot,\mathcal{P}(L)\big)\|_{L_{\infty}([0,1)^{d})}\leq C_{d}\,\sigma(L),

where
C_{d}:=d\,2^{d}u_{d}^{-1}
. This completes the proof of the upper bound of Proposition
6.13
.
\hfill\qed
